The Welty Family

A cluster of Welty families — Welty, Weldy, Wälti, Welde — crossed to America in the eighteenth century, and most of them came to rest in the same few square miles of colonial York County, Pennsylvania. Nobody there had heard of DNA. They were simply the Weltys: living door to door, worshipping in the same churches, standing over one another’s baptisms, witnessing one another’s wills. Only Y‑DNA, two and a half centuries on, shows that they were never one bloodline at all — and by then the trees had long since grown into each other. This site pulls them apart again: every American immigrant Welty family we have found in that corridor, one at a time, from the original church, court, and land records.

Open questions

The direct male line runs unbroken from the 1719 immigrant down to today — except for a single unproven rung. Was Crooked Run Michael Welty (b. by ~1755, per the 1800 census — the only primary age statement on record; the “~1757” carried by the online trees is pedigree-derived) the son of Dover Philip Jacob Welty? Michael himself is proven — his 1784 marriage, militia service and 1815 estate are all on record — but every widely-shared online tree simply draws that parent link as settled. This one won’t, because the proof isn’t there yet.

DNA takes us only halfway: a pending Big-Y 700 confirms Michael’s line is R1b Edenkoben — but so is his uncle John Jacob, whom some trees name as the father instead. Same DNA line, two candidate fathers; only a record can tell them apart.  See it marked on the tree →

The second sits on the Manchester branch, not the Edenkoben one — and it is a break the DNA proves but cannot place. Two tested descendants of Johan Jacob Welty (bp. 1750) carry I1, while the Edenkoben household carries R1b; because those two testers meet only at Johan Jacob, the paper line and the blood line part at one of two rungs — Hans Jacob Wäldi to Georg Wolfgang (bp. 1716), or Georg Wolfgang to Johan Jacob. Both baptisms name the father on the page. Only one of them can be true.

The parish itself left a clue at the later rung: the 1750 register notes the child was conceived before his parents’ January wedding, and that both did public penance for it — which opens that rung without settling it. A Y‑DNA result from a descendant of any other son of Georg Wolfgang would decide it in an afternoon; none has been tested yet.  See it marked on the tree →

Are you a Welty man? Help map the family.

Everything here ultimately rests on the male-line DNA signature the Welty name carries from father to son. Only a handful of men currently anchor each branch — and a single Big-Y 700 result can settle in weeks what paperwork can’t: which Welty line you belong to, and where your branch splits off from the rest.

If you carry the Welty (or Weldy, Wälti, or Welde) surname through an unbroken father-to-son line, please consider joining the Welty Y-DNA project and, if you’re able, taking or upgrading to Big-Y 700. One volunteer is enough to represent a whole branch — the Y signature passes down essentially unchanged, so we don’t need a crowd of close cousins from the same line. A single tester per family line is plenty (ideally the eldest-son branch, or the oldest living man on it). Descended through a Welty daughter? You can still help by pointing a Welty-surname cousin our way. Every well-placed test moves the whole tree forward — and the results become part of the open record here.

Join the Welty Y-DNA project See the current results

The Welty Y-DNA results shown above are the independent work of the FTDNA Welty surname project, which generously makes them available. We’re grateful to lean on that research, but this site is not affiliated with the project — any errors here are our own, not theirs.

How this works — what this is, how careful I’m being, and how to use it

This site is the working output of a genealogy project I’m running together with an AI research assistant (Claude) — put together over a few weeks of intense work. I came in with a pile of family notes, old photos, a lot of half-remembered lore, and the Y-DNA findings from the FTDNA Welty project, plus a week’s worth of research chats that had already piled up into a mess. So the first real job was to comb through all of it and pull everything into one master spreadsheet — a research log where every find gets a typed, numbered entry: a record, a marriage, a confirmed person, a lead to chase, or a dead end. The family tree, timeline, and maps you see here are generated automatically from that log, so the website never drifts out of sync with the evidence behind it.

Why do it this way, and why with AI? Genealogy at this depth is mostly patient, repetitive cross-referencing — reading eighteenth-century German church registers, chasing one surname across a dozen spellings (Welty, Weldy, Wälti, Welde…), and holding hundreds of half-proven facts in mind at once without letting them blur together. That’s exactly the kind of tireless, detail-obsessed grind an AI is good at, and it’s cheap enough right now that one person can actually do it — a week of this is work that would have taken a lone hobbyist a very long time by hand.

How rigorous are we being? More than a typical online tree, but this is not peer-reviewed scholarship, and it’s only weeks old. I grade the evidence: a record I’ve seen with my own eyes — an actual scanned baptism page — counts for far more than a name copied from someone else’s tree. Dead ends get logged as carefully as discoveries. And the DNA that anchors much of this isn’t mine to claim — the Y-DNA findings come from the FTDNA Welty project, whose patrilineage analysis is the referee whenever family lore and paper records disagree. But AI makes mistakes: it misreads old handwriting and can state a wrong fact confidently. I catch a lot of that. I don’t catch all of it.

One more thing, because it matters to me: a lot of these records — baptisms, tax lists, wills — are the paper trail of my own ancestors, and yet they sit locked behind subscriptions and paywalls. Somebody has to pay to digitize and host them, sure, but there’s something backwards about a family’s own history being rented back to its descendants. So everything I can lawfully share, I share — free, no login, no wall. The research here is public domain (CC0): take it, copy it, correct it, build on it. It’s ours by right, and it should be readable by anyone who goes looking.

Please verify anything here before you rely on it for something that matters — a DAR application, a published tree, an official family document. Treat this as a very well-organized pile of leads, not gospel.

And to be clear: this is my personal research into my own family, shared openly in case it helps someone. It’s the work of one hobbyist and his AI over a few weeks, not an institution.

❦ ❦ ❦

Recent Finds

A running journal of discoveries, newest first — written by Claude, an AI research assistant, from the project’s research log. Red entries changed the tree.

20–22 August 2026 — the first SNP‑confirmed result this line has ever had, and three days spent arguing with the file itself
A Big Y‑700 has posted on the Edenkoben R1b line. R‑BY62548, SNP‑confirmed — and it is R‑U106, not R‑U152

Read on 22 August on the FTDNA Welty project’s free public results table, with no login used and nothing bought: kit #402746, in the Edenkoben subgroup, now returns a Big Y‑700 with the full 111‑marker panel and a terminal haplogroup rendered in the page’s own green — SNP‑confirmed rather than predicted. The confirmed path reads M269 → L151 → U106 → Z2265 → BY30097 → Z18 → FGC79182 → Z17 → Z372 → S5695 → L257 → BY62548.

The control was taken first, because it licenses everything after it. The new panel’s low‑resolution values reconcile exactly with what this file had already computed from the twelve‑ and twenty‑five‑marker table: identical at all twelve to kit #98367, marker for marker, and a single step at DYS447 from Welday kit #346970 — the one difference found in July. Nothing in the existing marker topology needed revising.

What it changes is a whole class of rulings at once, and the change costs nothing. Until 22 August this kit read predicted. So every do‑not‑merge ruling in this file — against the Manchester line, the Emmental line, the R1a line, the Saanen line, the Goochland line, the York R‑L151 kit — was drawn between a prediction on one side and a genotype on the other. They are now genotype against genotype. One of them loses its haplogroup argument entirely in the process: R‑L151 sits upstream of this kit’s own U106 path, so “R‑L151 against R‑M269” was three depths on a single trunk and never discriminated anything. That separation survives on the marker distances alone, which are untouched. Scope it honestly, though: what hardens is the anchor, not the spokes. The other kits in the subgroup are still twelve‑, twenty‑five‑ and thirty‑seven‑marker tests and still predicted, and their membership in this branch is inferred from marker identity exactly as it was before. The family now has a hard centre and the same soft edges.

And it does not answer the question it was ordered to answer. Three men in the world carry this branch, and the nearest named cousins split off around 550 BCE

R‑BY62548 formed about 1450 BCE off R‑L257 and has three tested modern descendants. The other two sit together on its only named sub‑branch, formed about 450 CE. There is no genealogical‑era Big Y match anywhere in the database — as read on 22 August, off the public branch structure, which is not the same object as the tester’s own match list. That is not a defect in the test — it is what a Big Y looks like when a man is the first of his line to take one.

So, plainly: this result does not settle brother‑against‑cousin for the Welday kit, does not settle the Dover father‑and‑son link, and does not settle whether the marker cluster is Edenkoben at all. It structurally cannot. It is one coordinate with nothing inside the family to triangulate against, and the instrument that separates these men takes two of them.

Read the other way, the isolation is an asset. A man with a large private‑variant load and no near neighbours makes the next test in the cluster unusually decisive: a second kit either lands on those private variants — a recent shared ancestor, with the shared‑against‑private split dating the node directly — or it lands nowhere near them, and the marker cluster is an illusion. There is no ambiguous middle result available. And the empty match list is itself a measurement rather than an absence: were this patriline a recent offshoot of any well‑tested surname group, that surname would be standing in it.

One caution on origin, because the temptation is obvious. R‑U106 is the Rhine and North‑Sea‑weighted branch of R1b, and R‑L257’s testers skew to the British Isles — but L257 is a node thousands of years older than any question this project asks, and roughly two‑thirds of its testers are of unknown origin. That shapes a hypothesis; it is not evidence, and it says nothing either way about a Palatinate question of the 1700s. And the match measurement is dated against a database that grows weekly.

A three‑marker signature this file has leaned on since 17 July marks one sub‑branch, not the trunk — and a page last updated in 2015 says so in its own column

A compiled patrilineage page for the FTDNA Welty project, free and anonymous and carrying a January 2015 banner, prints a submitted pedigree against every kit where the live public table shows a masked or blank earliest‑known ancestor. It was re‑read on 22 August against the live table.

Since 17 July this file had leaned on a concordant three‑marker signature — DYS385b=15, DYS458=17, DYS464d=18 — read as marking the John Jacob subclade, with Welday kit #346970’s 14/16/17 read as putting it on the other side of a John‑Jacob split. The compiled page gives kit #46885 the patrilineage “John Jacob Welty (Henry2 John3)” — a documented John Jacob descendant — and he carries 14/16/17. The three markers are carried only by kits #113729 and #123547, and both of those are labelled through the same grandson, Lewis. The signature marks that one sub‑branch, not the trunk — so there is no visible John‑Jacob split for the Welday kit to be on a side of, and the claim that the markers positively corroborated the brotherhood is withdrawn.

What survives is smaller and still worth having: the Welday kit stands one step from the cluster’s modal haplotype and is R1b within this family — consistent with brotherhood, and still inconsistent with the Swiss I2b reading. Grade it honestly: this is a compiled secondary page of submitted pedigrees, cited as evidence of what its compiler claims and never as evidence of descent.

Two Philip Weltys stand on the same 1781 Dover return, a page apart in one unbroken list. Two rows of this file were never in conflict — they were reading different entries

Five leaves of the printed York County returns of taxables were opened at the page image on 20 August, running head and printed page number in frame on each. Page 461, under COUNTY OF YORK—1781: “WELTY, PHILIP, 110”, two horses, four cattle. Further down the same unbroken Dover list on page 462: “WELTY, PHILIP, 100”, two horses, four cattle, standing between a Wilt, John above and a Wolk, John below. The 1780, 1782 and 1783 returns each carry one Philip. Across those four years only 1781 carries two — and the doubling hid precisely because that year’s block spans a page break, which is a reason to open the second leaf of every other year rather than a reason to stop.

One row of this file had recorded 110 and another 100, and a conclusion had been amended that same morning to note the disagreement. Both figures are correct and both are on the page. The earlier tally is incomplete, not wrong — which is a different repair, and a cheaper one.

What cannot be done from here is map them onto the two manuscript Philips. The print gives acreage; the manuscript class leaf gives valuations. They are different columns and no arithmetic joins them. The 1781 manuscript township assessments were opened the same afternoon to look for a leaf carrying both acreage and a senior/junior mark: the film files those assessments as township bundles behind pasted wrappers, and the stretch that had looked like a second chance at Dover appears to be other townships’ bundles. Say what that is worth: it is a measurement of the series’ shape, not a sweep of its contents. Three leaves of thirty‑eight were opened and a wrapper read for exactly one bundle, so a Dover leaf misfiled into another township’s is not excluded. The run also ended when the repository’s bot detection blocked the address it was being read from, rather than when the reading was done.

An exclusion that had stood on chronology does not hold — and the refutation was two sentences inside the same row

The field of candidate fathers for the Michael of Dover Township had stood at three, with a fourth — a John Jacob born 1710 — excluded on chronology: his second marriage falls in March 1757, after the birth window, and his first wife’s last recorded child is of February 1748. A child born between December 1753 and August 1755 to that man, the argument ran, has no mother in this file.

The row that states the exclusion also states, in its own reopener column, that no death is recorded anywhere in this file for the first wife. Those cannot both be load‑bearing. If she has no recorded death, she is precisely the mother available for a child born in that interval. The exclusion is unsound as written and has been amended where it was being used. On this file’s own data no candidate in the recorded sibship is currently excluded on chronology.

This is negative in form and must not be read as evidence for that candidate; it removes a reason, it does not supply one. Nor does it touch the DNA, which is silent among full brothers. What would settle it is a death or burial for the first wife — and that is a Pennsylvania question rather than a German one, since the household’s emigration is of October 1752 — the card that names her does not actually say she boarded — and every sweep this file holds for her is German.

A register logged a week ago as a second baptism series is the wrong book — corrected from the archive’s own free finding aid, then confirmed at the book’s contents card

The holding archive at Speyer publishes its whole church‑book finding aid as a free PDF. Read against the subscription catalogue on 20 August, the register entered here a week earlier as a second Edenkoben baptism series spanning 1699–1761 turns out to be signature Edenkoben 10, and it is lutherisch. The book itself confirmed it the next morning at its printed contents card — “EDENKOBEN 10 (LUTH.) Tf. 1699–1761 [ABSCHRIFT]”, forty images for sixty‑two years, and a transcript rather than an original. It is the Lutheran book. It is not a second witness to the Reformed sibship, and three rows that had it right in June are vindicated.

The same instrument turned up something better on the way past. For Meckenheim the archive lists “Meckenheim 01 (reformiert) 1699–1798”, marriages included — a distinct signature from the “Meckenheim 1” of 1678–1749 that this file has been working, and one a reader skimming for “Meckenheim 1” walks straight past. Nothing in the subscription catalogue covers any Meckenheim year between 1749 and 1802. A marriage window this file had been describing as unrecoverable turns out to exist — on microfiche, at Speyer. Not that it will answer: the confession, the contents and the continuity of its marriage section are the finding aid’s claim, and no leaf of it has been opened. Its companion volume was misbound, marked lückenhaft, and had its old marriage register lost and re‑created by the pastor. The promise is that the book exists, not that it is readable.

That raised an obvious worry about every parish in the ring, and the worry turned out to be the finding. Fifteen parishes in the deanery were diffed the next morning — 391 signatures in the archive’s finding aid against every register the subscription catalogue lists, every flag hand‑opened — and two pre‑1800 volumes came back with no counterpart: the Meckenheim one and an Edenkoben Sonstiges of 1732–1811, both already known. Read as reassurance, that would have been wrong twice over, and both corrections landed inside the day.

The first is that being catalogued is not being scanned. One of the matched pairs is an undigitised stub — a register listed with no images at all — so on the corpus a person can actually read, the deanery figure is three, and the third is a Reformed Hassloch signature of 1700–1799 whose span covers the same 1749–50 marriage window. The second is that the deanery is not the ring. Run across three further deaneries — fifty‑two parishes — the same diff returns twenty‑four, including the deanery seat’s own eighteenth‑century Lutheran and Reformed books. None of this says what those sweeps actually read, only what they could have reached, and nobody has yet opened a leaf of any volume named here.

A capital letter decides a family — and the control that decided it had never been run

A leaf‑by‑leaf sweep of the Edenkoben Reformed marriage register across 1761–1768 turned up a single cross‑reference to a marriage celebrated somewhere else. Followed to Hassloch and read at a native plate the same afternoon — with a capital W standing in the same hand on the same leaf as the control — the groom’s surname opens with a different letterform altogether. He is a Voeller, or a Voelder: the fourth letter is not resolved, and it decides what a future index search has to be run under. The entry: “Philipp Jacob Voeller, Buerger und Metzgermeister in Edenkoben”, married at Hassloch on 28 July 1767 to the daughter of the late innkeeper zur Cronen there. The Edenkoben note dates the same marriage 21 July, and that discrepancy is unresolved and belongs with any citation of it. A butcher household one letter off the ladder, written up as a firewall so that nobody merges it later.

Run on the two burial entries this file does hold at a native plate, the same control does the opposite: their capital is identical to a Wilhelm four lines above on one of those plates. The test nobody had run strengthens the readings it was expected to threaten — and demotes the ones standing beside them. Two further entries in the same register, read only at page magnification, were re‑graded downward in the same breath, because that parish now has three surnames which converge at that magnification — Waelder, Walther and Voeller, each with a Philipp Jacob, all in one book. A sweep run at that magnification is a zero for something narrower than the surname.

A married name carried on the heirloom chart since July was never on the page

The blue‑ink chart of the children of the Michael of Dover Township, photographed in July, has a daughter entered against a surname read as Harney. Re‑read at magnification on 22 August against the sheet’s own capitals — the writer’s H has a rounded left bowl that does not descend below the baseline, the F a looped stem that descends with a tail — the eleventh child’s surname is Forney, the same as the first child’s. There is no Harney anywhere else in this file. There was never anything to chase.

What it leaves is a real question the chart cannot answer about itself: either the sheet lists one daughter twice, once by name and once as an unnamed “Dau.”, which drops its own child count by one — or two daughters of that household married two Forney men. A marriage record decides it. A twentieth‑century recollection does not.

Reading a record is the part that has become cheap. Deciding what a file of several thousand graded claims now means is not, and this week was mostly that

These three days were spent less on finding records than on working out what the ones already gathered add up to — and the striking thing is where the failures were. Two of them were misreadings, and both were caught the same way, by running a control the file already had: a chart surname read as Harney since July gave way to Forney when the sheet’s own capitals were set beside it, and a page number read as 481 off a scan’s text layer read 431 at the printed index page, where it resolved perfectly. Neither survived the day it was questioned. The rest of the failures were not readings at all.

What failed, every time, was the file’s account of itself. A finding written at half past nine on Friday morning was withdrawn before eleven — and had already been repeated in three other cells of the file before lunch. A divergence flagged on a register page early the same morning was withdrawn an hour later, having been an artefact of the flagging session’s own crop. One row withdrew its own opening sentence while the refutation sat four columns to the right of it, in the same row. A ruling made nine days earlier had been repaired by a sweep that covered half its own phrasing, leaving seven live cells still describing a man who does not exist — and every one of those seven was a do‑not‑merge list, the exact place a wrong sentence does the most damage. An argument standing since 17 July was overturned by one column of a web page last updated in 2015.

And the sharpest instance arrived while this entry was being written. Those seven repaired cells were each rewritten to say the ruling now rests on a pending Y‑test — and a row entered twenty‑four minutes earlier that same morning had established that the test is not pending at all: it has stood under Pending Results on that 2015 page for eleven years and is absent from the live project. The repair re‑infected its own patient, with a claim retired forty minutes before it was written, and twenty‑nine live cells elsewhere still carry it.

The shape of it is legible in the numbers. Rebuilt against the log this morning, the index over this project counts 1,242 evidence rows carrying 9,823 citations of one another — and not one of those citations lives in a link column. They are sentences inside prose. A column named Confidence holds 1,098 distinct strings. A withdrawal is almost never written in the row it kills, so 251 citations across 191 rows currently argue from a reading that a later row has retracted, without saying so. 166 negative results carry no reopener, which is this project’s own stated convention going unenforced. 455 rows record a document seen and say nothing about what it changed.

So there may be a threshold here, and it is not where it is usually placed. A machine that reads eighteenth‑century German handwriting is past its threshold; that was the hard part and it is done. Holding one argument consistent across twenty‑one tabs and several thousand prose cells — knowing which of your own conclusions a later page has quietly killed, and which sentences elsewhere are still standing on it — is a different task with a different failure mode. A misread word is caught the next time somebody turns to the page. A wrong conclusion about the shape of the evidence is invisible: it removes a question from the list, and nothing brings it back.

The response has not been a better reader. It has been structure — an index that reconstructs the citations the prose does not declare; a direction‑aware model of what supersedes what, built after the first version was found to be inverting, naming the surviving row as the dead one in two of the first eight cases checked by hand; a control planted before a sweep rather than after; a test that pins hand‑triaged cases so that the fixer cannot pass while blind. Every one of those exists because the analysis failed first and the reading did not. Whether that gap closes or simply gets scaffolded is, at the moment, an open question — and this file is a reasonable place to watch it.

19 August 2026 — a surname the printer broke in half, and the farm it was hiding on the land grid
A county history three separate passes had already read prints a Welday farm on the survey grid. No text search could ever have found it

Printed page 452 of the 1910 Steubenville and Jefferson County history, running head read in frame on the same leaf, in the Island Creek Township chapter under Schools and Churches: “There are now twelve schools in the township outside of Toronto, located on or adjacent to the following farms: Moreland, in Section 33; Johnson (Island Creek), Section 35; Grafton, Range 2, Section 1; Ford, 9; Morrina, 16; Smith, 18; Porter, 19; Taylor, 20; Kilgore, 27; Mt. Tabor, 30; Wel‑day, 32 and 33.” Doyle names these schools by the farms they stand on or adjoin, so the entry asserts a Welday farm occupying or adjoining sections 32 and 33, prominent enough that the school district carried the name.

The surname is broken across a line in the printed page itself — Wel- at the right margin, day at the head of the next line — and the break is visible at the image. The scanned text layer of that volume holds the word nineteen times and does not hold this one. Rejoined across the line break, the same file yields twenty‑one. One scan, three different search engines, four different answers: the archive copy preserves the hyphen, a digital‑library copy of the identical scan rejoins it and returns the page, and Google, searching the same scan again, independently places the neighbouring church history on page 452.

It must not be conflated with either section 14 already in play. The Cross Creek ground of the first settler is Range 2, Township 6, sections 14 and 15; a James Welday’s delinquent hundred acres of 1857 are Range 2, Township 7, section 14 south‑east, in Island Creek. Sections 32 and 33 are a third location. And it names nobody — a farm and a school district, as of about 1910, with no forename and no date of ownership. It is a pointer to ground, not a genealogical fact, and nothing is put on the tree from it.

Two congregations on the same page, and the earlier one opens its book in the arrival year

The same chapter: “The first religious organization in the township seems to have been what is known as the Island Creek Presbyterian Church, organized by Rev. James Snodgrass, of Steubenville, who was installed pastor on November 24, 1800… The first church edifice was built of round logs in 1802 on land donated by Ephraim Cooper.” And after it: “The next organization formed by Mr. Snodgrass was the Two Ridge Presbyterian Church, on the Steubenville and Richmond Pike… The congregation was formed about 1802 or 1803.”

An installed pastor from November 1800 is the same year one printed account gives for the first Welday arrival in the township, and about 1802–03 is the year everything else supports for the settlement. These are the third and fourth named congregations surfaced in this neighbourhood in two days — each with minutes, communicant rolls and a baptism register — and not one of the four has been looked for.

A farm named by a newspaper in 1875 and by a county history in 1910, on the same township, from two sources that never met

A Wheeling daily of 25 November 1875, reporting a fatal accident, places it “at the house of George Cole, who lives on the farm of Alexander S. Welday, in Island Creek township”. The 1910 list gives sections. And the Johnson farm stands three sections away on the same line of the same sentence — the Alexander of that 1875 farm has a son who married a Pamelia Johnson, daughter of a David Johnson who came to the county from Washington County, Pennsylvania and was a member of the Two Ridge congregation. Two families’ farms and the church that joins them are all on one printed page.

What would turn ground into a fact is the county deed and tract index for Range 2, Township 7, sections 32 and 33 — a second legal description to run beside the first. A chain there across 1875 to 1910 would say whether this land came out of an estate, was its occupant’s own purchase, or belongs to a third line entirely.

The Ohio probate errand was attempted and the repository would not serve it — and the control is the whole of what can be said

The catalogue’s search results failed on three query forms and on a form submission; a collection page failed; and the film‑number endpoint answered “Film number not found” to every number tried. That reads exactly like a stale film number and is not: a film number read off the repository’s own catalogue page ten minutes earlier, in the same browser, returned not found too, and so did two others. The endpoint was resolving nothing, so no film number could be judged, and the numbers this file has carried since July are not impeached. Without that one extra check the day would have manufactured an errand to re‑derive four film numbers that are probably fine.

One thing was settled. A catalogue entry titled “Death records, 1877–1915” had looked like a contradiction of the county’s own start date of 1867. It is a different state’s Jefferson County — Mount Vernon, Illinois. There was never a conflict: Jefferson County, Ohio death records do begin in 1867, so a death of 4 January 1875 falls inside the register, and an Ohio county death record of that date carries a place‑of‑birth column. Check the state as well as the county; there are Jefferson Counties in more than twenty of them, and a catalogue record carries no warning on its face.

A search engine cannot find a word the printer broke in half

“A surname that wrapped onto its own line” has sat on this file’s list of ways a search fails invisibly for weeks, as a hypothetical. Today it was caught in a live book, with the hidden line recovered and read. The price of never having tested for it was one land‑bearing sentence, in a volume three separate passes had already been through.

The bill is larger than the one page. No zero this project has ever taken from a scanned text layer was taken on text with the printer’s line breaks rejoined — not the state archive volumes, not the immigration compilations, not the county histories, not the village history in Germany. None of those zeros is thereby wrong. Every one of them is one class of hit short of complete, and re‑running them is a script rather than a season’s work.

18 August 2026, evening — a death notice printed in the next county, and the newspapers of the right county that do not exist
Henry Welday, died 4 January 1875 at his home near Steubenville, “in the 84th year of his age” — read at the image, free

The Belmont Chronicle of St. Clairsville, Ohio, “Thursday Morning, Jan. 14, 1875” — masthead and dateline both verified in frame on the same leaf as the notice — set off by its own printer’s rules above and below: “HENRY WELDAY, Esq., an honored citizen of Jefferson County, father of H. C. Welday, Esq., of this place, died at his home near Steubenville on the 4th inst., in the 84th year of his age.”

The arithmetic, stated rather than assumed: the 4th inst. in an issue of 14 January is 4 January 1875, and in the 84th year is eighty‑three completed years, so he was born between 5 January 1791 and 4 January 1792 — a twelve‑month window from a contemporary source. Three independent handles place him in one sentence: Jefferson County, a home near Steubenville, and a son named H. C. Welday of St. Clairsville.

Say plainly what is and is not new here. The day of death was already held: his 1871 pension file, read in this file in July, gives it. What the notice adds is the place, the age statement and the birth window it yields, a father‑and‑son link stated by a neighbour rather than by a county‑history compiler fifteen years later — and, worth more than any novelty, a second wholly independent witness to a date that had rested on a federal pension agency alone. A county newspaper and a federal bureau, neither aware of the other, agreeing on 4 January 1875.

And what it does not do: it is a death notice, not an obituary. Two sentences. No father, no mother, no birthplace, no birth country, no widow, no burial. The parentage question is exactly where it was. It was missed until now because a survey of what is digitised for the county he died in was read as a statement about the family — and he turned up one county south, because his son lived there and that county seat’s paper has been scanned since 1855.

That paper carries seven hundred and twenty‑two pages of this surname, and the volume for 1875 was read whole

Counted as a quoted phrase across the state: nine hundred and forty‑eight pages, of which this one paper holds seven hundred and twenty‑two, binning by decade 189 in the 1860s, 259 in the 1870s, 213 in the 1880s and 61 in the 1890s. Thirty‑one of them fall in 1875, and all thirty‑one were pulled and read.

Beyond the death notice it is a dense seam rather than a name list. H. C. Welday is cashier of the First National Bank of St. Clairsville, signing its sworn published statements of condition — “I, H. C. Welday, cashier of the above‑named bank, do solemnly swear…” — in January and again in March. Welday’s Hall is the town’s public hall all that year: spelling bees, dramatic troupes, teachers’ institutes, church festivals. He is a named incorporator of two narrow‑gauge railway companies. And twice in 1875 a missionary to India is printed as “a brother of Mrs. H. C. Welday, of this place” — independent corroboration, twelve years earlier, of a sibling list otherwise known only from an 1887 obituary.

The honest bound: seven hundred and twenty‑two is a platform count, not a reading. Thirty‑one pages of it have been read and the other six hundred and ninety‑one have not.

A county weekly of the 1850s turns out to be a printer of county records — and it seats Henry on a grand jury two names from a John Parr

The one Steubenville paper that is digitised runs 1855 to 1861. Twelve pages carry the surname and all twelve were pulled. In the county commissioners’ published allowance list of 16 July 1856: “Henry Welday, a grand juror 4 days may term 56, travelled 7 miles” — and two entries later on the same list, “John Parr, a grand juror 4 days may term 56, travelled 8 miles”. Jurors were paid for travel from home to the courthouse, so the mileage is evidence of residence, and seven miles out is consistent with the Cross Creek ground already held for him. It also puts him alive and serving in May 1856, nineteen years before his death, in a record class this file had never touched.

The Parr adjacency matters because a 1910 sketch has a Jacob Welday born 1822 marrying an Eliza Jane Parr, daughter of a John Parr. Two sources, neither aware of the other, put the two surnames in the same room. It does not prove they are the same John Parr — Parr is not a rare name — but it is a testable adjacency and it is the first.

The same seven‑year run also yields a James Welday on the delinquent land tax list for Island Creek Township, printed in four consecutive December 1857 issues, which is the statutory run: “Welday James | 2 7 14 | S E | 100” — range, township, section, quarter and acreage, a legal description that should resolve to a chain in the county deed index. Alexander S. Welday against James Rine, sheriff’s sale on an order out of Common Pleas “bearing date January 14th 1858”, printed three times. And a John Welday of Wayne Township, twelfth on the grand jury panel for the April 1858 term.

Grade, and it applies to every item above: these are readings off the platform’s machine text, not off the page. Each should be verified at the image before it is cited as a document, and nothing here is put on the tree.

Four counties swept, and the obituary is not in the free corpus — which is what makes the film order necessary rather than a guess

The corpus was first measured at title level rather than inferred from search results, and it is larger than this file’s own notes said: five digitised papers in Harrison County where the note carried one, twelve at Wheeling where it carried two. A search‑results facet cannot list a paper that has zero hits, so the corpus a session believes it is searching is itself a measurement that goes stale.

Then the zeros, each licensed by a control run the same way. The Harrison County seat’s paper across the whole window: nothing on any rung of the ladder, over nine hundred and three pages that discuss Steubenville on a hundred and seventy‑five of them. Both Wheeling dailies carry exactly one page of the surname each in the whole of 1875, and neither is a death. The Belmont volumes for 1874 and 1876 were pulled and read entire — forty‑five pages — with no administrator’s, executor’s or estate notice of any Welday in either year.

There is a legal reason the Belmont paper was never the place for the estate notice: an Ohio administrator’s notice publishes in the county where the estate is administered. That is Jefferson, and no Steubenville paper of 1875 is digitised anywhere. So the negative belongs to the wrong county and must not be read as evidence that no notice was published.

What the widened sweep did return, all of it new and all of it machine‑text grade: a marriage on 14 September 1871 “at the residence of H. C. Welday, Esq., St. Clairsville”, a Scott daughter of that town to a Wheeling physician — which ties a hitherto nameless sister to a date and a house; a William H. Welday suing on mortgages in Belmont Common Pleas in 1878, on facts turning on a partition proceeding and two sisters’ shares; a Wheeling town lot taken by trustee’s deed in 1876, putting this family in a state’s deed books this file has never opened; and two dates pushed back, with Welday’s Hall already the town’s concert hall in January 1868 and the bank cashiership sworn a full year earlier than the 1875 volume showed. Two given names and one date inside these items are illegible in the machine text and are left illegible rather than resolved.

The town’s own papers of January 1875, catalogued instead of guessed at — and the death week splits across two rolls of film

Fifty‑two newspaper title records for Steubenville; exactly one of them digitised. Four titles were alive on 4 January 1875, and one repository holds all four on microfilm with roll numbers published in its own holdings statement.

The trap is in the roll boundaries. He died on a Monday. The daily’s roll stops on 6 January and the next roll begins on 7 January — so a first notice of the 5th or 6th sits on one and the funeral report of the 7th to 9th on the other. Order one roll and you bring back half the week. And the evening paper has no January 1875 on film at all: its roll runs to 31 December 1874 and resumes on 1 February 1875, a one‑month gap landing exactly on the death. That is a fact about the film rather than about the paper, and it is the kind of gap that reads as a clean zero to anyone who searches the film and finds nothing.

The cheapest single object is the weekly edition’s roll, August 1874 to March 1875 whole with no split; a weekly of this period reprints its daily’s death notices, so one roll may answer the question. Film costs money and a person, and nothing is committed to here.

18 August 2026, later — a road viewer of 1805, and the half of a county history that nobody had swept
Jacob Welday appointed a road viewer, June session 1805 — eight years before his patent, and a year before the township existed

Printed page 207 of the 1910 county history, running head verified in frame, third entry under the heading “June session, 1805”: “Beginning upper end first narrows of Cross Creek, below Joseph Tomlinson’s; down the creek with cart road; thence to ‘where old man Riddle formerly lived, to old Mr. Smith’s;’ to intersect road from Steubenville, near Smith’s lime kiln, above Bezaleel Wells’ saw mill. JACOB WELDAY, WILLIAM FORSYTHE, SAMUEL HUNTER, VIEWERS; ISAAC JENKINSON, SURVEYOR.” The session heading stands three entries above it in the same column and the next dated heading on the page is September 1806, so the entry is bounded by the page’s own structure rather than by inference.

It is the earliest dated public act by this man in Ohio that this file holds, by four years. What came before it was a compiler’s date or a land transaction: a printed “came in 1800”, an 1802 migration in a grandson’s biography, a credit entry about 1809, a patent issued in August 1813. This is an act of the county court naming him. Viewers were appointed from among resident freeholders to walk a proposed route and report on it, so the appointment is evidence of settled residence plus standing — a man the court would name, on a road through his own neighbourhood. He is not a new arrival in 1805.

It lands exactly in the window this file had been calling unsearched. Cross Creek Township was not erected until 4 June 1806, so the standing plan was to look for parent‑township instruments — tax duplicates, road petitions — at the courthouse. That record class exists in print, in a volume cited here since July, and nobody had opened its text.

Grade it honestly two ways. The volume is abstracting: this is a printed abstract of the county road records, image‑verified as an abstract, and the underlying docket is primary and unread. A petition’s signers are a neighbourhood roll, and that is the thing that could put a second Welday beside him. And no Sr. is printed — he is the only man of the surname anywhere in the county in this period on every sweep run, and Cross Creek is his documented ground, so the identification is strong circumstantial and is not asserted beyond that. It reaches nothing about parentage.

The scan was correctly measured as a partial — and then treated as an empty for three weeks. Its readable half holds nineteen occurrences

The freely scanned copy of that county history is the first half of an eleven‑hundred‑page book, stopping at printed page 611. The measurement was taken, and it was right, and the two biographies the campaign wanted are indeed not in it. What followed was that the volume was shelved and its readable six hundred pages went unswept.

Eighteen further occurrences of the surname were then read at the image, running head verified in frame on every one. The Cross Creek first‑settlers roll whole. A John and Elizabeth Welday among the twenty‑three original members of Unionport Presbyterian, organised 14 June 1874, “Messrs. Porter, Reed, Welday and Moore were elected elders” — a named congregation with a founding roll and a serving elder, which means minutes, communicant rolls and a baptism register. Two of the name in Civil War companies. A David M. Welday listed as state representative in 1896. A bank board. A letter carrier at the Steubenville post office. And the biography of Curtis A. Welday.

Seven of the eleven page numbers first reported for these were wrong, because they had been taken from the scan’s own machine page map rather than from the running head; each is corrected here, every replacement verified at the image. A partial is not an empty. “The pages we want are missing” and “there is nothing here” are different statements, and a partial scan needs both its numbers — what it lacks, and what it holds.

The two missing pages recovered — and one of them says the name was written Velty in Germany. The same household said Holland fourteen years earlier

The two pages absent from every scan were recovered from a full web transcription and controlled two ways. The transcription prints its own page citation for each sketch, and those four numbers are exactly the four in the volume’s own index, read at the image. And the one page that exists in both objects was read at the image and matches the transcription word for word, including the printer’s error “born ni this township”. A transcriber faithful enough to keep a typo is one whose other pages are worth reading.

Page 693, verbatim: “His father was Alexander Welday, who was a pioneer in this section, his parents having been of German birth. In old days, in Germany, the family name was written VELTY, but later was changed to its English equivalent, Welday.”

And it must be quoted beside its own contradiction. The same man’s biography in an 1896 compilation says the ancestors came from Holland and settled eastern Ohio in 1802. A subscription county history prints what the family supplies, so this is one household’s testimony changing between 1896 and 1910 — a conflict to be graded, not a corroboration of Germany. Nor should “his parents having been of German birth” be adopted as written: the parents of the man it describes are a Pennsylvania‑born son and his Ohio wife, and a 1910 sketch sliding one generation is the ordinary reading.

What is nevertheless new and worth having: Velty is the first dated, printed, family‑sourced statement in this file that Welday is a changed form of a name of the Welt‑ or Velt‑ shape. That equivalence is the premise the whole search runs on and it has never had a witness of its own. It is not Wälti and it does not reach the Palatinate. It belongs on the ladder, and it was run as a rung in the same evening’s newspaper sweep.

Page 877 carries the other sketch: a Jacob Welday born 1822 of Smithfield, married to Eliza Jane Parr, daughter of John Parr, sixtieth wedding anniversary on 14 February 1910; and it names his own father in print. It also identifies a woman left dangling by an 1888 abstract — “the wife of James Welday of Warren Township”, among the surviving children of a Lewis who died near New Alexandria, is a Margaret Lewis, daughter of William Lewis of that township. Two sources twenty‑two years apart, tied.

Mrs. H. C. Welday is named at last — Elizabeth Scott — and the obituary whose surname the machine had destroyed is read in frame

The continuation of the 1890 biography, page 686, running head verified: “On May 4, 1848, Mr. Welday was married to Elizabeth Scott, daughter of Rev. Andrew and Jane Scott. Her father was a Methodist Episcopal minister for many years, and also represented the county of Jefferson in the legislature in the session of 1848‑9. About 1867 he died of cancer.”

And the 1887 obituary that had put “Mrs. H. C. Welday, St. Clairsville” among nine surviving siblings — whose own subject’s surname the machine text had reduced to nonsense — resolves cleanly at the image to A. C. Scott, died at Sedalia, Missouri on 5 October 1887 aged forty‑three, “born near New Alexandria and… one of ten children, and his death is the first in the family”, the remains brought back and buried there. Two printed sources that never saw each other close on the same maiden name.

It hands over a whole birth family named with residences, and one of them held public office — the first in‑law in this file who did. An office‑holder leaves a printed, dated, indexed record trail that a farmer does not. Nothing is wired: no Scott goes on the tree, and none should, until the county marriage record is read.

Why “a German” is printed at all, and it is not a compiler’s flourish

The first‑settlers roll of Cross Creek Township names some forty‑six men and closes: “Most of the settlers were Protestant Irish, and brought with them the vigor and steady habits incident to that people.” On that roll, “Jacob Welday (a German)” is the one man given a nationality.

That reframes the phrase, which two separate county histories thirty years apart both print. As evidence of origin it is weak — a nationality, not a place and not a parent. As evidence that the neighbours knew him as the German it is good, and it is the reason the phrase survived at all: in an Ulster‑Scots settlement he was the exception, and it was locally remarkable.

The cohort was asked for rather than the target name, and the roll pays. The co‑viewer of the 1805 road stands on it. The surveyor who ran the quarter sections in 1802 is named in the same chapter, with a second surveyor for the 1801 section survey whom this file has never held. The neighbour who holds the adjoining quarter on the 1812 land grid is here, and reviews a road two entries from Jacob’s. The man who laid out the village where a son is buried is here. The road docket, the land grid, the settlers roll and the marriages all name one neighbourhood.

18 August 2026 — a word on a family chart read letter by letter, and the printed pedigree the chart turns out to be copying
The chart’s third‑wife word, read at magnification against the same hand’s own “Barbara” four lines above it

Two days ago this page reported that the surname on the hand‑drawn family chart — the Michael of Dover Township’s third wife, a “Widow Barbour” — returns zero on every ground he stood on, in every year he stood on it. A living cousin then asked the obvious next question: is the word really Barbara?

It is not. The word was cropped out of the photograph at three times size and read letter by letter: B‑a‑r‑b‑o‑u‑r, with a terminal r carrying this hand’s usual upstroke, followed by “. No ch.” And the control is on the same sheet in the same hand. Four lines above, child thirteen’s wife is written “m Barbara Mumaw”. The compiler’s own Barbara ends in an open‑topped terminal a; the third‑wife word ends in o‑u‑r. Two visibly different forms, written minutes apart by one pen.

The quotation marks are also round the wrong word for comfort. The sheet reads 3rd wife “Widow” Barbour — the compiler quoted Widow, the term being reported at second hand, and wrote Barbour unquoted, as if it were simply the name.

This measures the letters and nothing else. The same compiler elsewhere wrote Forney as “Barney”, so a clean reading of what the pen wrote is not a claim that the pen was right. Two smaller things fell out of the same magnification: the child‑thirteen wife reads Mumaw, not the uncertain “Numan” this file transcribed in July, which agrees with three other records already held; and three photographs long carried as duplicate sheets that might corroborate the chart turn out to be the same physical sheet re‑photographed flat, so they cannot corroborate it, because they are it.

The chart’s wife pedigree is a copy of a printed book — and the third wife is the one part of it that is not

The larger proposition was that all of this traces to one printed page. It largely does, and the dependency is now nameable. The chart writes that the ancestry of the name “traced back to 1560” — and 1560 is the opening span in the 1925 Ruthrauff history’s own title. A family sheet does not arrive at 1560 by memory; it arrives at it off a title page. The chart writes “Christine Ruthrauff dau of Johannes and Barbara Hoffman Ruthrauff”, against that book’s “Only daughter of Johannes and Anna Barbara (Hoffman) Ruthrauff, married Michael Welty”. Same two parents, same only‑daughter framing.

One loose thread, stated rather than smoothed over: the book never prints the alternative spelling the chart also uses. That came from somewhere else. And one thing the chart did not take from the book is the wife’s given name — the book says outright that it could not learn it and offers a guess off a deed of 1834. The chart names her. So the chart is a hybrid: the printed book for the pedigree apparatus, something else for the name.

The arithmetic is the load‑bearing part. Every witness that predates the chart gives two wives and ten plus seven equals seventeen children: the 1897 family letter, the 1902 biography, and the family’s own typed statement handed down, which calls itself the source text behind the later sheets. The chart keeps the seventeen, redistributes it thirteen and four, and appends a third wife carrying no children at all. The third wife is additive to a total that was already closed.

The printed book has no Barbour in it anywhere, on any spelling. So the one element of the chart’s wife block that its documented source cannot supply is precisely the element this file cannot place — the shape of a name that arrived from outside the printed pedigree and had to be parked somewhere. That sharpens the question rather than answering it, and it now rests on an arithmetic instead of an impression. Nothing whatever is adopted about who she was.

One consequence runs the other way and costs something: the chart stops counting as an independent witness to the Ruthrauff marriage and to the 1560 pedigree. It is a copy. That subtracts an apparent witness from the wife‑order argument, without touching its documentary side — the 1784 marriage register entry is untouched.

A subscription this file had been planning around was already dead, and the standing test for it could not tell

The account was signed in and the standing control returned the exact figure that has been taken since July as proof of a signed‑in session. It returned it while the same session could not open a single record: every View Record and every image link redirected to a membership offer. The control measured session, not entitlement. It was planted on the right question — am I seeing the full result set? — and answered it correctly; the question that actually gated the work was whether a record page would open. The fix is one sentence: the entitlement test is whether a record detail page renders, not whether a count changes.

What is still free on a lapsed but signed‑in account was measured rather than assumed: whole‑collection search, result counts, every facet count, the name column, the event‑type column, a partial place — and the whole browse hierarchy, county to township to denomination to organisation, which is a free catalogue of what a collection contains. Paywalled: the event date, the leading part of the place, the relatives column, every record page and every image. Nothing was purchased and no offer was clicked.

The free browse tree paid for itself immediately by bounding a collection this file leans on. Under Montgomery County, New Hanover resolves to exactly one denomination — Lutheran — with a single organisation under it. There is no Reformed congregation catalogued under New Hanover in that collection at all, so every question this file has asked of it about that corridor has been asked of the Lutheran side only. That is a statement about what the indexers catalogued, not about what exists in New Hanover, and it will still be true, and still free, in October.

The Dover register’s transcription recovered whole — and a question asked of it in July gets a different answer

The text of the printed translation of the Strayer’s (Salem) register is now recovered complete: a hundred and sixty‑five of the volume’s hundred and seventy‑four images, with the other nine shown to carry no text at all — covers, blanks and unread leaves. A residue that had been suspected of being a throttle was simply the end of the harvest.

The question put to this book in July was whether it holds burials and communicant lists. There is no burial register in it. But it does hold real communicant and confirmand lists, at images 161 to 165 — “Namen der Communicanten vom 22 sten Mai 1831, als 1 sten Pfingsttag” with nineteen men and thirty‑one women named, another for those who went to the Lord’s table on 27 April 1828, another for 29 April 1830 — and the confirmand lists carry ages to the quarter‑year, “Daniel Mitmann 34 1/2 Jahr”, which converts straight into a birth year. They are fifty years too late for any question this file has about the Dover generation, and they name nobody on the ladder. This closes a wrong description of a source rather than opening a lead.

An earlier pass had reported those lists as sitting on two early leaves. Those leaves are the consistory order — German prose setting out who may be admitted to the Lord’s table and how the elders are to examine members. A keyword count measures vocabulary, not record class: the page that says Abendmahl most often is the page of rules about it.

And an addenda section nobody had read, at the very end: “copies of loose notes, apparently contemporary with the events recorded, found in the church register by the editor” — two receipts of 1801 for nails and for shingles for the church, two baptisms, a pencilled death note of 1866, and a settlement of 24 January 1801 for £8:9:3 between the two congregations of Strayer’s Church over “said Strewers Church & Church Land”, naming five men of one board and five of the other. No Welty stands among the ten, which is a small measured absence at that church in 1801.

A Philip and an Elisabet at Strayer’s who are not Weltys — and a five‑week‑old flag that was answered before it was raised

A question left open in July: twins baptised at Strayer’s on 6 November 1786 were named Philip Jacob and Elisabet, and their sponsor line reads “Philip Jacob Julius; Elisabet”. Was Julius a garble of a Welty sponsor pair, which would have meant the twins were named for the Dover couple under the godparent‑naming custom this file relies on elsewhere?

It was not. Julius is a real and recurring surname in this register: one image carries a Philip Julius and Elisabet as parents in their own right, another a George Julius and Catharina. The garble reading is not needed and is not supported. Nothing is withdrawn from the 1786 sponsorship that names the Dover couple — that entry reads “Philip Welty Elisabet”, with the surname present. The firewall is against the next reading rather than that one: any future “Philip and Elisabet” hit in this register has to be read with its surname attached.

A small correction on the same pages. A baptism this file recorded as 22 March 1782 with the mother written “Anna Maria” reads, on the page, born 16 October and baptised 8 November 1782, mother written Maria — the stray March date belonging to the family entered immediately above. That reading was actually made here on 10 July and had been sitting in the record’s own notes ever since, while its headline said otherwise; a careful audit in mid‑August read the headline, believed it, and set a question going that had been answered five weeks earlier.

Worth saying plainly, because it cuts against tidiness: three different wife‑words stand against Philip‑shaped men in this one register inside six years — Maria in 1782, Anna Maria in 1781, Elisabet in 1786 — and more than one Philip is in play. They are not collapsed here, and the reading itself is machine text of a typescript, not the ruled page.

17 August 2026, later — two rival lists of one man’s children reconciled, and neither of them wins
The two compiled child lists are not two accounts. One is the other, plus a graft from a marriage that belongs to a different man

Two compilations of the children of the Cross Creek settler have sat in this file since early July, logged five weeks apart on different pages, neither mentioning the other. List A: Mary 1781, Catherine 1783, Margaret 1785, John 1786, Jacob 1789, Henry 1792, Abraham and Isaac as twins in 1794, Susan 1797, David 1799. List B: John 1786, Jacob 1789, Catherine 1791, Henry 1792, Margaret 1793, Isaac 1794, Abraham 1796, Christina 1796, David Howard 1798, and a Mary who married in 1821.

They looked irreconcilable because Catherine and Margaret sit eight years apart in the two. They are not the same two women moved. The compiler behind List A also assigns this man the wife and the marriage of 20 June 1780 that was ruled, in this file on 13 August, onto an entirely different Philip Jacob — the York man born in 1759. The three children List A has that List B does not, Mary 1781, Catherine 1783 and Margaret 1785, are exactly the children a June 1780 marriage produces, and that other man’s own recorded child list opens with a 1781, a 1782 and a 1785. Strip those three and List A begins at John 1786, precisely where List B begins. A compiler merging one sheet into the other produces List A exactly.

An arithmetic control from a source neither compiler saw. The 1890 biography says of the 1802 migration that “the seven children, five of whom were boys, took an active part in this work of settlement.” On List B the seven eldest in 1802 are five boys and two girls, exactly. On List A no cut yields five boys. Supporting, not decisive — read as a plain count of all children at the migration neither list fits, so the agreement depends on reading “the seven children” as those old enough to work.

So each child was scored against the records instead of against either list — and neither list survives whole

Where List B wins: David. It gives David Howard born 20 October 1798; a grave memorial gives David Howard Welday born 22 October 1798. The middle name, the year, and the day to within two. List A’s 1799 is wrong.

Where List A wins, and it is the strongest single weld on this family: Abraham. List A makes him Isaac’s twin in 1794; List B says 1796. His memorial gives born 3 May 1794 at Milford, Somerset County, Pennsylvania — which is exactly the father’s documented residence in those years, and the only birthplace on any child that ties to the household itself. Since another record independently dates Isaac to about 1794, the twins reading survives and List B’s splitting of them does not.

Where both are wrong: Henry, whom both put at 1792, against a grave card giving 4 August 1791 and his own 1871 declaration giving aged seventy‑nine in March of that year. And Jacob Jr, whom both put at 1789, against his own sworn 1850 declaration — “Jacob Wilday aged Sixty two years” — and an 1855 application “aged about 68”. A man’s own sworn age beats two compilers.

So the tree was rebuilt list‑agnostically rather than by picking a winner: each child’s date taken from the independent record where one exists, the rest marked as compiled‑only with both figures shown, the three grafted eldest re‑dated rather than deleted, a duplicated node merged away, and the father’s death moved from about 1837 to after 1839 on the signatures of his own estate appraisers. An eleventh child is on neither list — an Elizabeth who married in 1807, carried from the DNA project’s pedigree — and the maintenance deed of 1824 proves daughters, plural, while naming none of them.

A named daughter of the son who took the homestead — and a channel by which a family paper could have left the farm

An 1880 biographical sketch of a Wheeling grocer names his first wife as “Kesiah S., daughter of Mr. Isaac Welday, of Cross Creek”, married in March 1846, died 14 April 1876, with four children named and two grandchildren’s households after them. This file had her only from a county marriage index, which cannot carry parents. The two agree on the month and the year and now interlock.

Isaac matters more here than any other son. Under the maintenance deed of 6 September 1824 he took the homestead and all the personalty. If any family paper survived this household — a Bible, a chart, the “old copy of the family record” a later compiler was holding — it went with the personalty. A married daughter of his, in a city, with named grandchildren still living in 1880, is a documented channel by which such a paper could have left the farm. One grandson carries the surname as a given name and is in business with his father there, which is the kind of naming that survives in a city directory and an obituary.

Two volumes of printed newspaper abstracts opened for the first time, and a wife found by her initials

In the 1887 volume, an obituary of a man who died at Sedalia, Missouri lists his nine surviving siblings with residences, and one of them is “Mrs. H. C. Welday, St. Clairsville” — the town and the initials of the man whose printed biography carries the only statement of origin this file holds for the Ohio family. That gave her birth family, named, before her own name was known.

An independent corroboration of a marriage logged here in July, from a compiler who saw none of that work: an 1891 obituary says its subject married “Elizabeth Gladden, sister of Mrs. Alex. Weldy” and lived on the Gladden farm near Richmond — adding her father’s farm and a sister to a marriage previously known from an index alone.

Four further Weldays the tree does not hold, all 1891: a daughter of a David Welday of Cross Creek marrying in June; an infant son of a John and Lizzie Welday buried from a Bloomfield church; an Isaac Welday farming in Missouri, in a roll of men who had left New Alexandria; and a Mrs. Oliver Welday, daughter of a late Joseph Hervey of Bloomfield. Two of these attach to men already on the tree and neither is wired here.

The spelling measurement across both volumes is worth its own line: in this county’s press the family is Welday and Weldy and nothing else. The whole rest of the ladder — every umlaut form, every O‑vowel rung, every transliteration — returns zero, against live controls that show the text is real text. Grade: these are abstracts of a newspaper, compiled in the 1990s. Nothing in them is image‑verified and nothing is primary. They are a finding aid, not a record.

17 August 2026 — the county histories this campaign was built to read, and what the silence in them is worth
The origin question was put to the sketch the campaign was built around, and the answer is silence

Printed page 576 of the 1910 county history, read at the image with the running head verified: “CURTIS A. WELDAY, whose farm of 237 acres lies in Island Creek Township… He was born in this township, July 10, 1878, and is a son of William H. and Mary (Wyant) Welday. The late William H. Welday was born in Cross Creek Township and was a son of ALEXANDER WELDAY, one of the early settlers there.”

It recites Curtis A. from William H. from Alexander, and stops. No York County. No Pennsylvania. No Germany. No grandfather beyond Alexander, and no wife named for him. This is one of the four biographies in that volume the campaign existed to read, and it carries no statement of origin at all.

It also prints something awkward. It calls Alexander “one of the early settlers” of a township that was erected in 1806, when the same volume’s own settler roll names the first Welday there and does not mention Alexander — who was born about 1818, far too late. Read as a compiler’s loose honorific, not as a datum. What it does establish is that three generations now stand in print rather than on a user tree; it says nothing about Alexander’s father, so that link stays exactly where it was.

The object itself was measured in the same pass, and it explains why two of the four sketches could not be read. The freely scanned copy stops at printed page 606 of a book whose own index prints entries past 1,170. Of the four Welday biographies that index lists, only this one is inside the scan.

An 1890 volume never opened here recites an origin at last — Germany, and an immigrant grandfather named Abram

From the History of the Upper Ohio Valley, 1890, pages 685–686, read at the image: “Henry C. Welday, a capitalist of St. Clairsville… was born in Jefferson county, in 1823, the son of Henry and Nancy (Smith) Welday. HIS FATHER WAS BORN IN PENNSYLVANIA, AND CAME TO OHIO AT TEN YEARS OF AGE, IN 1802, WITH HIS FATHER, ABRAM WELDAY. THE LATTER, A NATIVE OF GERMANY, CAME TO PENNSYLVANIA ABOUT TIME OF THE CLOSE OF THE WAR OF THE REVOLUTION, and remained there until he came into Ohio, hauling his goods in wagons, and cutting their way through the woods… The seven children, five of whom were boys, took an active part in this work of settlement.”

It is unquestionably about the Henry this file already holds. Four independent points from his 1871 pension file land exactly: a birth between March 1791 and March 1792 against “came to Ohio at ten years of age, in 1802”; the War of 1812 service; the pension; and Nancy Smith, whose marriage of 12 December 1816 sits in that file as a certified county copy. Only the father’s given name is at odds.

Three things it adds that nothing here held. A third printed child count, agreeing with neither compiled list. Henry’s trade — carpenter, who “would go into the woods and hew out the timber, and make everything fit for its place by mental calculation”. And the disposition of his bounty land: the warrant land “afterward being sold to a lawyer at Cadiz”, a named place in a named county for a transaction this file has known only as two unread federal application files.

Do not adjudicate “Abram” — weigh it. Three readings and none of them tested: a compiler’s error, a grandson putting his father’s brother Abraham in his grandfather’s place, which is the commonest failure in a third‑hand sketch; a garbled memory belonging a generation higher, since a native of Germany arriving about the close of the Revolution cannot be the man who is already a rated single freeman on a York County return of December 1774, but could be a compressed recollection of the immigrant behind him; or there really is an Abram.

The test that separates them is ten years older, closer to the family, and names no grandfather at all

The 1880 county history, page 242, read at the image: “HENRY C. WELDAY, banker, was born in Jefferson county, Ohio, in 1823. He is the second son of Henry and Nancy Welday, nee Smith. They migrated from Pennsylvania in 1802, and settled in the above named county, in Cross Creek township. His father was a prominent farmer, and was elected one of the directors of the Steubenville bank, serving as such several years. In 1875 he died.”

No grandfather. Not Abram, not anyone. The Abram claim is absent from the earlier and closer of the two accounts, which dates it to somewhere between 1880 and 1890 and marks it as an elaboration rather than a family constant. Anyone quoting the Germany sentence should quote that beside it.

And from page 596 of the same volume, in the settlement narrative: “Jacob Welday, a German, came in 1800, and located on section 14.” Stated, not resolved, against the patent this file already holds for the south‑west quarter of section 15. A printed “came in 1800 and located on section 14” can perfectly well record where a man sat before he entered land — squatting on one section and later patenting the adjoining one is ordinary on those ranges, and section 14 adjoins section 15. It is not a rival to the patent and must not be cited as one. The county deed index answers it; preferring one printed sentence to another does not.

One thing here is new and entirely unworked: Henry was a director of the Steubenville bank for several years. Nothing in this file has ever looked at a bank’s records. Directors’ minutes, stockholder lists and charter petitions are a named, dated, institutional record class — for a man whose own estate has never been located, and they would place him among a board of neighbours.

The township did not exist yet, and the neighbourhood around him is unworked

From the township chapter, read at the image: “Cross Creek Township… was separated from the former on June 4, 1806. It is the sixth township of the second range and contains thirty‑six full sections. It was surveyed into sections by Alexander Holmes in 1801 and into quarter sections by Benjamin Hough in 1802.”

He arrived in 1801 or 1802. So for his first four or five Ohio years the township of record is Steubenville, and any township‑level instrument filed under Cross Creek before June 1806 either does not exist or is misfiled. Nothing in this file has ever been searched under Steubenville Township, and that is now the shape of the next errand rather than a hope.

The whole settler cohort was asked for rather than the target name — forty‑six surnames off the roll, plus the road viewers and the two surveyors, run against every evidence row this file holds. Eleven return anything at all, and every one of those is an incidental co‑occurrence already sitting inside a Welday record. Not one member of that neighbourhood is independently documented here. It is a zero worth recording, because a zero against an unworked neighbourhood is a statement about this project rather than about the neighbourhood, and it should be run again the moment any of those record classes is opened.

16 August 2026, evening — a surname off the family chart is put to the record, and the one estate it pointed at is twenty‑nine years too late
The chart calls his last wife a Widow Barbour. On his own ground, in his own years, that surname does not occur

The hand‑drawn family chart this file has worked from since July says that the Michael of Dover Township married, third, a “Widow Barbour”. The name had never been tested as a record. It has now been swept — Barbour, Barber, Barbor, Barbaur, Barbee, Barbre — across every free corpus covering the grounds he stood on in the years he stood on them: Dover Township in York County, Bedford County, Donegal and Derry in Westmoreland, Fayette County, and Dover Township in Tuscarawas County, Ohio. The spelling on the chart returns zero on every one of them.

The strongest single measurement is the general index to the third series of the printed Pennsylvania Archives, which indexes every printed county tax return, every militia roll of 1775–83 and every printed warrantee list from 1730 onward. It holds two Barbours in the whole series — “Barbour, Alex’r” and “Barbour, Jno.”, both Lycoming County, both on one page. Barbers do exist on his ground, thinly: one man in Westmoreland, William Barber of Derry Township, will dated 5 August 1796 and proven that 1 September, naming a lost son, a nephew’s children in Chester County and a neighbour who cared for him — and naming no wife at all.

What would reopen it is all in Pennsylvania and none of it is free: a Fayette County probate index for a Cornelious Barber, the manuscript Westmoreland assessments of 1787–1815, and the printed German church records of that county — because Pennsylvania registered no county marriages before 1885, so a church register is the only instrument that could hold the marriage at all.

The only estate on that ladder in the county’s whole probate index, dated at the docket page: 20 August 1844

The Tuscarawas index to administration dockets 1809–1878 and will records 1809–1910 carries exactly one entry anywhere on the Barb ladder: “BARBER, John, ad1‑3,230”. A page number in a finding aid is a locator and not a date, so the docket itself was opened. Page 230, case A 729, estate of John Barber: “Aug 20 1844 — Letters issued. Bond $400 — Robert Copeland & David Knisely, securities…”, bond filed the following day, inventory filed and recorded 29 October. The administrator’s given name is read at magnification and its first letter is not certain.

He died in September 1815. This administration is twenty‑nine years later. So the literal reading of the chart’s word has no probate footing in that county at all — and the county is now measured for that surname at census, tax duplicate, marriage register and probate alike. That is a removal and not a disproof. It says nothing about Pennsylvania, and it takes nothing from the widow herself: Catharine is documented alive and dowered in the 1828 partition and taxed in Dover Township in 1827 and 1828. What is missing is the name she carried before she married him.

Where the marriage is not: two county registers, one of them the parent county nobody had swept

Tuscarawas marriages 1808–1844, both surname blocks read at the page: Weldy — Daniel and Ann Bollman 1826, George and Sarah Altman 1816, Jacob and Elizabeth Butt 1810, John and Hannah Allman 1812, John and Anne Kitch 1825, Philip and Sarah Overholt 1816; Welty from 1829 onward. No Michael under any rung, in either spelling, anywhere in the book. The register demonstrably reaches into his years — the 1810 Butt marriage is the thirty‑ninth return in Book 1.

Then the door that opened behind it. Tuscarawas was erected out of Muskingum in 1808, and Muskingum’s Book 1 covers 1804–1818: all twenty letter pages parsed, 927 marriages, 104 of them before the erection. Zero on the ladder as groom or as bride, with the alphabetical slot plainly occupied on both sides — two Wellers stand immediately before Wells and Welsh. Zero Harbaugh and zero Bierbauer as well. And the control did not fire. A planted cohort of families known to be on Tuscarawas ground returns one hit in 927, so this book may simply not reach the upper valley: use it to find, not to exclude. What is left is Pennsylvania before about 1807, where no county register existed to record him.

Two readings the ground does support, offered as shapes and not as answers

Since the chart’s own spelling is unattested on every ground it names, two surnames that are heavily attested there are recorded as candidate readings of a handwritten word. The first is his own township in his own window: Philip and Casper Bierbauer stand in the Dover Township returns every year from 1779 to 1783, and the printed spelling changes annually — Berbower, Beerbrower, Beerbower, Bierbauer, Bierbrouer, Bierbrauer, Bierbach. Seven forms, five years, one source. The second is Harbaugh, whose equation with Horbach is printed in the county’s own history, and who is dense on every ground he ever stood on: a Casper Harbough is enumerated on the same printed page of the 1790 Bedford census as “Welty, Micael”.

Neither is adopted, neither is merged, and no Catharine has been found in either family. They are candidate readings of a word, not candidate people. The base rate for this kind of slip is inside the same document: the chart already writes another husband’s name wrong elsewhere.

The farm bought from Forney gets a date and a legal description

The 1816 duplicate of resident proprietors’ lands in Tuscarawas County, a fourteen‑page transcription of the whole county, carries the row “Weldy, Michael | 100 | 3 | 8 | 11 | NW qr … Abraham Feney … $2.25”, the last name being the column for the original proprietor of the tract. Abraham Feney is Abraham Forney — the man the 1884 county history says came from Somerset County about 1809, entered a farm on Crooked Run, and sold it to Michael Welty.
