So the sale is on or before 1816, and the tract is named for the first time: the north‑west quarter of Section 11, Township 8, Range 3, one hundred acres, the same Section 11 that carries the estate farm. A Jacob Weldy holds 150 acres a page later. This is a transcription and not the film, and the man taxed in 1816 is presumptively the son — Michael Sr died in September 1815, and an estate can be taxed to an heir. No identity is assigned.

1820, Dover Township: the household is found on the leaf, and one column rules the older man out

Michael Welty heads a household on leaf n178 of the Tuscarawas roll, in the run Frederick Reinhart – Abraham Overholt – John Collins – Joseph Shrock – Michael Welty – Andrew Critz – Peter Walters, with Abraham Welty on the same leaf, Jacob Welty on the next, and a John Witty heading a house in Sugar Creek. Abraham Overholt standing four names away is the family Philip of 1789 married into in 1816.

The row itself was then read. These schedules were copied onto ruled paper with no printed column headings, so the leading narrow cell cannot be told from the name column’s margin and two alignments one column apart are possible. What survives both alignments is the column that matters: the sixth male column, “of 45 and upwards”, falls on a dash either way. There is no man of forty‑five or over in that house. Whoever he is, he was not born before 1775 — an exclusion, not an identification, and the full bracket profile waits on reconciling the page against its own totals and the county recapitulation.

A record class a surname index is built not to reach

On the page facing the Barber entry: case A 731, estate of Richard T. Bunnell, administrator John Welty, 1844. A probate index keyed on the dead man can never surface a Welty who appears as administrator, security or appraiser. The docket now has a film, a manuscript index and a page‑to‑image formula fitted at three points, so the four ladder pages the printed index points at — including a Wilty Abraham Sr. standing in the same county’s probate record — are one session’s reading rather than a search.

A Welt household inside a Chester County monthly meeting — a confession this file has never met

Six index rows, three people, eighteen years, all on the bare stem: Sarah Welt in May, June and July 1768, the first of the three indexed Wielt; Joseph Welt and Susanna, intention 16 March 1769 and marriage that 11 May; Ann Welt, 15 June 1786. They surface under four separate rungs and are the same six rows under each, so the ladder is expanding rather than finding four families. Not one leaf was opened — that is the next step and it is one session’s work. The interest here is the confession rather than the county: the Edenkoben line is Reformed, the Manheim–Ohio family is Brethren and Mennonite, and a Barbara Welty stands on the 1770 Coventry Brethren roll in this same county. A Welt family inside a monthly meeting is a fourth setting and is unaccounted for. The Chester Wildy family of the 1740s is named as the firewall to test first, and is not asserted.

The same afternoon’s other result is bibliographic, and it is worth more than the search zero beside it: for a German Elizabeth born in Chester County in 1728 there is no register in which she could stand. Vincent Reformed’s baptisms begin in 1733; Brownback’s pre‑1832 register is lost rather than unread; and the Coventry Brethren baptise believers, so that congregation has no infant record class at all, by doctrine and not by loss. Only the Quaker registers reach the year, and their 1728 Chester cohort of that given name is twelve records, six surnames and exactly one birth — an Elizabeth Allen with no date, no place and no parents carried in the index — while none of the six surnames occurs anywhere in this project’s papers. The place field on that collection is blank on a large share of its rows, so the honest statement is a zero among rows the index placed in Chester. The Chester birthplace this file once carried for Michael was retired long ago and none of this reopens it.

16 August 2026, later — the York register has a hole in it exactly where this file has been looking, and a name off the ladder stands in a list dated January 1754
A correction to the entry of 14 August: the eighteenth‑century burial page is on this film after all, and the book is not offline

Two nights ago this page reported that the York 1744 volume had been walked end to end, that the eighteenth‑century death page was not on the film, and that the book it belonged to was therefore somewhere off it. The leaves were opened today and all three sentences are wrong.

Node 1, image 9: a ruled display heading with the year 1762 set in the left margin and, across the head of the column, “Notitz der Abgestorbenen” — record of the deceased — the entries separated by ruled lines and numbered between them in the clerk’s own centred cipher. Read in order: Dionisius Meyer, buried 31 December 1762, aged 24 years 3 months; Görg Jacob Rummel, aged 3, buried 6 January; Killian Schmid, died and buried 12 January 1763, aged 69 years 10 months and 2 days; Johann Peter Steiger, 4 months 3 days, buried 13 January; Margaretha, of the Hack household, 4 years 4 months; Jacobus Welsch Hans, aged 47, buried 2 March 1763. That is the section the 1930s typescript was copying, entry for entry — a content match rather than a folio‑number match, which is the honest grade for it.

And the map of the front of the book was wrong with it. Image 3 is the title page, signed by the pastor for York, Kreutz Creek, Codorus and Bermudian. Images 4 to 6 are the church order in numbered articles, closing “den 30 April 1751”. Image 7 is a numbered subscription list dated 3 January 1754. Image 8 is prose of 1762 with lists of officers and the first ruled burial entry at the foot. Image 9 is the burial run. The baptisms do not begin until image 10, under a hatched cartouche reading “die Nahmen der Kinder … von Anno 1744” over four columns for infants, parents and witnesses.

The baptisms run to May 1745 and then start again in 1762 — and the years this file needs are in the gap

Read off the book’s own date lines, leaf by leaf. Image 10 opens “Den 2 Augst: 1744” and reaches 17 March 1745. Image 11 recto continues in the same four‑column form and carries four date lines, every one of them 1745, the last “Den 26 May 45” standing about eight entries above the foot of the leaf. Image 11 verso is a new section head: a Latin display heading over a German dedicatory paragraph that closes with the year set large and alone — “Von 1762.” Image 12 is 1762 throughout, and in a different form altogether, running prose per entry with a line naming the sponsors.

The surviving book has no baptismal leaves between the middle of 1745 and 1762. Since 1 July this file has carried the sentence “the earliest Welty entry in this register is 1763” and read it as a fact about the family. It is a fact about the book: an entry earlier than 1762 was not possible for any family, on any rung, in any spelling. The birth window this project is working — roughly December 1753 to August 1755 — falls squarely inside the hole. The claim covers four consecutive openings that were each opened and read; the rest of the volume is still unread.

Why the hole may be there: the church burned on 4 July 1797, and all the records went with it save one book

From the 1907 county history, in the account of the pastor’s second ministry: “During this period the stone church, built in the time of Rev. Wirtz, was destroyed by fire on July 4, 1797, and all the records were burnt save one book.” The 1886 history narrates the same building sequence independently. The one book is the 1744 volume — the volume every negative this file has recorded at that congregation was measured against.

This is print rather than manuscript, and a 1907 statement about a 1797 event is secondary by construction. What it changes is not the silence but its meaning: not “he was not baptised at this church” but “he is not in the one book of this church that came through a fire.” Where the leaves of 1746 to 1761 went is untested. They may have been in a second book that burned; they may be in the pastor’s own private pastoral record of 1744 to 1769, which survives, is reachable, and has never been read. That is now the most valuable unopened object on this ground.

A name off the ladder in a dated manuscript list of 3 January 1754 — inside the birth window, read at the image

Image 7 carries, beneath a rule, the date line “geschehen den obigen dato d[en] 3 Jan: 1754”, and under it a numbered list of men in two columns on the recto and continuing on the facing page — one hand, one ink, continuous numbering. On the verso stands Goerg Weldy: read at more than four times native resolution, a tall looped l, a looped d, and a terminal descender carrying the clerk’s dot.

The control is inside the same list. At recto line 20 stands “Michael Weidre” and at the head of the verso “Christoffel Weidre”; cropped at native resolution and stacked against the first name, they are structurally different words. That settles two things at once. Two printed county histories reprint a list of members “before 1754” carrying a George Weldey, and one of them prints a Michael Welder where the other prints Weider — the manuscript shows the printed list is this list, and that the Welder is a Weidre. And it places a ladder name at that charge on a named day, earlier than the 1755 baptism and the 1757 covenant signature already published here. No identity is assigned and the obvious candidate is not adopted; the list has not yet been read to its foot.

And a probable ladder household as parents in the mid‑1740s, in the book that was supposed to hold nothing before 1763

Image 10 recto, in the run that opens 2 August 1744: an infant “Cath: Elisabeth”, parents “Michel Wel…” and Margaretha Barbara, witnesses Johannes Wolff and Catharina. The surname was first read as Welsch and that reading is withdrawn here rather than left in a footnote: there is no s‑c‑h on the page.

What it is not, each excluded against a control in the same hand: it is not a ‑tz name, because the clerk’s own ‑tz stands eleven lines above in a witness’s name and is a tall crossed t joined to a looped z, and this word has no crossbar; it is not the Weldy of the 1754 list three leaves later, which has an unmistakable dotted descender; and it is not the Wolff standing in its own column. The stem and a medial looped d are verified at the image and the terminal letter is not settled, so the reading is probable and not proven — one word, on one leaf, that a better raster would decide. If it holds, the register’s “no Welty before 1763” was never a fact about arrival; it was an artefact of a transcription that never covered these leaves.

The one Pennsylvania record class that states where a man was born, read at last — and the ladder is not in the legible part of it

The enlistment tables in the fifth series are printed sideways on a portrait page, so the published text layer renders them as noise: over the band worked here it turns the column head “Remarks” into a string of characters running backwards, and not one leaf returns a legible column head. Every machine sweep that counted this band as searched was reading rubbish.

Sixty‑eight leaves were pulled at native resolution, rotated three ways and read again; thirty‑five came back by rotation and thirty‑three read best unrotated, being the horizontal muster rolls interleaved with them. Over the forty‑six leaves recovered legibly, no name on the ladder appears in the place‑of‑birth column. Twenty‑two did not recover and are listed individually, so the re‑run is a list rather than a search. This is machine‑read and not eyes‑on, and the zero covers only what came back readable.

What two of today’s zeroes were actually measuring

The register’s silence before 1763 was measuring a fire and sixteen missing years of leaves. The fifth series’ silence over the enlistment tables was measuring a page set sideways in the press. Neither was measuring a family, and both had been read here as though they were. An instrument has to be described before its zero means anything.

16 August 2026 — a single digit on a 1781 tax leaf, and a second copy of the list that quietly agrees with it
A one‑digit correction to a figure published here last night, and it changes who stands tallest in the 1781 column

The entry below quoted the Dover class list of 1781 as “Philip Welte 666 / Philip Welte Jun 016 / Michel Welte 082…”. The first figure is 066. At magnification it carries the same leading zero every other entry on that leaf uses, and a 666 standing beside neighbours of sixteen and eighty‑two never sat right. The entry has been corrected in place, and so has every working file that repeated it.

It matters more than one digit usually does. At 666 the elder Philip read as a substantial holder standing over the rest of the column; at 066 he sits in the ordinary run, and the largest figure among the five Weltes belongs to Michel, at 082. Nothing about the junior changes — he is still the smallest of the five, still in the young‑man band. What changes is the shape of the group.

And the correction paid for itself. The same bundle holds a second copy of that column, written out for the collector in a different hand. It reads Philip at 64 and Jacob at 42, against the leaf’s 066 and 041 — agreement within a unit or two, on a copy whose figures run a shade low throughout. Against 666 they agreed with nothing at all. A misread number is not only wrong; it hides the corroboration that would have caught it.

15 August 2026, evening — the Edenkoben burials that survive after 1771, and a surname the index deletes
A volume ended in 1771; the parish’s burial record did not

Since 2 July this file has carried a sentence that closed a question at the top of the Palatine pedigree: no Edenkoben burials survive before 1707 or after 1771, so the death of Hans Jacob Wäldi — the oldest man in this line, with no baptism of his own — cannot be tested. The reading behind it was honest and the volume was swept properly. The sentence was about a volume and it was written about a parish.

The repository’s free public catalogue lists twenty‑eight Edenkoben registers. This file had ever named five. Two of the unnamed ones carry burials from 1772 to 1805. One of them was opened this evening, and it is a good instrument: not a bare list but prose, giving age at death to the day and often the cause — “aetat 27 Jahr 6 Monath 14 Tage”, “Johann Philipp Lehn, aetat 63 Jahr 2 Monath, starb an der Auszehrung”. For a man with no baptism, an age at death is the whole ballgame.

It was then mapped leaf by leaf — the burial series runs across fifty‑four images at roughly one and a fifth leaves to the year — and the first eleven years, 1772 to 1782, were swept entry by entry on the full ladder, both page‑sides, including the feminine suffix forms and the searches by relationship rather than by surname (“the wife of…”, “the surviving widow of…”). An external control was planted first — the wife’s own family, who ought to be dying in this parish in these years — and it fired five times. Neither the old man nor his wife is in the eleven years. The remaining twenty‑three years, 1783 to 1805, are unread, as is the second volume, and that is where the sweep goes next. The 1707–1771 reading is untouched by any of this; only the sentence generalised out of it is.

The apex’s home parish is tested at last — on confirmations, which nobody had thought to ask for

Six parishes were ruled out for the baptism of Hans Jacob Wäldi around 1691, and the ruling was correct for what it searched. It searched baptisms. Checked against the catalogue this evening, three of the six hold confirmation registers that have never been opened, and the years they cover are precisely the years a boy born in exile during the war would be confirmed: one runs 1681–1784, one 1682–1706, one 1688–1706. A fourth parish holds a communicant roll running 1667–1767 — a presence instrument spanning the entire evacuation, against which only a baptism register had been tried.

The one in the documented home parish was read the same hour, 1698 to 1714, which is the first time any instrument has tested this man’s own generation where his family actually was. The result is a clean zero, and the leaves argue for themselves rather than resting on a search: 1699 has its heading written and no entries under it, a genuinely blank year rather than an unread one; 1702 and 1712 do not appear at all, which are parish gaps; and the three nearest confusable surnames on those leaves — an attested local family among them — were each read at maximum magnification and refused. The two leaves were afterwards bought at native resolution and re‑read whole, which corrected three of the evening’s own earlier readings, including a word read as a family name that is in fact “Ambtmans”, the bailiff’s.

What this measures is that parish, not wherever the congregation sheltered between 1690 and 1697, which remains the real question. Three of the four new instruments are still unopened. And a fourth premise fell the same evening: a register dismissed in July as beginning a year too late to hold the baptism turns out to be a different book in a different archive — the right one carries baptisms from 1680 to September 1707 and confirmations from 1688 to 1706, so the cheapest online move at the top of this pedigree exists again after all.

A survey draught of 1750, a tax column of 1751, and a warrantee named Catharine

Three instruments were set against each other tonight and they agree, which is not something this file often gets to write. The first is a copied survey draught returned 8 August 1750, image‑verified at four hundred dots per inch: a hundred and seventy‑six acres in Hempfield Township surveyed to John Newcomer, its adjoiners written round the margin — Jacob Heistant north, Herman Long and John Coffman east, Henry Heistant south, and Christian Welty west.

The second is the 1751 assessment column that produced this afternoon’s widow: “Henry Musselman / Herman Long / John Cauffman / Hans Newcomer / Widow Welty / Joseph Cochenour…” — which reproduces the draught’s own adjoiners in the draught’s own spatial order. A base‑rate control was run across all six surviving township lists before that was allowed to mean anything, and one of the draught’s names failed it and was struck from the set. This is not an adjacency argument — the rule here forbids reading proximity on a tax list as kinship, and it still does. It is a test of whether one of these lists preserves ground order at all, and on this stretch it does.

The third is the Land Office’s own patent index, read at four hundred dots per inch: a warrant of 8 June 1743 and a patent of 30 May 1746 for two hundred and sixty‑seven acres in Lancaster County, patentee “Welty, Christian” and warrantee “Catharine Welty”. That reading was parked in doubt on 13 August as possibly the project’s own misreading; nine lines below on the same leaf, in the same hand, the clerk writes “White, Cotharine” in one column and “Catharine White” in the other, which fixes his letterforms and confirms the word. The competing reading — one Christian throughout, and a clerk’s slip — is live and is not decided here. Nothing joins Catharine to the widow of 1751 except a surname, a county and a decade.

And a county history of 1924, reached through a repository this project had never queried, puts the tract on a map for the first time: “Christian Welty had 267 acres of land situated about a mile northeast of the Lancaster and Marietta turnpike; he received patent for that tract in 1746, but probably had settled upon it many years earlier.” The last clause is the author’s inference and must never be quoted as a record. Two sentences above, he describes a neighbouring tract “part of it in Manor and part in East Hempfield” — which dissolves the contradiction that the survey says one township and the assessment says the other.

A surname index that prints thirteen page references, and a machine text that hands them to the wrong family

A 1936 surname index to the standard Frederick County history is free and open. Read at native raster, one line of it prints: “Welty, 324, 460, 546, 560, 631, 723, 733, 1083, 1118, 1358, 1405, 1445, 1446”, with “Weltner, 8, 86, 479” on the line above.

That same page’s machine text deletes the word Welty entirely and reattaches all thirteen page numbers to Weltner. Six controls on the same page fired correctly. Nothing in the search would have shown it; only the image caught it. This is the third distinct way this week that a page has read as empty while carrying the name — after the sideways tables and the ruling line at Coventry — and it is the worst of the three, because an index is exactly the instrument a researcher trusts to be complete.

Ten of the thirteen pages have since been read in the free 1910 edition of the history itself, and they are the already‑known nineteenth‑century Maryland families. The one entry that looked eighteenth‑century is a mirage: the text layer reads “Trucks, George Welty, John Wimmer” in the printed 1790 census, and the government’s own edition shows the true run is “Trucks, Geo. / Welty, John / Wimmer, Abraham” — a given name shifted one surname to the right across three entries. There is no George Welty there, which confirms rather than disturbs the reading this file already had. Three of the thirteen pages remain unread.

The Württemberg parish becomes reachable in its own right, and a transcription tool invents a page it cannot read

Every register entry this file holds for the Württemberg village came through one subscription; the other one — the one used all summer for the Palatinate — holds the same parish as twenty‑four volumes of its own, and not one had ever been opened here. They are now enumerated with their identifiers. The volume opened this evening is not what its catalogue title suggests: its first leaf reads “Communicanten Buch”, signed by the pastor and dated 23 June 1721, with the parish’s cost of paper and binding noted underneath. Its first roll carries the two men of the godparent family that locks this line into that village. It is a presence instrument covering years the event registers do not — and it ends in 1748, six years before the crossing, so it cannot name the son this file is looking for. The long hunt for a soul‑register at that village also has an answer: there is no volume of that name there. The neighbouring parishes have them.

One caution belongs in public, because anyone working these registers will meet it. The same subscription offers on‑demand machine transcription. A credit was spent on a two‑column list of about two hundred names, and it returned fluent, grammatical German prose that is not on the page — whole invented sentences, with real surnames from the leaf scattered through them. A typed film target card on an adjacent image transcribed flawlessly. On a page of running prose it may well be useful; on a list, it produces text that reads like a transcription and is not one, and a zero taken from it is worth nothing.

On the Dover return of December 1774 the rateable Welty freeman is the older of the two, not the younger

No record was opened for this one. Three readings already in this file, each verified at its own image and never before set side by side, were simply put in a row. On the Dover return of 8 December 1774 a Jacob Welty is rated as a single freeman — which puts him at twenty‑one by then, born about 1753 or earlier. The Michael of Dover Township is measurably not in that block, and the block is known to rate single sons against their listed fathers, because it does so for another family on the same leaf. Michael’s earliest appearance anywhere is March 1778. And the 1800 census in the west puts Jacob’s household head in the forty‑five‑and‑upward bracket, born before 1755.

So on the earlier of the two instruments the Welty freeman is the older man, and it is Jacob. Several rows in this file still frame him as the younger brother on the strength of a birth year that came from a 1946 magazine query rather than from a record, and that year has now been contradicted twice. It also re‑reads the tax geometry in the plainer direction: Jacob taking eighty and then a hundred acres while Michael holds forty‑four as a tenant and is landless by 1786 looks like ordinary elder‑son succession rather than an oddity. The caution stated with it: absence from a freemen block means not rateable in this township, which under‑age satisfies but so do not resident and bound elsewhere. Nothing here touches whose sons they were.

“Philip Welte Jun” has stood on the richest Dover document in the file for a fortnight, and nobody asked whose junior he was

Five Weltes stand consecutively in one column of the Dover class list of 1781: “Philip Welte 066 / Philip Welte Jun 016 / Michel Welte 082 / Jacob Welte 041 / George Welte 064”. The suffix is not an artefact of the order — this clerk is known to mark senior and junior when two men of one name stand in one township, and he leaves the others unmarked. At the smallest valuation of the five, the junior is in the young‑man band.

He has no roster row, no identifier and no place on any candidate list on any tab. The question was asked in terms in July — whether the second Philip in that township is a son or a brother — and it was never run. The unexciting answer is the leading one: a Philip Jacob born 1759 who married at York in June 1780 and whose father’s household was in Dover — that father stands fifth in this same column — would be a newly married twenty‑two‑year‑old in his father’s township, and he belongs to a branch already firewalled from this one. There are four men of that name already kept carefully apart in these files. A fifth will not be created on a valuation and a suffix.

A Wälder household in the parish itself — a ladder‑shaped surname, the same compound given name, and a seat on the village court

Four entries in the same burial volume, three householders, read the same evening. 5 October 1782: “Joh: Conrad, des hiesigen Bürgers und Metzger‑Meisters, Johann Jacob Wälder, ehel. Söhnlein von 9 Monathen” — a master butcher’s infant son. 16 September 1783: “M. Margaretha, H. Philipp Jacob Wälder, Gerichts‑Verwandten und Schaffneris dahier, Ehefrau, in dem 39ten Jahr”, so his wife was born about 1744. October 1784: a son Johann Philipp of the same man, dead at seventeen. 8 March 1785: an infant daughter of a Gottfried Wälder. A Gerichtsverwandter sits on the village court, and with the honorific and a second office this is an officeholding household rather than a cottager’s.

The surname took two plates and the scribe’s own hand as the control. The viewer cannot resolve the word at all, so native leaves were bought for the first two entries. On the first, the second letter carries a superscript of two short parallel strokes — and the same scribe on the same leaf writes his u‑umlaut as two round dots and his o‑umlaut as a broad connected zigzag, while an unambiguous a‑umlaut elsewhere on the leaf takes the two strokes exactly. Thirteen months later the same hand writes the name again, and the fourth letter is a plainly looped d with no crossbar.

No connection to the Edenkoben line is claimed, nothing is merged, and nobody is added to the tree. What makes it worth publishing is that this is the first household of that name located in the home parish itself, in the right century, carrying the compound given name this file has to watch. And a caution belongs beside it: six leaves earlier in the same volume stands Philipp Jacob Walter, “der alte Eisenmüller”, buried in November 1776 in his sixty‑second year — same parish, same compound given name, a milling trade, and a different word on the page: a bare round a with nothing above it and a plainly crossed t. The two are distinguishable at the letter and must not be run together. What will decide the Wälder family is not more burials but the marriage register: a wife dead in her thirty‑ninth year in 1783 and a son born about 1767 put that marriage around 1764 to 1766, and it would name both fathers.

15 August 2026, later — a widow remarrying in New Hanover, and a woman named in her own right at Coventry
The Trappe register read end to end: five hundred and sixty‑four thousand characters, and exactly two names on the ladder

Having taken one entry out of that volume in the morning, the whole of it was read in the afternoon — marriages from 1730, confirmations from 1745, burials from 1746, a subscription list of 1760, and a second congregation’s register bound after it. On the full ladder, diacritics folded and hyphenation repaired, the entire volume yields two occurrences. One is the confirmand of 1 June 1760. The other is new:

“Frohaeuser, Johan Kraft, as a widower / Weltin, Christina, as a widow / September 7, 1755, in New Hanover.”

The entry above hers is a marriage of 17 August 1755 and the one below is of 9 September, both in the register’s ordinary form; the page feminises surnames throughout, so Weltin is what this book calls a woman of the name. She is a widow in September 1755. Seven months earlier, in February 1755, administration was granted in Philadelphia on the estate of John Welte, blacksmith, of Hannover Township, whose wife is recorded as Christina and who baptised two daughters in 1753 and 1754 and then disappears from the registers. That is a candidate identification and it is not made here: no document in hand joins the two women, and there are other households of the name in the same district. What would join them is the administration bond, which names the widow, and which nobody has read.

The place of the June 1760 confirmation was settled at the same time, as far as the book allows. The class before it is headed “At New Providence:”; the 1760 heading carries no place line at all, and every away‑class elsewhere in the register — Pikestown, the Oley mountains, New Germantown — is explicitly labelled. So it reads as the home congregation by the book’s own convention, which is an inference from a habit, not a stated fact, and it is published at that grade. One further check was worth the trouble: a rival scan of the same title sets the two columns interleaved, and its machine text pairs the surname with the wrong person entirely — wrong sex, wrong age, wrong father. A scan that keeps the columns apart lets the pairing be counted, eight surnames against eight entries, and the eighth pairs with the eighth.

Barbara Welty, communicant at Coventry in 1770, traced back past four reprints to the census that made her

A denominational record class this project had never touched — the Brethren, or Dunkers — produced a member roll: “List of members in 1770, of the Coventry Brethren church: Martin Urner, minister, and wife, Peter Reinhart, exhorter, Owen Reinhart, Henry Dasker and wife … Christian Monsieur, Barbara Miller, Barbara Welty, Frederick Reinhart and wife, Barbara Urner, Elizabeth Ingles …” The roll’s own grammar is the finding as much as the name: it collapses married couples into “and wife” and names women individually only when they stand alone. Nine women are named in their own right on this list and she is one of them, which makes her single, widowed, or a householder.

Four separately published works print the roll and all four read Barbara Welty while disagreeing with each other about other surnames — which looked like independent corroboration until the provenance was run down. It is not. An 1898 congregational history quotes a sketch that says in its own words, “The above list is taken from Morgan Edwards’s work” — the 1770 printed census of the American Baptists. One witness copied four times, not four witnesses. So the first edition was pulled instead, and the name was read at native raster on the 1770 leaf itself: “Barbara Welty,”, terminal y and comma unambiguous, between Barbara Miller, and Frederick Reinhart and wife. The reading is now two removes better than it was this morning, and it rests on the printer of 1770 rather than on a reprint of 1900.

She is not attached to anyone. She is not the Barbara of the Ohio household, not Family A’s, not anybody’s yet. The same 1770 census enumerates fourteen Pennsylvania congregations with their counties, four of them in York County including one in the township next to Dover, and the York rolls carry no name on the ladder at all. One warning for anyone reading these pages by machine: a ruling line crosses the word on the leaf, and the text layer renders it “Barbara Welt” — a whole‑word search returns nothing on a page that plainly reads Welty.

Whose son was the confirmand? The arithmetic says cousin rather than brother — and it dislodges a household identification on the way

The obvious question was then asked of the morning’s find: is Jacob, son of the late Johannis, a brother of the Michael of Dover Township? The answer was worked out of records already in this file, without opening anything new, and it is probably not — more likely a first cousin, and possibly neither.

Three men named Johannes are candidates for his father. The Philadelphia blacksmith, dead by February 1755, fits the death requirement but the arithmetic is tight: a son born about 1739 and daughters in 1753 and 1754 is possible but crowded, and the full‑sibling window against Michael’s own birth bracket narrows to about eleven months. A shipboard Johannes of 1751 was assigned to a decoy in July and that assignment was withdrawn a fortnight ago, so he is unassigned and available. And the Johannes baptised at Edenkoben in 1713 has no household there baptising a Jacob at the right date. A conclusion reached on 4 July, that this Johannes died young in the Palatinate, turns out to rest on the assignment that was withdrawn — and was never amended when it fell.

The stronger result is a by‑product. A New Hanover household headed by a Joh. Jacob Welte and a Maria Margareth had been identified as the uncle baptised at Edenkoben in 1710. It cannot be him. The family record gives that man a second wife married in March 1757 with a child born in 1762, which cannot be reconciled with a different wife bearing a child in September 1761. The identification is refused, and the vacated slot fits the new confirmand rather well — confirmed at twenty in June 1760, a first child fifteen months later. That is a shape, not a proof, and it is published as one. Y‑DNA cannot arbitrate any of it: the three Edenkoben brothers share one chromosome, and no descendant of the middle one is in the surname project to test.

A widow assessed in her own name at Manor Township in 1751, found by looking for relationships instead of a first name

Every machine sweep this project has run has ranked its results by nearness to the word Michael. That is a filter shaped like the answer, and it cannot see a woman, a widow, or a son named as somebody’s child. A different triage was built — three axes, none of them a given name: is a feminine word or the word widow nearby, is a kinship formula nearby, and does the surrounding text have the shape of a list. All seven controls fired, and the three quarry‑shaped records already known sorted straight to the top, where the old method scores all three at nothing.

It paid out once, in a printed transcription of the surviving Lancaster County tax lists: “… henry musselman / herman long / john cauffman / hans newcomer / widow welty / joseph cochenour / frederick hoffman …” — Manor Township, 1751, a taxable householder in her own right, and the only name on the ladder in the whole surviving 1751 corpus for that county. The volume’s own printed index carries her too, alphabetised between Weller and Wendling, which is a check no machine misreading passes.

The grade is a text layer over a printed transcription and the manuscript behind it has not been found; the word widow may be the collector’s convenience rather than a probate fact, and about forty such entries stand in the volume. What the pass also surfaced is bigger than the entry: the editor’s own preface says the county’s tax records for roughly forty years in the middle of the century have never been published — a whole body of assessments outside every printed corpus swept here to date.

The Philadelphia errand was aimed eight years wide, and the surname it turns on occurs once in half a billion characters

The next step everyone agreed on was the Philadelphia Orphans’ Court, on the chance that a guardianship names the blacksmith’s children. The films this file has been citing for it cover 1763 to 1775, which is correct for the row that first named them and eight years too late for a 1755 death — and a note written ninety minutes earlier the same afternoon had quoted them for exactly that. Two films that do cover 1755 were named for the first time, one of them a volume of estate papers for 1749–1757 never mentioned here before. Both were checked and both are behind an access padlock; no name search reaches them, and a control confirms the search itself is scoped away from those films rather than merely empty.

The remarried widow’s new surname was then measured rather than searched for. In five hundred and eighteen million characters of printed corridor literature it occurs exactly once, and the once is this project’s own marriage entry. It is absent from the full‑text corpus of Pennsylvania records before 1790 in thirteen spellings. Whatever became of that household, it left no printed trace — so the question of who raised the blacksmith’s daughters has to be asked of manuscripts, not of books.

The cheapest remaining door was costed instead of merely named. The county’s Register of Wills states in its own words that it is “the only repository in possession of original Philadelphia Administration files”; the request is a letter with a twenty‑dollar fee and a return envelope, and this file already holds everything the form asks for — the name, the date, the file number and the book and folio. Before spending it there is a free test worth twenty minutes: an earlier administration book from the same office is in print, and reading a dozen of its entries will show whether entries of this class name a widow at all.

Five Maryland congregations named and dated in 1772, and a printer who says plainly why there are no members’ names

The 1770 census had a companion volume for Maryland, Virginia and the Carolinas, written in 1772, and it reaches the exact ground on which the Manheim‑to‑Frederick group of these families is documented. It names and dates five Frederick County congregations — Conococheague, begun 1743; Monocacy, 1749; Middlecreek, 1747; Antietam, 1750; Pipe Creek, 1751 — with their ministers, and then simply stops. There are no member rolls in it at all. That is a property of the genre, verified at two independent witnesses, and the compiler says why in his own voice twenty years later, of a different congregation: “I am sorry I could not come at more of this good man’s history (for these people keep no records).” A congregational historian said the same thing of Coventry in 1898, and a Maryland historian said it again in 1936. Three independent witnesses across a hundred and sixty years agree that the early books of this denomination do not survive.

What the volume does give is a cohort walking the corridor this file has been arguing about. The minister at Middlecreek in Maryland in 1772 is the same man the Pennsylvania volume records leaving Codorus in York County — “this Tanner left them to go to Monocacy” — the Pipe Creek minister shares a surname with a woman standing a few names from Barbara Welty on the Coventry roll, and the man named superintendent of all the Maryland congregations later moves them south to the Pee Dee in Carolina. Nobody on the ladder is named in any of it. It is the road, described by someone who walked it in 1772.

The denomination’s western Pennsylvania history was swept alongside it — a million characters, forty‑one spellings, and a clean zero — over a book that opens: “First Settlers, 1762. Among the first settlers that came from the East across the Alleghany Mountains into Somerset County, about 1762 or 1763, was a colony of Brethren, who settled in the northern part of what was then called The Glades.” That is precisely the ground the Milford man stands on, and the narrative of it carries no name on the ladder. It is a narrative and not a membership register, which is the reopener: the congregations’ own rolls, if any survive, have not been looked for.

15 August 2026 — a confirmand named for his dead father, and a militia list taken in the Glades
“Welty, Jacob, s. late Johannis, age 20 years” — the first time a Welty in America is named as somebody’s son

A confirmation class of 1 June 1760, printed in a nineteenth‑century edition of the Trappe registers and read at native raster, runs eight boys deep. Seven of them are ordinary. The eighth is not: “Welty, Jacob, s. late Johannis, age 20 years.” The register uses late as its own standing convention for a dead parent — it does so repeatedly on the same pages, for other families — and it gives every confirmand’s age.

Four things follow from one line, and none of them is a merge. A Jacob Welty born about 1739 or 1740. A father named Johannes. That father dead by 1 June 1760. And the family inside the Lutheran circuit that ran out of Trappe — the same corridor between Philadelphia and the Schuylkill in which every other eighteenth‑century record of the name in this file sits. This is the first record any search here has produced that names a Welty as the child of a named Welty on American soil.

Two limits, plainly. It is not the Michael of Dover Township, and it is not attached to the Edenkoben line: there is a Johannes baptised at Edenkoben in 1713 who has never been accounted for, and the temptation to make him this Johannes is exactly the kind of move this file exists to refuse. And the entry was reachable only because the search was run on the surname with no given name attached — every earlier sweep of this register class had been keyed to a given name, and a confirmand named as his father’s son is invisible to that.

Michael Wildy on the Milford Township list of 5 February 1789, standing among the same neighbours the church books already gave him

A printed county militia return, taken fourteen months before the first federal census, reads: “A list of the inhabitants of Milford Township subject to the performance of militia duty, taken by Ludwic Young 5th. Feby. 1789”. Fourteen names into it stands Michael Wildy, and the names around him are not strangers: Frederic Wimer and his son, John Waggoner, David, Jacob, John and George Wimer, Michael Waggoner, with three Ankenys and a Kniseley and two Berkeys on the same sheet.

That cohort is the point. The Michael of these Somerset and Bedford years is already tied to it by the registers — a son baptised in 1788 as “Michael Woldy”, a communicant of Easter 1790 entered as “Michael Waldy (Welty)”, and a 1796 township assessment carrying “Jacob & Michael Weldy”. Wildy is the same frame with a different vowel, written by a different clerk. Nothing is merged on the strength of it, and standing on a list beside a neighbour is not kinship — but this is a named man of the right frame in the right township in the right year, and there was no militia record for him at all before today.

One risk was found and retired within the hour. The reading rested on a single digitisation; a second scan of the same printed volume returns nothing at all for that spelling, which would ordinarily be reason to distrust it. But the printed general index to the series, compiled by hand from the pages rather than by machine from a scan, was located the same morning and carries the entry independently: “Wildy, Michael, 6th S., III, 41”, read at magnification, in a run that keeps Wilds, Wildy, Wildrick and Wilt carefully apart. The manuscript return in state custody is what would settle the surname; that is an errand, not a search.

Twelve township lists of 1789 amount to a near‑census of the county, and two more men of the name turn up unattached

The Milford list did not arrive alone. The same printed volume holds twelve complete Bedford County township militia lists taken in January and February 1789 — effectively a name‑by‑name canvass of the county fourteen months before the 1790 enumeration, and a body of evidence this project had assumed did not exist, because a standing note said the printed Bedford series stopped in 1784. It does not.

The sweep that found them ran across eight hundred and twenty‑seven volumes tied to the counties this family moves through, four hundred and seven million characters, on the full ladder of spellings. Two positive controls fired first. Across the whole of it there is one name on the ladder in the Bedford section, and it is the Milford man. Two others surfaced elsewhere and belong to nobody yet: a Henry Welty on a Franklin County company of October 1786, and a John Welty on a York company returned in April 1786.

It also corrected an old negative of this file’s own. A search made in July for a particular York cohort — Bierbower, Messerly, Ramsey, Feyser — had concluded they were absent, having looked in the wrong volume of the right series. They are there, on a Third Battalion return of 18 November 1788, and “Jacob Welte” stands in the third class among them.

An 1884 county history prints the 1796 tax list whole, and an 1894 road history hands back a Michael who is not this one

The full‑text search of an entire open book archive was run on the ladder for the first time, without county or date restriction. After filtering to pre‑1930 imprints, thirty‑one volumes survive and all but two were already in this file. The two are both worth having.

The first prints the Milford Township tax list of 1796 in full — “… Jacob Wimer, John Wagoner, James Wilson, Michael Wagoner, Thomas Wilson, Abraham Whipky, Jacob Weldy, Michael Weldy, George Wimer …”, closing with the collector’s name and the sum collected. That list had been held here only from an online transcription; it now has an independent printed witness from 1884. The same book carries a second, earlier list of the district’s settlers on which Jacob stands without Michael, and that asymmetry is not explained.

The second is a warning rather than a find. An 1894 history of the National Road lists the wagoners by their county of origin, and under “The following were of Washington county, Maryland” stands a Michael Welty. He is not the Michael of these pages, whose death in Ohio in 1815 predates the traffic described; he is filed as a decoy so that the next researcher who meets him does not have to work it out again. The Somerset County list on the same page carries no name on the ladder.

The Land Office’s oldest rights, a hundred and eighty leaves of them, printed sideways and never machine‑read

The printed abstract of the Land Office’s Old Rights for Philadelphia County R–Y, all of Bucks and all of Chester is set as landscape tables rotated ninety degrees, which is why no text search has ever touched it. All hundred and eighty leaves were pulled, turned, and read out. The result is zero on the ladder in all three counties — and because a zero of that shape deserves the working, the W‑runs of each county were transcribed in full instead of merely swept: Whitpain, Whartnaby, Watkins, Wood, Woodmanne, Willcox, Worley on the Chester side; Wate, Walton, Welch, Waddy, Watts on the Philadelphia; Wally, Waly, Waddy, Waite, Weight on the Bucks.

One near‑form was chased to the image at three times magnification and refused: “Reynolds & Rawson Wolly”, a joint entry of the twelfth month 1685–6, one letter from a spelling this family is genuinely recorded under, and a Chester man of 1685 who can be nobody’s ancestor here. The more useful measurement is the section’s own date profile: of two and a half thousand year‑tokens across it, ninety‑four in every hundred fall between 1680 and 1719, and fewer than sixty fall after 1730. The series closes before this family reaches Pennsylvania at all. What would reopen it is the Philadelphia A–Q run, which is not in this volume, and the true terminal date of the manuscript series, which has been taken from the print rather than from the archive’s own description.

The pages a machine cannot read are now mapped and read by hand — and the printed indexes reach where the scans do not

A per‑leaf quality screen was built over the whole printed archive series and it turns a suspicion into a number: two thousand eight hundred and thirty‑one leaves, in thirty‑three of eighty‑three volumes, have never been readable by any machine — sideways tables whose text layer is gibberish. Thirty‑one volumes are clean. Every flagged leaf was then pulled, rotated, re‑read and swept on the full ladder, and the sweep is silent: no new name anywhere in it. Four candidates were chased to the image and died there — two are Kelly, one is Welter, one is Weltig.

The screen also caught itself. Two known records of this family sit on pages whose published text contains not one occurrence of the name, and the same two printed pages score above the alarm threshold on one digitisation and below it on another, because one scan is coarser than the other. The threshold is a property of the photograph, not of the book — which means some of this file’s machine zeros are softer than they look, and re‑running them against a better scan is the reopener.

The cheap answer to all of it turned up the same morning, twice. The printed general indexes to the fifth and sixth series were located and read. They were compiled by hand from the printed pages, so a sideways table is no obstacle to them, and between them they print the complete footprint of the ladder across two whole series — forty‑seven entries, name and volume and page. Most are already in this file. Four have never been logged at all: a Witty, Philip on the page adjoining the depreciation‑pay entry that carries Michael; a Welty, Danl.; a third spelling and page for Anthony; and a Wildey, Philip. Those are pointers, not records. Nobody has opened any of the four.

A 1941 typescript recovered whole, and a contents page that says what it left out

A search handle inside a genealogical digital library was worked out and turned into a tool: it returns the full text of a page rather than a snippet, which meant a book that had previously been recovered thirty‑five pages at a time by hand came down complete — a hundred and seventy images of a hundred and seventy, in about four minutes.

The book is the 1941 typescript of the Falckner Swamp Reformed register, and it carries no name on the ladder anywhere in a quarter of a million characters, on thirty spellings, with no near‑miss. But its contents page is the finding. It reads “IV. Confirmations. (Not Copied) 1439 listed.” The transcriber counted the confirmands in the church book and declined to copy them — and a whole‑book census confirms it, the word appearing on three of a hundred and seventy pages, all of them front matter. So the fourteen hundred confirmations in the subscription index opened yesterday cannot have been keyed from this typescript, and for that one role the index is a second, independent witness to the church book after all. The correction made yesterday afternoon was right about the marriages and burials and wrong about the confirmations.

The same handle was turned on the Dover register the following half‑hour and stopped part‑way through by choice, at a hundred and sixty‑two of about a hundred and seventy‑four images. It is enough to answer a question standing since 9 July: this book carries no burials at all — baptismal language on a hundred and forty pages, confirmation matter on ten, communicant matter on two early leaves, and burial language on none. Its eight ladder occurrences are all entries already in this file, including the union‑church covenant of 30 May 1757 with George Weldy first on it.

14 August 2026, later — a second copy of the Dover assessment, and a land‑office minute that says “son & devisee”
The struck line under Welty Jacob is answered by a second copy of the same return, in another hand

The entry published here on 13 August reported the one line this project most wanted and could not have: the entry standing directly under Welty Jacob on the Dover assessment of March 1778, abraded, struck through with a deliberate cancelling stroke, and unreadable even at native raster. It was either Welty Philip or his absence from Dover, and the pull that proved the strike could not decide which.

It is decided, and not by better imaging. The same film carries a second manuscript copy of the whole Dover 1778 return, one hundred and sixty‑four images further on, at images 483–492, written by a different clerk. Its W‑run reads out in full at 5,800 by 4,100 pixels: “Weldy Widow, 50 acres”, “Weldy George, 100”, “Weldy Jacob” with no figures in any column, and “Weldy Phillip, 100”, with a figure in every column after it. The rate copy bound with the return prints “W[e]ldy Phlip 7 → 1.15.0”, which is the exact figure read off the cancelled line on the first copy. Michel and Jacob Weldy stand together in the single men’s block.

Two guards were run before the names were written down. The letterforms were graded in frame against Wild Paul, three lines away in the same hand — W‑i‑l‑d against W‑e‑l‑d‑y, the discriminator this file insists on in York. And the reading is a reading, not an attribution: the packet carries no date on its face, and 1778 rests on six concordances — the freemen order, the flat rate, the collector’s name, the wrapper. One anomaly is left standing rather than smoothed: a second Phlip at an identical rate sits immediately below on the rate copy, and nobody can yet say what it is. What the leaf buys is a continuous Dover holding of one hundred acres for Philip in 1778, 1779 and 1780, anchored at its earliest end by manuscript rather than by inference.

A 1761 caveat states a father and son in the state’s own words, and claims the ground under a Maryland right

Fourteen volumes of the third series of the printed Pennsylvania Archives, and six of the second, had never been opened here — not through any decision, but because the identifiers this project had been using did not resolve to them. A second and third naming scheme were found, and twenty complete volumes came open at once, about a hundred and thirteen million characters of text. Two of them carried records.

The first is a minute of the Land Office, and it is quoted whole because it is short and it says everything: “Land Office, March 2d, 1761. John Welty, Son & Devisee of Peter Welty, enters a Caveat against the Acceptance of a Survey or Patent being granted to Thomas Armor, Daniel Reisher or any other Person in Rights of Michael Beigler’s Warrant for 50 as. of the 17th of March, 1745, for a Tract of Land in Manheim Township, York County, claimed by the said Welty under a Maryland right. And I appoint Thursday the Sixteenth Day of April, to hear & determine the Dispute each Party having due Notice. Wm. Peters, Sec’ry.” Directly beneath, in the same hand: “Judgment given in favour of Beigler.”

He lost. The value is not the outcome but the language: this is a third independent state instrument stating the relationship in explicit kinship words, and it puts a Maryland claim to York County ground in the family’s own mouth in 1761 — which is the Manheim Township group of the Land Office rows, and is firewalled from the Dover line and from the Manchester branch. The printed page is cited from the volume’s own index and has not yet been pulled to the image; that is the next step before it is quoted with a page number. Behind it sit two documents nobody has opened: the Caveat Book entry the print abstracts, and whatever Maryland patent or warrant John was pleading — which would be the earliest Maryland instrument this file holds for anyone of the name.

The same twenty volumes put two Lancaster County men into a record class they had only been known outside of. Anthony Weldy stands twice in Captain Boyd’s company, and Daniel Weldy four times on three different spellings of his own name — Weldy, Welty, and a new one, Welday — always beside the same neighbour, Jacob Switzer. Neither man is attached to anything. A class roll carries no age, no origin and no kin.

The consistory book was opened, and the years it would have to cover are missing from it

The entry published here that morning ended on a new handle: “Philip Weldy (suspended)” on the 1823 communicant leaf, and the observation that a suspension is an act of the consistory, minuted with a cause and often a restoration — and that the congregation’s consistory book sat on the same film, located and never read. It was read the same afternoon.

The book has a thirty‑one year hole in exactly the wrong place. Its last dated minute is “The Bord of Elders, Council and Church wardens met at the Said Reformed Church on the 11th of January 1818 and Proceeded to Business”, signed by a president, a secretary and a treasurer; the next dated minute in the volume resumes in April 1849. The suspension of 1823 cannot be minuted here, because 1823 is not here.

Reading the rest of it whole was still worth the hour, and it answers the question the suspension raised. He is not restored at this charge. The communicant roll of 9 May 1824 runs about a hundred and seventy‑five names with no Weldy on it, and the roll of 1 April 1825 carries none either — and the same 1824 leaf shows the register does annotate discipline, printing two members as “(since excluded)”. The volume also gave up something nobody knew existed: the congregation’s charter of 16 October 1808, about a hundred and fifteen signatories read one at a time, with no name on the ladder among them. What would move any of this is the missing minute book for 1818–1849, which is a letter to a seminary archive rather than a search.

The 1777 York volume is not tax assessments at all, and its last leaf explains its own gaps

A lead had been carrying a promise for weeks: a bound run of 1777 York County township assessments, which would have given a Dover freemen block a year earlier than any yet read. The volume was opened at its title page and it is something else. The warrants inside run to a fixed formula — “York County ss. To [collector], Collector of [township] Township … Given under our Hands and Seals this 10th Day of May 1777” — charging a flat three pounds ten shillings a head. It is a bound volume of non‑associator fine rolls, not assessments, and a fine roll cannot deliver a freemen block, an acreage or a household.

All sixty‑two leaves were then opened rather than sampled, which took an hour and settled the shape of it. Twenty‑three townships are named. Four are not: Dover, Manchester, Codorus and Huntington. The temptation is to read that as four townships making no return — and the volume’s own closing leaf forbids it, in the clerk’s words: “This Dublicate of Fine is now Collecting, need no Transcribing.” This is a transcript book, and a transcript book skips what was already in a collector’s hands. Its gaps are evidence about the copying, not about the townships. Its own docket numbering runs to twenty‑four against twenty‑three named townships, so one numbered packet is unaccounted for, and that is the last place inside this volume a Dover roll could sit.

Mapping the whole five‑hundred‑image film off its own archival target cards then produced a caution worth more than the map. The cards are wrong in both directions. The card opening the 1778 volume lists six townships and omits Dover, which is in that volume twice; it omits Shrewsbury and Newberry, which are also there; and a leaf two hundred images past it is headed with a year three years later than the card claims. No card on this film can be used to rule anything out. The map did locate one thing worth the errand: a volume at images 141–216 whose card reads “Tax Records — Hellam, Newberry, Dover, Warrington, Monaghan — 1774–1775”, which is where the December 1774 Dover freemen list should be sitting.

A keyed index of fifteen thousand entries, silent on the ladder in every role — and wrong about its own source by tea‑time

A subscription index of the Falckner Swamp Reformed register, never used here before, was opened and worked facet by facet. It holds nearly two and a half thousand sponsor rows and one thousand three hundred and eighty‑seven confirmands, two hundred and fourteen of them inside the corridor window this project cares about. Ten wildcard sweeps across every spelling family returned not one name on the ladder in any role — not as child, parent, sponsor, bride, groom or confirmand. Five external controls were planted before the sweeps and all five fired, including a count control that matched a thin year an earlier reader had counted by eye.

The row that came out of it claimed something more than the zero: that the index was an independent keying of the church books, and that because its marriage and burial series show the same vacancies as the 1941 typescript, two independent copies agree the church book itself is blank in those years. Ninety minutes later the same session took that back. A catalogue lookup on the film number behind the index returns the identical title, compiler and volume span as the typescript — so the index is a keying of the copy, not of the original, and two witnesses collapse into one. The zero stands; the argument built on it does not.

A printed baptism table names most of its women only as “and wife”

The whole sponsor column of the printed New Hanover baptism table — both halves of it now, the whole table — was measured rather than searched, and the measurement is a fact about every sponsor‑anchored search this project has ever run. Of roughly two and a half thousand sponsor rows, about forty‑four in every hundred give no surname at all — some printing the couple simply as “X and wife”, more giving a bare given name and nothing else — and only about eleven in a hundred name a woman with a surname of her own. A search that keys on surnames is structurally blind to nearly half this column, and the blindness is in the book, not in the method.

That changes how one line in it reads. The compositor sets “Joh. Martin Miller and Anna Barbara” and then, on its own line, “Welte.” Because naming a woman in full is the minority form and “and wife” is the ordinary one, the likelier reading is that she is named in her own right rather than as the miller’s wife. It is a reading from typographic convention, not a statement in the record, and it is filed at that grade. Every line‑break in the whole table was then enumerated — two hundred and eighty‑seven of them, hyphenated and unhyphenated — and exactly one ever concealed a name on the ladder: this one. What would settle it is the original church books behind the print, where the women given here as “and wife” may be named.

14 August 2026 — a register that names its own churches, and a shipmate this file has held since July
A correction: the eighteenth‑century death page is not on this film, and the book it belongs to is offline

The entry published here on 3 August reported a typescript burial section headed “DEATHS or BURIALS — (Page 21 of oldest register)” and named its reopener: page 21 of the oldest register, and the leaves around it, which the transcript proves exist and which nobody has looked at. The volume has now been walked end to end, and that sentence was wrong in its second half.

The filmed 1744 book is three sections and no more: baptisms at images 3–136, one continuous run — German script and numbered entries at 1762, a four‑column page at 1802, a full‑page display heading reading “1844 Baptisms” at image 127; marriages at 137–159, opening “AD 1755” and closing in English in 1853 with “Look in the New Church Book for the later Names”; and deaths at 160–162, beginning “Died — The 11th of June 1844”. There is no death‑format page anywhere between. The band that had been named as the last place one could hide was opened at its first leaf, and its first leaf is already 1802.

So the transcriber of the 1930s had a book this film does not carry. The negative is not “the page is not there” but “the book is not here” — and the honest consequence is that the only surviving route to an eighteenth‑century burial page for this charge runs through the original registers in institutional custody, at the Evangelical & Reformed Historical Society at Lancaster Theological Seminary or with the congregation itself. That is a letter or a telephone call, not a search.

The burial register turns up in the clerk’s own hand, and its margin letters decode

The typescript line published here on 3 August — “Ag 1 1822 — Jacob WELDTY, adult. (Christ Church)” — rested on a 1930s typist’s expansion of something. The manuscript behind it has now been found, four leaves away from where the film was being read, and the entry is there in the clerk’s own hand: “Jacob Weldty  adult  do  do  Aug.t 1.  c”. The vowel is a clean e and carries no dot.

The letter in the right margin is the finding. The manuscript marks some burials with a single letter and leaves others blank; the typescript transcribes the same entries by the same dates and spells the church out. Set side by side, entry by entry, they decode: a = Quickel’s Church (five points), b = Wolf’s Church (four), c = Christ Church (four), and no letter at all = the town church at York — every unlettered manuscript entry prints without a parenthesis. Thirteen concordant points and no counter‑example.

Two limits, stated because the cipher will be used. A single “d” appears once, against one burial of May 1822, and the typescript prints that entry with no parenthesis at all — which contradicts the no‑letter rule rather than extending it. Do not read it as a fourth preaching point. And one manuscript margin mark appears to be dropped by the typescript entirely, which if confirmed means the typed copy is lossy and the manuscript is to be preferred wherever both survive.

What it buys is real: burials in this charge have carried a four‑way uncertainty about which of its churches they belong to since this project first read them. Every entry in this run is now place‑resolvable from the register itself, and the run reaches back at least to November 1821 in a volume that opens in 1811.

Three names read at the page, and a reading that had been building comes apart

An index search of this congregation had returned five rows on the ladder for 1822 and 1823 — Susanna, Ann M, Elizabeth and Philip twice — all typed as event Residence, bare year, no day. They were read as five communicants, which put four people of the name on one roll and made a household reading look obvious. All three women have now been opened at the page, each graded against a WILDT exemplar found on its own leaf, in the same frame, at the same magnification, before the name was written down.

Every one of the three survives the letterform test: the Wildts in these lists are dotted emphatically and terminate ‑dt, and all three of these are undotted and terminate ‑dy. Nothing was de‑attributed. But the record class was wrong for two of them, and the placement for the third. Ann M Weldy is a communicant — of Quickel’s Church, standing in a block headed in words and totalled at fifty, on a leaf whose four blocks tally to two hundred and twenty‑four communicants in the charge that year. Elizabeth Weldy is a confirmand of 1823, written “Elizabeth Weldy (married)”. Susanna is a confirmand of 1822. Neither confirmation column is annotated by church — it annotates by township, Bottstown, Conewago, Dover, Codorus — so neither woman is placed at all.

Philip Weldy is where he was: at Christ Church, communing in 1822 and again in 1823. And the man buried at Christ Church on 1 August 1822 is still buried there. Two people of the name at one named preaching point in one year stands untouched. What does not stand is the reading that was building around it: the only one of the three women the register actually places is at a different church, and the other two are not placed. The errand was set up expecting to add two more names to that household and it subtracted three.

One word on the 1823 leaf is a new kind of handle. Under the Christ Church subheading, the entry reads “Philip Weldy (suspended)”. Suspension from communion is not a clerk’s note; it is an act of the consistory, minuted with a cause and a date and often a restoration. The consistory book of this congregation for 1811–1851 sits on the same film, located, and has never been read for content. It is the first reason this project has had to open it.

A daughter for a York household, and a sponsor column that says PARENTES

A marriage recorded in this charge on 18 October 1827 — Henry Welty and Catharina Coleman — has been in the file without issue. The register carries their child: Elisabeth, born 10 May 1828, baptised 20 July 1828, parents Henry Welty & Catharina, in a four‑column German register ruled Kind geb. | Eltern | Zeugen.

The sponsor column is the point, and it is an answer rather than a silence. It reads “Parentes” — the parents stood for their own child. Naming in these families runs by godparent, which is the discriminator this project reaches for when it wants to tie one household to another; for this baby there is no godparent to reach for. It is worth recording that the register was asked and said so plainly.

One letterform flag, not a re‑grading: at high magnification the surname on that leaf carries two crossed ascenders between Wel‑ and ‑y, so it reads Weltty or Weldty rather than a plain Welty, while the index prints Welty twice. And a household this file did not have: a Philip Welti and Cath baptise a child born 14 November 1819 at the same charge. That leaf has not been opened.

The will page carries no signature at all, and the York Jacobs are two men

A 1782 probate page in the York wills had been read from a full‑text transcription, and the row built on it implied an autograph. Challenged, the page was opened. It is a register record copy and it says so in its own words, directly under the name block, in the Register’s hand: “A true Copy taken from & Compared with the Original — Arch.d McLean, Register.” The three witness names beneath the deposition are written in one English round hand, one pen, one ink, identical to the body text beside them. They are not three signatures.

Two things follow, and the second is the more useful. No comparison against the 1754 shipboard signature is available from this document, and none should be attempted from it again. And the spelling on that page is the Register’s, not the man’s — a record copy carries the clerk’s orthography, so a rung taken off it measures Archibald McLean rather than the family. Two other rows in this file already say the same thing about the same kind of document; this is the third.

The larger question behind it — whether the Jacobs in the York records are one man — was then worked through the tax years, and the answer is no. In 1782 a Jacob is assessed eighty acres in Dover and a Jacob one hundred acres in Manchester, in the same county book. The stronger leg assumes nothing about double assessment: the Dover Jacob is a landless single freeman in 1779 and again in 1780, while in January 1781 the Manchester Jacob already holds one hundred and thirty acres and is numbered on the Continental class list. No man is both.

And the Jacob who signed the ship list in 1754 is neither of them, on arithmetic done before any record is opened: oath‑takers were sixteen or over, so that signer was born by 1738 — which makes one of these two men four years old at the time and the other ninety‑nine at his death. Nothing is merged, and the York witness of 1782 is left unassigned; neither of the two men separated here is a taxpayer in the township where that testator’s family clusters, so a third man is the leading shape.

Two controls were run at the page image in the same pass and the second caught an error. A printed militia roll used here for an adjacency claim is not alphabetised — its privates run Weigel, Mitman, Ham, Gross, Rahauser, Romigh, Coder — so the initial‑letter trap that voids adjacency on tax lists does not reach it, and that adjacency is real. But the same volume’s text layer had placed a third name under the wrong heading, and this session believed it: at the page image he sits inside the roll, and the linearisation of a four‑column page was the artefact. The image said so and the session was wrong.

The western counties swept whole — and the congregation’s own neighbours turn up six years early

The last volume of the printed Pennsylvania tax series covers Bedford, Huntingdon, Westmoreland, Fayette, Allegheny and Washington. It was swept whole, at machine grade, with every candidate resolved at the page image. Across 1,033,880 characters: welt occurs zero times; weldy, welty, welte, welti, waldy, woldy zero each; weld three times, and all three sit inside Caldweld and Swartzwelder. Not one standalone token on the ladder exists in the volume. On the ground that later became Somerset County — Brothersvalley, Turkeyfoot, Quemahoning, Milford, W‑blocks enumerated township by township and two lookalikes killed at the leaf — the same zero.

The corpus boundary matters more than the zero. Somerset County was not erected until 1795, so returns for that ground in the 1780s are Bedford returns, and the printed Bedford series stops dead at 1784. A standing next action in this file — run the Somerset lists for 1786–1791 — was never a free desk errand; it is a manuscript errand, and it is now written down as one.

And the honest grade of that test, stated before anyone orders a film: it cannot do what it was asked to do. The idea it was meant to test is a man present without his household. A tax assessment names householders and single freemen; a man boarding with kin, landless, or a non‑taxable dependent is invisible to it by construction. A clean return would not refute the idea. Only a positive could confirm it.

What the sweep did produce came from asking for the whole crew instead of the name. The Easter 1790 communicant list at Samuel’s Church gives twelve men who took communion beside the Michael of Dover Township. Five of them are on the Milford Township returns of 1783 and 1784: Peter Ankeny at five hundred acres — the church elder who heads that very communicant list — Christian Ankeny at three hundred and ninety‑six, and John, Frederick, Jacob and George Weymer, printed there as Wimer and Winier. Settled landholders, six and seven years before that Easter. Michael is the only one of the group absent from the ground. Which turns the next move around: a Weymer or an Ankeny deed, estate or church record naming him would be a positive route, and no tax sweep can ever be one.

One bibliographic trap out of the same pass, because it would have been published as a find. The running head on one printed page reads “COUNTY OF BEDFORD — 1788” in the text layer. It is the only 1788 Bedford string in the volume and it sits squarely inside the window this project cares about. At the image it says 1783 — the final 3 rendering as an 8 — with the running heads either side of it agreeing. The rule this file already keeps for surnames extends to headings: a misread heading invents or destroys a whole year.

The one county the pedigree actually names had never been opened

The Land Office warrant registers are a county‑by‑county series and this project has worked several of them — York, Lancaster, Northampton, with Dauphin named. Chester had never been opened, and Chester is the county the family pedigree names for the birth this project is trying to date. It was not a decision anyone took; it was a gap.

It is now read. The whole W section — five interface pages, 154 numbered warrants running from 1733 to a warrant of 20 April 1903 — eyes‑on at 200 dpi, the crop bands deliberately overlapped so that no name is lost at a seam, because a name lost at a seam is exactly how this project has manufactured a false negative before. Zero on every rung of the ladder, and no bare stem of any kind. The section boundary was measured rather than taken from the interface label: the page before it is the V run, the page after opens the Y run, and one blank ruled leaf sits between.

Three near‑misses were magnified and ruled out rather than eyeballed — “Wertz, Jacob”, whose third letter is a short hooked r and not a tall looped l, which makes it the single line in the section most likely to be misfiled later; “Wholey, Timothy J.”, a round o; and “Welch, Sarah”, a clear ch ligature. Two English‑neighbourhood readings are firewalled so nobody re‑chases them: Wield, John, an ield order rather than eld, and two separate Robert Witt‑ entries on different leaves and different dates.

The county’s tax lists were already a whole‑corpus zero. Land now says what tax said. The reopener is structural rather than hopeful: a man who acquired by purchase or by patent is invisible to a warrant register, and two free series that would catch him — the patent index and the Chester old rights index — are the next places to look.

The whole 1752 crew read at last, and the master named on a German index card is aboard

Instead of searching a passenger list for the family, the list was transcribed whole: both leaves of the qualification list of 16 October 1752, boundaries fixed at each end, seventy‑four names against a heading that says “No. 75” — a discrepancy recorded rather than smoothed, since no second exemplar of this ship survives to check it against.

The find is in the signing order on the second leaf: Jacob Schuster — Philipps Schenckel — Geoerg Welde — Henrich Schenckel. A German emigration card index for this same household, in this file since 2 July, says that one of the Welde children “dient bei Jakob Schuster in Nantmeal Tp. Chester Co. Pa.” — serves with Jakob Schuster in Nantmeal Township, Chester County. The card’s named master and a passenger two lines from the Weldes carry the same name, and both facts had been sitting in this file for six weeks without being put beside each other.

The join was then given its first test. The manuscript county tax and exoneration series returns exactly six Schuster‑ladder taxables in Chester County, and three of them are one man in one township: Jacob Shuster and Jacob Shooster, all 1768, all East Nantmeal — and Jacob Shuster, East Nantmeal, again in 1789. 1768 is the first year the series covers, so he is on the earliest surviving list for that township and still there twenty‑one years later. The card’s date is 1773; the tax record brackets it from both sides, and the township matches the card word for word. A second adult of the surname, Peter Shister, is on the same township’s 1781 list.

The limit is exact and it is not small. The falsifier this join named for itself — any Jacob Schuster documented in Chester County before October 1752 — cannot be tested in that series, because the series begins in 1768. The pre‑1760 leg was run in the church and town records instead and returned zero Chester Schusters of any given name, but northern Chester’s German registers are thin by construction, so that zero is coverage‑limited and proves little.
