What would settle it is a deed reciting the purchase, a naturalisation, or an East Nantmeal assessment for any year before 1768. Nothing is merged and no identity is assigned; what has changed is that the one detail on that card nobody could corroborate now has an independent American document behind it.

Two things from the same leaf that had never been noticed: three Schenckels aboard, two of them bracketing Geoerg Welde, and a small French‑surname contingent — Jacquart, Gachon, Salladin — in a shipload otherwise German throughout. An adversarial re‑read the same day struck a fourth name off that French list as a transparently German toponymic. Three, not four.

An A where the searches all had an E, and a household nobody’s query could match

A reconciliation this project relied on had shown a church‑records collection to be complete on the ladder. It ran on two rungs, and both of them carry an E. The A‑vowel rung had never been read. It has now been read whole, one hundred and forty‑three records, partitioned by date and reconciling exactly — and it held a household.

At Moorestown, Moore Township, Northampton County: Philipp Waldy and Emma Margaretha, with Johann Nikolaus Waldy baptised 23 June 1782 and Emma Caterina Waldy baptised 11 October 1784; then Johannes Waldy confirmed in 1796 and Jacob Waldy in 1798, same congregation. The town lists make it a community rather than a couple: Philip, Georg and Johannes Waldy as three adult males in the residence list of 1791, and in 1792 a Joh Philip, a Joh Georg and a Philip, which reads as a father and a son sharing a name.

Moorestown is in Moore Township, and a Philip of that township has existed in this file only in land paper — warrants of 1774 and 1787, surveys of 1775 and 1786, a patent as late as 1823, and three tax years in the 1780s. He now has a wife, four children and two more adult men of the surname beside him. He is not merged with any other man of the name, and the firewalls between them stand. The same village turns up a household on a third rung baptising in the 1820s.

The lesson costs nothing to state and it is the reason the record existed to be found: the source did not change. The reach did.

A wall that returns success and no results

Four German genealogy domains that this project would ordinarily query from a script were measured directly. All four return HTTP 200 — success — with a 4.4 kilobyte proof‑of‑work page reading “making sure you are not a bot”, and zero result rows, for any query. A script sees a successful request and an empty answer. It is a perfect forgery of a negative, and it is worth publishing for any researcher scripting against those hosts. A real browser passes it invisibly, with no puzzle and no human step.

The first thing checked was whether anything in this file rests on those hosts. Nothing does — the whole workbook returns no mention of any of the four. The next thing was a positive control before the sweep rather than after it: the surname Welde unqualified returns 223 entries, so the search works; Welde with Edenkoben returns a true zero, now measured instead of assumed.

The bycatch is the find. Maria Elisabetha Welde, born 1743 at Altdorf in the Pfalz, died 1817 at Böbingen, and Salome Welde, born 1750 at Altdorf — both inside the ring of parishes around Edenkoben this project has been working, neither with parents recorded, and both at submitted‑pedigree grade, which is the lowest grade this file recognises. They are a lead, not a record. But the ring was swept once already and returned nothing, and the reason is now visible: that sweep read Böbingen’s marriages, and these are births. The scope was right and the section was wrong. Altdorf itself keeps nothing before 1759, as this project reported in July — so whoever compiled these two read them somewhere else, most probably in the Böbingen book, which is one leaf each to check.

A note saying a thing was never done ages exactly like a note saying it was finished

This file is careful about closures. A line reading finished or exhausted stops future work, and every closure this project has had to walk back was correct inside its own stated scope and wrong in the summary line somebody later skimmed. So negatives here are written as measurements, with their boundaries attached and a named condition that would reopen them.

Four times in one day the failure came from the other end, and it is the harder one to see. A note saying a volume was never located, a band never opened, a body never swept, looks like diligence. Nobody re‑checks diligence. One session acted on such a note without opening the volume and had to withdraw a correction it had just made to a perfectly good sweep; there was no missing volume. Another opened the band a note had named as the last place a burial page could hide, and found the band was already 1802. A third took an audit’s report of unfiled records at face value — the most trustworthy shape of all, because a measurement produced it — and the audit had matched two sets of filenames that share no key and could not have matched anything. And a fourth built a whole question on a record class an index had flattened, when the page said two of those records were something else entirely.

The common part is not carelessness. In each case a real, correctly‑made note was read years — here, days — after the thing it described had moved. An index is a finding aid and its spelling is an interpretation; a heading is a claim about a corpus; a source’s statement about its own provenance is evidence of what that source knew, not of where the record came from. The cheapest safeguard turned out to be the same one every time: open the object and read its year before spending a day inside it.

13 August 2026, evening — the flat sums on the Dover lists turn out to be citations
The second list in the 1778 packet names itself, and the word is FINES

The entry published earlier today reported a list bound into the Dover packet on a completely different plan from the assessment beside it: a flat £3‑10‑0 charged on every man, alphabetised by given name, most entries struck through, with a Philip on the ladder three lines into its P run. It was described and deliberately not characterised. It has now been read twice more, and it identifies itself twice over.

First, the sum. Three pounds ten shillings is a statutory quantity and it occurs exactly once in the whole volume of Pennsylvania session laws for these years. The act of 14 February 1777 directs that the commissioners “charge every such person not associating the sum of three pounds ten shillings on the lists made out on their order”, and has those fines collected “in the same manner as directed in the county levy act … for collecting the county taxes” — which is why a fine roll is made township by township, worked by the ordinary tax collector, and ends up bound in a tax packet.

Second, and this is the one the document supplies without any statute at all: the roll’s own arithmetic proves the charge is per head and reaches sons living in their father’s house. On one leaf a man is charged 3‑10‑0, and directly beneath his name, as a sub‑line of his own entry, “& three sons” at 10‑10‑0 — exactly three times the flat rate. It is the only figure on the list that breaks the uniformity, and it was legible in the very first image pulled. A tax assessment cannot behave that way: on this same packet, single men living at home are charged as single freemen at thirty shillings.

Then the warrant heading the list was opened, and it settles the class in one word. The two Dover warrants are the same formula in the same hand six weeks apart, and they differ in exactly one slot: the March warrant appoints a collector “of the within TAX”; the warrant of 10 May 1778 appoints him “of the within FINES”. Same sentence, same position — a five‑letter word with a tall looped ascender where the March leaf has a three‑letter word ending in x. The list is a list of fines. It still does not say which fines, so the identification with the act of February 1777 is upgraded rather than proved, and the leaf is heavily foxed exactly there and deserves a second reader.

The roll was tallied exactly, five columns across two leaves: 107 names. And the trap has to be stated before anyone draws the obvious inference, because a document that says fines at the page invites precisely the reading it forbids. The fine reaches able‑bodied men between sixteen and fifty who did not associate, with ministers, schoolmasters and purchased servants excepted, and entries could be discharged before this copy was made. Absence from this roll is not evidence that a man associated. The Michael of Dover Township is absent from the whole list — and that is not a militia fact, not an allegiance fact, and is not logged as one.

A correction: the 1778 act named here this morning is not the act that governs the 1778 list

The entry published earlier today ended on a qualification: the Pennsylvania supply act for 1778 had not been read, and if its freeman clause were conjunctive — twenty‑one and six months clear of service — the Dover return would yield a hard floor of born on or before March 1757. The clause was read. It is conjunctive, word for word as expected. And it does not govern this list.

Two independent reasons, either sufficient. The act was passed 27 March 1778, and the warrant this return sits under is dated 26 March 1778 — the day before. And the decisive one, because it is on the page rather than in a book: that act charges a single freeman £3, and every freeman on the Dover list is charged thirty shillings. Thirty shillings is the rate set by the supplement of 13 October 1777, and that clause is disjunctive: “every single freeman, whether residing with his parents or elsewhere, who at the time of the assessment shall be of the age of twenty‑one years or hath been out of his apprenticeship or servitude for the space of six months … shall pay the sum of thirty shillings.”

So there is no floor. A man could be rated at under twenty‑one provided he was six months out of service, and the birth window stands unchanged at roughly December 1753 to 1755 — now for a read reason rather than an assumed one. One inference published this morning is withdrawn with it: under a disjunctive clause, being rated in March 1778 does not show a man was clear of an indenture. That is a withdrawal, not a claim that he was a servant; nothing here measures what the assessors actually did.

What the day gained is worth more than the floor would have been. A flat sum on a colonial list is a citation, not a number. Both flat rates in this one packet name the law that produced them, and neither had been looked up in five weeks of working the film. An undated list can often be dated and classified by its own rate — and a rate matching no act is itself a warning that a document has been mis‑classed.

The warrant at full resolution, and a second way to date a list from its own arithmetic

The 26 March warrant and the packet wrapper were pulled at native resolution and read whole. The date is now read at the page rather than inferred — “the 26 Day of March Annoque Domini 1778”, in the commissioners’ own hand — which matters because the whole argument above turns on this warrant preceding the 1778 act by one day.

The measured negative comes first, because it is what was asked. The warrant recites no act. There is no “by virtue of an act of Assembly passed the ___ day of ___” clause anywhere in it, and the packet’s exterior docket is equally silent. That errand is closed as answered rather than as unrun — and it is normal rather than exceptional: these warrants are administrative forms, and the act lives in the commissioners’ instructions, not on the collector’s copy.

Which makes the arithmetic the primary tool rather than the fallback. The assessment columns are headed Rate and Tax’d, valuation and charge side by side, and the ratio is exactly five shillings in the pound, without exception, across sixteen consecutive entries: rate 4:10 charged 1‑2‑6, rate 2 charged 0‑10‑0, rate 9:10 charged 2‑7‑6, and so on down the column. Five shillings in the pound is a quarter of assessed valuation, which is a war‑supply rate and cannot be a county levy, since county levies are laid in pence in the pound. An undated, headless leaf whose freemen block has been lost can still be dated from any single householder’s two figures.

One thing on the page, recorded because it is on the page and not because anything is read into it: both Dover warrants and the wrapper were first made out to one man and readdressed to another, the strike visible in all three places — so the collector’s name is a late hand throughout this packet, and the man first appointed stands on the fine roll himself, struck.

The likeliest register in the birth window, read leaf by leaf, including the column nobody had read

If the Michael of Dover Township was born between about December 1753 and 1755, the American register most likely to hold him is the Lutheran book at New Hanover in the Falckner Swamp, where a household on the ladder was baptising in exactly those years. That book had been page‑read here only for 1756–1759; the window itself rested on an index and a printed table. It has now been read at the manuscript, every entry of 1753, 1754 and 1755 — twenty‑four pages, about a hundred and ninety baptisms.

The two ladder entries are confirmed word for word, dates included, and one of them is a small demonstration in itself. “Johannes Welde | fr: Christina || Anna Catharina | gnb: d. Novemb: 1752 | gtft: d. 20. May 1753”, and eight months later in the same book, “Johannes Welty | fr: Christina || Helena Christina | gnb: d: 2. Dec. 1753 | gtft: d: 13. Jan. 1754”. One household, two spellings, and the shift is the scribe’s own — not a modern editor’s, which is what a printed transcription always leaves open.

Then the zero. Across those hundred and ninety baptisms there is no further entry on the ladder of any spelling, as parent or as sponsor, and no Michael on any rung. The household presents these two daughters and does not appear again to the end of 1755 — no third child, no posthumous 1755 child, within thirteen months of the death of the Hannover Township blacksmith whose estate file was published here this morning.

The reason this was worth doing at the manuscript is the sponsor column, and it is a coverage point rather than a method flourish: the index carries no sponsors at all and the printed edition is a table, so anyone on the ladder standing only as a godparent was invisible to every previous sweep of this congregation. The column was read. Zero in three years. The control that licenses that zero was planted before the sweep and read at the manuscript for the first time on the way through: a woman of the name stands as sponsor to another family’s child in February 1757, in this hand, in this column — so the ladder is legible here and the method does find a rung sitting in the sponsor slot.

The near‑misses are enumerated rather than waved off, because two of them are precisely the trap this project keeps setting for itself: a Michael Walther — an emergency baptism, the parents standing for their own child “in noth‑fall” — and a Michael Weigel, alongside a Philip Walther, a Christophel Weigel and a George Wissner. Each was settled on a control letterform taken from the same page. None is on the ladder. So a child of this family born in that window was baptised somewhere else, or not at all, or the family was not there — and this reading does not choose between the three. It is a closing find rather than a discovery, and the escape it closes is the one that says the printed edition simply dropped him.

13 August 2026, later — the Dover assessment of 1778, and a year off the blank
A tax return nobody had opened puts Michael in Dover a year earlier than any record this project holds

The York County film this project has been working since 10 July carries a contents description in the catalogue, and nobody had read it. Of one bundle it says: “1778: all townships except Manchester, Franklin.” A note in this log about Manchester being missing had hardened, over five weeks, into an assumption that Dover was missing too. It never was. The 1778 Dover return sat three images past leaves that were opened on 10 July.

It is a whole packet. “York County ss. To JACOB MAY, Collector of DOVER Township … the Day of Appeal is the 21 Day of April next at the House of JOHN ERMAN in York Town … Given under our Hands and Seals the 26 Day of March Annoque Domini 1778” — signed John Hay and William Mitchell, Commissioners; then the alphabetical run of taxables; then, at the foot, the single‑freemen block, every man charged a flat thirty shillings. Two of its lines read WELDY MICHAEL and, directly beneath, WELDY JACOB.

The cohort standing round them is the control, and it is the strongest in this file. Abraham Messerly, Joseph Pew, Paul Deerdorf, Michael Spahr and George Shaffer all stand in this 1778 block and again in the Dover single‑men list of 1779 — which is where, until today, Michael’s documented life began. The 1778 block is the 1779 block’s direct predecessor, man for man.

The householder run of the same return, twenty lines above, carries Welte Widow, Welte George and Welty Jacob: five entries on the ladder in one document. And the same hand writes Welte, Welty and Weldy inside twenty lines — as clean a demonstration as this log holds that the variation belongs to the clerk rather than to separate families. No identity is assigned anywhere in this: standing next to someone is not kinship, and the rule here forbids reading it as one.

One line is named rather than papered over. Immediately below Welty Jacob stands an entry rated at the same figure as Welte George, on a rubbed band of the leaf, beginning with W and unresolvable at the magnification the viewer will render. Welty Philip is nowhere else in that W‑run, and he is on the Dover returns of 1774, 1779 and 1780. So the line is either Philip, or his absence from Dover in March 1778 — and the second reading would be a real event needing an explanation. Neither is asserted. One native‑resolution capture of six lines settles it, and it is the highest‑value capture outstanding here.

What the tax statutes actually say, and what a man’s absence from a list is worth

Three assessment regimes were read clause by clause in the statute books instead of inherited from other people’s summaries. The colonial rule, which governed the Dover assessment of December 1774: “no single man who at the time of assessment is under twenty‑one years of age or hath not been out of his servitude or apprenticeship six months shall be rated by this act.” It is framed as an exclusion. It says who shall not be rated and nowhere says a man must be rated the moment he turns twenty‑one — so a first appearance yields a ceiling on a birth year, never a birth year.

Then the December 1774 Dover freemen block was read again, and it does not end where this project had it ending. It continues at the top of the next leaf, five more lines in the same hand at the same flat rate, and closes with JACOB WELTY — a Welty record five years earlier than any other Dover single man, and an age datum in itself: he was twenty‑one, or six months clear of an indenture, by December 1774. Michael is not in it. Under the act that governed that list he was therefore under twenty‑one, or still in service, or not resident in Dover. On the first branch he is born after December 1753, which against the ceiling this log already holds closes his birth window to roughly December 1753–1755 — the tightest bracket this project has ever had on him. The other two branches are not excluded, and this is not offered as a conclusion.

One escape did close, on the same leaf. The obvious objection is that a son living in his father’s house was simply not rated. But “Michael Sparr Junr” stands in the freemen block at the flat charge while Sparr Michael stands among the householders of the same return. Dover in 1774 did rate a single son against his father.

The war is written into the statute too, and it cuts a particular way. Every supply act from 1777 to 1779 exempts “such as shall be actually engaged as enlisted soldiers or mariners in the service of the United States of America during the time of levying the same.” Michael is rated as a single freeman in 1778, again in 1779, again in 1780, and is classed in the militia in 1781 — and militia classing presupposes a taxable inhabitant rather than a Continental soldier. His presence on those lists is positive evidence that he was not an enlisted soldier in those years, reached without a single name search. The stretch in which an enlistment could still hide him narrows to about 1776–1777.

The qualification, because it is load‑bearing: the Pennsylvania supply act for 1778 has not been read. If its freeman clause is conjunctive — twenty‑one and six months out of service, as the October 1779 act is — then the 1778 return yields a hard floor of born on or before March 1757, and the bracket above tightens again. It is one clause, in one volume, already open in this project.

A correction to this morning’s entry: the naming custom is intermittent, not absent

The entry published here earlier today reported that no child of the Gültstein household is named for a godparent, and drew from it that a naming custom proved in one branch is not a property of the surname. A fourth and a fifth baptism have since been read at native resolution, and the fifth overturns the first half of that. Johann Martin, baptised 30 January 1748, son of Hans Jerg Welthe — written Baur, a farmer, an occupation this log has never had for him — and his godfather is Hans Martin Nueber. The child is named for him.

So the custom is not missing at Gültstein. It is intermittent — one baptism in four in this household, against five for five in the Edenkoben line. What survives is the practical form of the rule, and it is worth stating plainly: a godparent match may be treated as a weak positive, and a mismatch never as an exclusion. The fourth child, Catharina Elisabetha, baptised 2 May 1746, is new to this log as well, and the same sponsor pair stands at her baptism too — the sixth attestation of one couple, across both of the father’s marriages, from 1741 to 1750. That is the hardest internal lock this household has, and it does not depend on how any clerk spelled the surname.

The Württemberg register is now read end to end, and it supplies no candidate of the right age

Four sweeps by four sittings now join up, and the Gültstein baptisms are continuous from 1736 to 1754 with a single unread window at 1733–1735. In all of it, Michael Welthe the younger and his wife Catharina have exactly three children on record: Jacob baptised 23 March 1736, Anna Maria baptised 8 October 1741, and a stillborn daughter buried 22 May 1748. There is no son Michael.

The stillbirth is what makes that silence honest rather than suspicious. A stillborn child is not baptised, so she can appear in the burials and cannot appear in the baptisms — which means the couple were demonstrably still at Gültstein and still bearing children in 1748, in the very window being read, and the baptisms still show them no son. The absence is not an absence of the family. It is the absence of a son.

The one unread window cannot rescue it either, and the reason is arithmetic rather than evidence. A boy born in 1733–35 is 44 to 46 years old at the 1779 Dover appearance and 49 to 51 at the 1784 marriage — not a single freeman rated on forty‑four acres. His father is worse: married in 1732, so born about 1705–12, and 71 to 78 years old in 1783. Both German readings of the Michael of Dover Township are now in serious trouble, and that should be said outright rather than left implied. The Württemberg thread has produced a great deal this fortnight — a village, a household, a signature on a ship list, a Pennsylvania address for one of its men. It has not produced him.

Two corridor townships read leaf by leaf, and the family is in neither

Douglass and New Hanover in Montgomery County — the Falckner Swamp ground where a Welte blacksmith died in 1755 and a Wilte household in 1767 — had never been opened in the county’s own tax returns. They have now been read whole: Douglass, all 58 leaves across five books; New Hanover, all 66 leaves across seven books, 1785 to 1789; roughly 4,750 taxable entries, every W‑surname magnified individually and the complete W population of both townships written into the log. Not one entry on the ladder, in any year those books cover.

What makes it a measurement rather than a gap in the reading is that the neighbours are all still there. Yeager — Conrad the blacksmith in New Hanover, Conrad the farmer and cordwainer in Douglass — and Dachenbach, spelled six ways across the books, stand in every single one; so does Potts. Eighteen to twenty‑two years after the estate that named two of those surnames as appraisers, the appraisers’ families are on the rolls and the decedent’s is not. That agrees, from an entirely independent record class, with what the Falckner Swamp church register says by itself: entries on the ladder run there from 1752 and stop dead in 1767. Two archives, one answer. The bound is stated as well — nothing in this path runs later than 1789, and the federal tax of 1798 is still owed.

Ninety‑seven years of Pennsylvania patents, and a Welty landholder in the direct‑line county nobody had

The Land Office’s index to every patent enrolled in Pennsylvania between 1684 and 1781 was read through its whole W section, twenty‑four leaves, all of them. It holds exactly three entries on the ladder in ninety‑seven years.

The one that matters is the second: 7 July 1761, patentee ABRAHAM WELTY, 195 acres 40 perches and 59 acres more — about 254 acres — in YORK COUNTY. York is where the Dover question lives, and this log did not have him. The structure of the entry is the other half of it: only the middle of the three warrants behind that patent was taken out in his own name. The other two were Adam Simon’s, dated 1746 and 1751, and Welty is holding them at patent — which means he acquired them, by purchase, by marriage or as heir. A named non‑Welty with a documented property relationship to a Welty in York County before 1761 is a handle the Dover work has never had.

The first is Christian Welty, 267 acres in Lancaster, patented 1746 on a 1743 warrant taken out by CATHARINE WELTY — a woman warrantee, uncommon enough to be worth chasing on its own. The third closes a conflict opened last week: the Manheim tract patented 6 July 1764 ran to JOHN WELTY on PETER WELTY’s warrant, patentee and warrantee in adjacent columns of one line in one clerk’s hand. Peter died in 1755; nine years later the Land Office issued the patent on his warrant to John. The warrant register’s own patentee cell, which says Peter, is a fair‑copy clerk carrying the wrong name down a column — a demonstrated error now, not a suspected one.

The negative that comes with it needs stating at its real weight. No Michael, Philip, Jacob, Henry, George or Peter appears as a Welty patentee anywhere in Pennsylvania before 1781. That does not say those men held no land. A warrant and a survey gave possession, and patents were routinely deferred for decades — the Northampton tract published here this morning waited thirty‑five years, and was then patented to a different man altogether. The negative bounds a record class, not a family.

A blacksmith’s estate read as a bundle, and the ceiling above it

The 1755 administration on a JOHN WELTE of Hannover Township had only ever been read at a single leaf. The file was pulled instead, and it is five leaves, closed and complete: wrapper, administrator’s account, account docket, the administration bond itself — never seen before by this project — and the bond docket. The bond gives him a trade the log has never had: “JOHN WELTE BLACK‑SMITH deceased”, administration granted to Casper Singer, bound in one hundred pounds to the Register General on 13 February 1755. There is no inventory in the file. And the sureties are not Weltys — a fourth instance in one day of the assumption that the people standing round a German household are its kin.

Both that estate and the 1767 one now have their recorded book and page: Administration Book G folio 11 and Administration Book H page 41. Neither can be read. The bound administration books are not filmed at all — the catalogue was enumerated, all 350 film notes and 97 titles, and the indexes are filmed while the books they index are not; the printed abstract series runs A, C, D, E, F and stops one volume short of G. This project can learn that a bond was recorded, and where, and can never read it by that route.

The same ceiling stands over the Philadelphia Orphans’ Court, with a sharper edge. It has been carried as the next step in this log since 2 August, by four separate sittings. It is image‑restricted — every thumbnail padlocked, viewable only at a FamilySearch Center or an affiliate library — and it is not in the full‑text index either, which was measured rather than assumed. It was never a search anybody skipped. It is a record class that cannot be reached from a desk, and it is now written down as an errand, with the four films named in the order they should be used.

One smaller correction, recorded because it is a search key rather than a curiosity. The appraisers’ oath in the 1767 file had been read off a sideways scan and its names Anglicised. Turned upright and magnified, the clerk wrote “MARTHIN DACHAUBACH & GEORGE JAGER”, sworn before a Justice signing POTTS. A neighbourhood control aimed at Daughenback and Yeager would have been aimed at forms the manuscript does not use. The spelling rule this project applies to its own surname applies to the neighbours too — and it paid the same day: those are the surnames standing in the Douglass returns above.

Four spellings every sweep in this project has been blind to

A controlled test over one whole printed tax volume — one file, one thirty‑four‑rung ladder, three pattern shapes — turned up something nobody was looking for. The ladder found fifteen entries in that volume. The bare stems WELT, WELD, WILT and WILD, which the ladder does not carry at all, found nineteen more.

It has already cost something specific. “Weld, Jacob, weaver” stands in the 1773 Lancaster borough return between a potter and a shoemaker, one leaf before a Welty freeman entry this project transcribed, inside a neighbour run it had read and written out. He was not missed by carelessness: no pattern in use could match him. Nothing is claimed about who he was — only that he was invisible.

The stems are still not promoted, and York is the reason. On the 1783 Dover roll the assessor prints Wild John and Wild Paul immediately above Weldy Phillip and Weldy Jacob, and Paul’s own spelling runs Wilte, Wilt, Wilt, Wilt, Wild across five printed years. The clerk who wrote Weldy wrote Wild on the same page of the same list, so in York these are two families and the discriminator is the record’s own. Outside York it is untested. Two further whole volumes — Cumberland across six years, and six western counties — were swept on the full ladder and return zero, and they print WILTZ, WILLTS, WILTS and WILDT, four more spellings the ladder has never carried. Three of the four belong to a Michael Wilt of Greenwood Township, Cumberland County, who excludes himself by arithmetic: he is rated there in 1779, 1780, 1781 and 1782, the same four years the Michael of Dover Township is rated in York. One man cannot be assessed in two counties in one year.

The whole 1754 shipload was tested against Pennsylvania, and it is essentially not there

A different way of testing the corridor: instead of searching for the family, search for its fellow passengers. Twenty‑five surnames distinctive enough to be unambiguous were lifted from the 1754 signed passenger leaf — Schnurenberger, Dockenwadel, Blumenschein, Feinnauer, Knobelich, Steidtle and the rest — and run, with Anglicised stems, across the whole printed Pennsylvania tax series: eleven counties, 1765 to 1788.

Sixteen of the twenty‑five return zero in every county. The remainder resolve to German surnames common enough to be there regardless, or — in one county — to a shipmate’s surname being used as another man’s given name. The shipload is essentially absent from the printed Pennsylvania tax record, and that is a measurement against the corridor model rather than for it: the Falckner Swamp grouping this project has been working is a cluster of the surname, not a cluster of that ship. And the asymmetry is worth ending on. In York County the ladder returns thirty‑seven tokens and the shipmates return none. The Michael of Dover Township sits in a county that, on this corpus, not one of his supposed shipmates reached.

A second list in the same 1778 packet, and the rubbed line turns out to have been cancelled on purpose

Two things happened after the entry above was written, both from one decision: the record images were pulled at full resolution instead of being read inside a viewer. The leaf carrying the 1778 Dover assessment downloads at roughly 5,700 by 4,000 pixels — and that is worth stating, because a leaf tested this way last week returned a file byte‑identical to what the viewer already gave. It is a property of the individual film, not of the archive.

The line this project most wanted to read — the entry standing directly under Welty Jacob, which is either Welty Philip or his absence from Dover in March 1778 — is still not readable at full resolution. The ink is abraded. But the pull settled something else about it: the line was struck through, by the same deliberate cancelling stroke that cancels Walter Widow eleven lines above it. It is a deleted entry, not a faded one. The raster is exhausted; reading it now needs raking light or multispectral imaging, on site, which makes it an errand rather than a download.

The same pull opened a document nobody had looked at. Beneath the second Dover warrant of 10 May 1778 runs a list on a completely different plan from the assessment: a flat £3‑10‑0 charged on every man, arranged alphabetically by GIVEN name, most entries struck through. Three lines into its P run stands PHILIP WELDY — or Welty; the d and the t are not separable at this grade, and both are spellings this family is recorded under. So a Philip on the ladder is in the Dover packet six weeks after the assessment — which does not identify the cancelled line, but does make the reading in which Philip had left Dover the weaker of the two.

Then the list was read to its last line, which is where it earned its place here. It is five columns across two leaves, opening at Andrew Huns and closing at William Herold with the leaf blank below. Every surname beginning with W was magnified one at a time: Walk, Wolf, Wilt, Wheigle, Weaver, Worley, Herold — none of them on the ladder. And one that is none of them: CHRISTOPHER W——, struck through, in the C run on the other leaf entirely. Its capital W is unambiguous and its terminal is a descender that drops below the line and loops back left, which rules out this clerk’s Walk (terminal k) and his Wolf (terminal f) — and rules out nothing else. It does not establish a y: the same hand writes Swartz twice, two columns away, and at high magnification his ‑tz and his ‑y are the same shape. Four readings are live — Welty, Walty, Woltz, Wolby — and the entry is filed at exactly that grade, which is to say it carries no weight yet and is counted in no tally. It is recorded here for how it was found rather than for what it says.

The reason it was found is worth one sentence, because it is a rule rather than luck. The first reading of that list covered the M block, looking for a Michael, and reported honestly that its negative covered one column only. In a list alphabetised by given name, a column is a letter, not a sample — a search for a surname scoped to one column is scoped to one first name, which is the wrong axis entirely. The Michael, Jacob and George absences survived the widening and are now whole‑list. The Christopher did not survive it; it appeared because of it.

13 August 2026 — a Württemberg village and a Pennsylvania congregation, tested against each other
A passenger off the 1754 ship acquires a Pennsylvania address — and the one objection turns out to be internal

Two things this project already held were finally set side by side. On the German side: Hans Jerg Welthe of Gültstein, near Herrenberg in Württemberg, baptised 22 April 1710, a widower who remarries in 1749 to Anna Barbara, daughter of Andreas Rotter of Kuppingen — and who signs the 1754 Richard & Mary passenger list as Hans Jerg Welte. On the Pennsylvania side: John George Wilte of Douglass Township, Philadelphia County, dead by June 1767, whose administration is granted to “Anna Barbara Wilte, widow and relict.” The same unusual double given name, the same wife’s name, a township in the middle of the corridor this family is chased along, and an age that fits a landless jobbing tradesman who landed at forty‑four and died at fifty‑seven leaving a hammer, an anvil and a bed.

What is uncomfortable is the bookkeeping. Two entries in this log asked, on 2 August, whether the Gültstein man had a wife called Anna Barbara. Two other entries had answered it on 1 August, in the same spreadsheet. Nobody put them together for eleven days.

One record stands against the join, and it is a printed line: “Welte, George — April 26, 1767 — 36 y.” A man of thirty‑six was not born in 1710. But the same printed volume carries, for June 1764, “George Waelte, George Waelte’s son … 22 years” — a son born about 1742, to a father who on the burial’s arithmetic would have been eleven years old. Both figures have been image‑verified here since 2 August and nobody had subtracted one from the other. The contradiction is inside the book, not between the book and a theory. The whole W page of that death list has since been read end to end: there is no second George Welte in it to take the blame. Nothing is merged and no identity is assigned. But the objection has changed character, and the honest reading is that either the printed age is wrong or the confirmand’s father died somewhere this register does not record.

Said plainly, because it should be: this is not the Michael this project is trying to place. If the join holds it seats the third of three brothers, and it moves the corridor up the queue for the other two. That is all it does.

One man, three spellings, six leaves, five weeks

That Douglass Township estate had only ever been read at one leaf — the inventory somebody once found by searching a name. The whole file was pulled instead: Philadelphia administration file no. 95 of 1767, images 297 to 302 of the film, with file no. 96 beginning at 303, so the packet is closed and complete at six leaves. Wrapper, inventory docket, the appraisers’ sworn oath, the administration bond of 19 June 1767, the bond docket, the inventory.

The wrapper and the bond call him WILTE. The appraisers’ oath calls him GEORGE WELDY. The inventory calls him GEORGE WELTY. One man, one bundle, three clerks, five weeks. And the practical half of it: searching Welty returns the inventory, searching Wilte returns the wrapper, and neither returns the oath. Four of the six leaves exist only to somebody who walks the film image by image. Spelling cannot firewall an identity, and a name search is not a reading of a record.

The assumption underneath the corridor model was tested three times in one day and failed all three

Much of this project’s reasoning about German families in Pennsylvania leans on a premise: that the people standing round a household — godparents, sureties, witnesses — are its kin. Three independent tests landed on the same day and none of them supports it for this family.

At Gültstein, the sponsor column of Hans Jerg’s four baptisms was read for the first time. The same couple — Hans Martin Nueber and Elisabetha, with a Mayer pair — stands at every one, across two marriages, from 1741 to 1750. Not one Welthe kinsman sponsors any of them, in a village where his father and brothers were living. In Pennsylvania, twenty surnames lifted from his Gültstein world were run against the entire New Hanover register: sixteen return zero, and Nueber — the tightest tie the German record shows — does not appear at all, in the baptisms or in sixteen confirmation classes spanning 1743 to 1825. And the 1767 bond names the widow’s co‑obligor: not a Douglass neighbour and not a Welte, but Christian Schneider, gentleman, of the City of Philadelphia — a professional surety, signing in his own hand while she signs by her mark.

A fourth casualty, from the same reading. The godfather‑naming custom documented five times over in the Edenkoben line does not operate at Gültstein: neither child there is named for a godparent. A naming custom proved in one branch is not a property of the surname — and namesake hunts aimed with it in the Württemberg thread have been aimed wrong.

A burial register read line by line, so that a negative could be trusted

The Gültstein baptism of a Johann Georg in August 1741 — the only candidate for the twenty‑two‑year‑old confirmed in Pennsylvania in 1764 — carries a death cross in the register margin. If he died as an infant the whole thread collapses. So the village’s burials were read entry by entry, both pages of every leaf, 1741 through 1748 entire. There is no burial for him, and no Welthe child is buried at Gültstein in any of those eight years.

One Welthe burial does exist in the run and it was not what anyone was looking for: 22 May 1748, “Joh: Michel Waelthe, todtgeb: töchterlin” — Johann Michel Welthe’s stillborn daughter, which puts him at Gültstein as a married householder six years before the crossing. That is what makes the zero worth logging: a sweep that demonstrably detected the surname when it was present is a different grade of negative from one that simply reports nothing.

And one near‑miss refused. March 1746 offers “Hß Waelther hier” — tempting at viewer scale. Cropped at full resolution and set directly beneath the 1748 Waelthe in the same clerk’s hand, the terminals do not match: the 1746 form carries a distinct Kurrent -er. It reads Walther, a different and common surname. No rung is claimed, and it is written down so the next reader does not claim one.

Two counties opened for the first time, and a landholding family in each

Northampton County — the Lehigh Valley, due north, neither of the two directions this project has been working — had never been searched under any spelling. It holds a household. Philip Welty of Moore Township is assessed at 40 acres in 1785 and 60 in 1786 and 1788, all four entries read at the printed page, and three assessors spell him Welty, Welty, Weldy. Eleven years earlier a Philip Weldi warrants 50 acres in the same county, on 14 July 1774. Same given name, same county, compatible acreage. Not merged — the county is large and the townships are unstated — but it is the strongest single reason to work it.

The Land Office’s own warrant register for Lancaster County, covering the ground before York was carved out of it in 1749, produced four warrantees this project had never seen: Peter Weltee, 135 acres on a branch of the Conestoga, 19 January 1733 — the earliest land record on this ladder by twelve years; Joseph Welds in 1738, never returned and never patented; Andreas Welt in Lebanon Township in 1742; and Christian Weltie, 267 acres in Hempfield Township in 1743. That last surname is a fourth new rung, of exactly the shape found last week: two letters at the tail, where every older rung has one, so every tail‑anchored search this project has ever run went past it.

Hempfield then turns up in a second record class entirely. A 200‑acre landholder there prints as Walty Dan’ll in 1772, Walty Daniel in 1773 and Weldy Daniel in 1782 — same acreage, same neighbours, three returns. It is the cleanest demonstration in this log that Walty and Weldy are one assessor’s hand doing one man. And the same register confirms, from a second state series, the Manheim tract found last week: Peter Welty, warrant no. 265, 26 February 1745, returned at 97 acres 120 perches — to the day and to the perch.

One entry is recorded specifically so nobody leans on it. “Weldy, John — 400 acres — Dauphin County — 3 April 1794” is the seventh of seven consecutive 400‑acre warrants all dated the same day. That is a block application at the land office, not seven neighbours taking up farms, and it evidences a name on a list and nothing about residence.

Two defects in this project’s own searching, both found by reading pages instead of text

The first bounds a great deal of past work. In the printed Pennsylvania Archives tax volumes the rated taxables print surname first — “Walty, Dan’ll” — but each township’s list ends with a freemen section that inverts the order: “Jacob Walty.” A search pattern shaped Surname, Given therefore matches no freeman anywhere in the series. A freeman is a landless adult male over twenty‑one — a son of the house, or a young immigrant — which is precisely the demographic every unresolved question here turns on. Two sweeps of this log claim forty‑three volumes between them and do not record whether their pattern was bare. Their coverage is now stated as rated taxables only until somebody measures it.

The second is a rule quoted from the archive itself: a county’s warrant register begins when the county does, and earlier records were never transferred from the parent county. So this project’s finding that no Welty warrant exists in the York register is correct and correctly bounded — it measures the register, which starts in 1749, not the land. Everything before that lies in Lancaster’s volume, which is exactly where the four warrantees above were sitting.

On the Manchester side, a father-in-law in his own words

George Shram of Manchester Township, yeoman — father of the Anna Maria who married into the Manchester Welty household — made his will on 3 October 1772, and letters testamentary were granted on 10 November 1772. He died inside those five weeks; this project previously had only “before 1790.” His widow Elizabeth takes a third of the moveables and the plantation “during the term of her widowhood” until the youngest child turns twenty‑one — and she was still a widow eighteen years later. He devises the plantation to “my nine children … share and share alike”, which confirms in the testator’s own words the nine‑share arithmetic that had been inferred from a 1790 quit‑claim. He signed by mark, as his daughter still did in 1790. And a long‑standing puzzle dissolves: the Welshhans who turns up as a court‑appointed guardian in that 1790 deed is there because Joseph Welshhance had been George Shram’s own executor eighteen years earlier.

The cheapest lead available is two entries nobody has multiplied together

Three times in a fortnight now, the answer to a question in this log has already been in the log. A row asked on 2 August what another row had answered on 1 August. A Philadelphia tax entry and a Philadelphia baptism entry named the same man and sat uncompared for weeks. And a confirmation line and a burial line, in one printed book, filed on one day from one session’s captures, turned out to be arithmetically impossible together.

That is not luck, it is a symptom. The evidence file has outgrown what any single working session can hold in mind, so the material accumulates faster than the connections between it. The practical consequence is cheerful enough: before opening a new archive, the best-value hour is usually spent cross‑reading what is already written down. It costs nothing, it needs no subscription, and lately it has been outperforming the archives.

12 August 2026 — a butcher in the city, and a ship list that is not a passenger list
The first Welty household this project has inside the City of Philadelphia

Bernard Welty, butcher, of Mulberry Ward West Part — five assessments across three printed volumes, 1779, 1779, 1780, 1781 and 1782, every one read at the page. The trade is stated only on the 1780 list; the other formats carry no occupation column. But the neighbour block is the same every year, and it is not an accident of alphabet: Peter Dehaven, Philip Grummell butcher, Bernard Welty butcher, Elias Fry butcher, Widow Christler’s estate. Three butchers consecutive is a trade block.

He was not unknown to this project so much as unresolved: an index headword, “Welty Bernard — xiv, xv, xvi,” has sat in the log for weeks with nobody cashing it. And he meets something else already filed here — three children of Bernard Welte and Mary baptised at St Joseph’s Catholic, Willing’s Alley, in 1771, 1776 and 1778, read eyes‑on and graded at the time as an unrelated family. One Bernard, one city, 1771 to 1782. The two records had never been put side by side. The join is a hypothesis with five named tests written under it, no identity is assigned, and nothing is merged.

The 1754 ship list counts fares, not people — and the difference is about 139 human beings

The heading of the Richard & Mary list states its own arithmetic: 230 freights, 91 qualified. A freight is a fare unit, not a person — children under five travelled free, children between five and ten at half fare — and only males of sixteen and over took the oath and signed. So at least around 139 full‑fare passengers of that ship appear on no list at all, and a wife or a child travelling with any of the three Welte men who signed is invisible by construction, not by accident.

This matters because two of the longest‑running questions here have been argued partly from the absence of a name on that list. Both are re‑armed. Separately, a claim this project had carried — that a Welty paid ten dollars toward somebody’s passage — was chased to its source and does not survive: the record is a John Welte, blacksmith, of Hannover Township, charged ten shillings sixpence, roughly three per cent of a Rotterdam fare. Redemption was worked off in service and was never logged as cash. The reading is dead, and the printed Philadelphia city and county tax corpus was swept whole in the same pass, returning a measured zero everywhere in the county.

A Berks County colony that dissolves the moment somebody looks at the page

The printed Berks tax volume yields ten candidate tokens on this family’s spelling ladder. Read at the leaf, eight of them are not on the ladder at all. The Rockland Township cluster — a Michael, a Jacob, a Peter and a Gottlieb, the exact shape a migration corridor would predict — is the WEIDA family. The Cumru man who prints as Welth is a Weith, one man across three spellings. The remaining two are real, and they are the one Berks man this project already had: George Welde of Oley in 1767, George Welte cooper of Oley in 1768.

The earlier Berks measurement therefore holds, and is upgraded from machine grade to printed‑page grade. What is worth keeping is the warning attached to it. A machine sweep trusting the text layer of that volume would have reported a Welty colony in Rockland Township — and it would have looked exactly like the thing the model predicts. A false positive that confirms your model is the most dangerous kind there is.

10 August 2026, later — a militia bundle read from its wrapper instead of its target
The packet names its own townships, and three of its collectors

Every previous pass at the 1781 York County militia classing had opened the film at the leaf carrying the Welty names and read outward from there. This time the bundle was read from the beginning, images 358 to 373. The first two leaves — filmed twice, and never looked at — are the exterior wrapper: “1781 / Township Assessments / DOVER / MANCHESTER.” A question this project had been trying to answer from printed controls and class headings is answered outright, in the record’s own hand, on a leaf nobody had turned to. It is a two‑township bundle, which also explains why Manchester leaves keep surfacing in the same run.

Inside it: seven class lists, but only three notification lists and three duplicates survive. Three collectors are named — George Schettel for the first class, John Obertheur for the third, Charles Hoffman for the fifth — and two of them stand on the very class lists they were collecting, so the collector was drawn from among the men he called on. The fifth class’s notification is endorsed “The within Class being Notified proved DELINQUENT”: it furnished no man. Three of the images turn out not to be name lists at all but the printed circular of a second recruiting act, of 2 June 1781 — so the Dover classes were worked twice that year, which is a thing any request to the state archives about this material now has to ask about.

What the bundle does not contain is the leaf that would settle whether the Dover Welte in question stood in the third class in January 1781, before nine names were struck from it. There is no third‑class notification list, and a native‑resolution re‑download of the relevant images returned files byte‑identical to the ones already on disk. The question is unreachable on this film by any route, which is a genuinely useful thing to know: it stops the next session spending a day on it.

10 August 2026 — the printed roll gives up its ordering, and takes a neighbour with it
The Dover tax returns are grouped by initial letter — which corrects an entry on this page

Two printed leaves were read at the page image for the first time: the Dover returns of 1781 and 1782 in Egle’s transcript of the Pennsylvania Archives, both pages whole, top to bottom. Every earlier reading of this material here was taken from the volume’s machine-generated text layer, which is a different thing from the page.

Read whole, the 1782 Dover run marches Wolgemuth, Wolf, Wolf, Wentz, Wolfgill, Wild, Walse, Walch, Weever, Worley, Worley, Welty Philip 80, Welty Jacob 80, William Michael 40 — then Yoner, Yage, Yage, Yoh, then Zin, Zin, Zimmerman, and then an unsorted tail of late additions before the single men. The 1781 leaf does the same: a solid block of S, then T, then U, then W. The list is grouped by initial letter and unsorted inside the group. The letters march A to Z; the names within a letter do not.

This corrects a sentence published on this page a week ago, which read that the printed returns are demonstrably not alphabetized, on the evidence of a 1779 run that prints Wilte Paul, Wilte John, Welty Philip, Welty George in that order. That evidence was real and the conclusion drawn from it was too narrow: it is right about the inside of a letter and wrong about the shape of the list. Two consequences follow immediately. The gap that was measured and could not be explained — John Rothrauf standing some four hundred entries from the Weltys in the print and four lines from Michel Welte on the militia class leaf — is now explained, and it is arithmetic rather than evidence. He is an R. The print files him with the R men wherever his farm actually lay. And the caution about a York list of 1787 being alphabetized now reaches back six years further in a weaker, partial form.

The holding this project already had on the question does not loosen; it hardens, and now it has a mechanism under it. Being printed beside a Wilt, a Wallick, a Wilcock or a Wilhelm means that surname begins with W and nothing else. What the entry deliberately does not decide is whose grouping it is — the assessor’s, reproduced faithfully, or the editor’s, imposed in 1898. That is the whole of the open question, and it can only be answered at the manuscript rolls. If the manuscript leaves are grouped the same way, then no colonial Dover tax list of any year can carry neighbourhood information at all. No identity and no acreage moves: Philip at 110, 100 and 80, Jacob at 80 and 100, Michael at 44, John deceased at 50, the widow at 80 and George at 120 and 80 all stand exactly as read.

A phantom Michael, and the broken type that would have made him

The man printed immediately below the Weltys in both years is a Michael Wilhelm — sixty acres in 1781, forty in 1782 — and this page has already flagged him as the likeliest way a second Michael Welty could get invented by a tired reader. At the image he turns out to be a worse trap than that, and for a different reason. The 1781 page is broken type. The surname sets as “Wiinilm” or “Wilnilm” — letters physically damaged in the 1898 printing, not mangled by a scanner, as this project had recorded. The 1782 page sets the same man cleanly as WILLIAM, Michael, 40.

So one volume gives one man two surnames in two consecutive years, one of them not a word in any language. And with the letter-grouping established above, the other half of his story goes too: he was never standing next to the Weltys in any sense that means anything. He was standing next to them in the alphabet.

9 August 2026 — the county’s own index answers, and a federal tax does not
The new spelling turns up in an eighteenth-century Pennsylvania hand

Three days ago a rung was added to this family’s spelling ladder off a single label on a twentieth-century draughtsman’s map: W‑H‑E‑L‑T‑Y, lettered beside a Manheim Township tract. One label is one label. Today it stands in a second place, in a different century, written by somebody else. York County’s own index to its Quarter Sessions dockets carries “WHELTY, John — Officials — Manheim Twp — March 1778 — overseer of the poor,” docket book 1‑3, page 268.

An overseer of the poor was appointed at the March sessions from among seated, rated householders, so this is not merely a name in a list — it is a man established in his township and answerable to its court, which is a class of record this line has very little of. Fourteen years after the patent, a county clerk letters the surname the way the Land Office draughtsman would. The same index does the trick a second time in the same township: Abraham is entered as constable of Manheim in March 1766 as Welty and again in April 1766 as Weltey — one man, one office, one year, two spellings, and the earliest civic record on the ladder anywhere in the county. No identity is assigned to either man. What is established is the spelling, not the person.

A county archive read end to end, and the last cheap hope for the missing years closes

The York County Archives publishes sixty-two record sets, and this project had been using only one of the two ways in. Besides the scanned deed indexes read last week, the portal runs a searchable name database covering forty-three of those sets — free, no login, and matching on any fragment of a name rather than on a whole one. That last property is not a detail: a fragment supersets several rungs at once, and it is literally how the entry above was found, because a search anchored on Welt can never return Whelty.

Five of the searchable classes reach the years in question — the Quarter Sessions dockets from 1749, the sheriff’s and treasurer’s deeds, administration bonds, the coroner’s records and naturalizations. The full ladder was run across all five. No Michael, on any rung, in any class.

The one worth firing was the apprenticeship long shot, and it missed properly rather than vaguely. That index classifies apprenticeship and indentured servant explicitly, and carries 46, 51 and 89 such entries across 1749–1834, naming masters, servants and the fathers who petitioned — not one of them on the ladder. The twenty-two blank years before Michael surfaces in Dover are not going to be answered by a York binding-out at this finding aid. Two drawers nobody here had named came out of the same read and go on the list in exchange: the county’s mortgage indexes, which begin in 1775 — mortgages before 1842 were recorded inside the deed books, so those volumes have been read but this separate finding aid over them never has — and the Clerk of Courts’ miscellaneous papers from 1767, which the county’s own description says may include records of indentured servants and apprentices.

A Welte holding township office in Dover, four years before Michael appears there

“WELTE, George — Officials — Dover Twp — March 1775 — supervisor of highways,” docket book 1‑3, page 261. In Dover Township before 1801 that index holds exactly two entries anywhere on the ladder: this one and Philip Welty in 1786. It is not a thin corpus — the same index carries 342 Dover rows across 133 surnames for 1775–1800 — so the scarcity is a real measurement rather than an artefact of coverage.

The temptation here is obvious and is being refused. A George Weldy baptised three children at Strayer’s Reformed in Dover in 1763, 1764 and 1767, and that last baptism is currently his final dated record; if the two are one man, that bracket moves eight years. But the index gives no age, no father and no residence beyond the township, George is not a rare name, and the entry sits in tension with a “Walty widow” who first appears on the Dover provincial tax of the very same year. A serving supervisor of highways and a widow in one township in one year are not obviously one household. The entry is filed unassigned, with the test written beside it: the 1775 Dover tax, read against this.

A correction this site may owe itself, reported rather than made

The other Dover entry is “WELTY, Philip — Tavern — Dover Twp — July 1786,” docket book 14, page 124. That classification contradicts a reading published on this page: an entry that walked back an older tavernkeeper claim, on the grounds that the July 1786 licence list, read at the image, holds no Dover township and no Philip.

Both halves can be checked, and the index says that reading was right about the page and wrong about the list. Page 123 does hold no Dover — confirmed. But the list continues across the page turn. Reconstructed township by township out of the index, page 123 carries 20 entries in 6 townships and page 124 carries 63 in 18, all 83 classified as tavern licences at the July 1786 sessions — and Dover, with Philip on it, is on the second page. The count even reconciles with the roughly one hundred men the original reading tallied, once the two townships whose queries overflow the server are added back.

It is left standing as a contradiction on purpose. A licence would be the first occupational record this project holds for him, and he appears in no other year, in a township whose licence names otherwise recur year on year. That is exactly the sort of claim that should not be settled by an index against a page somebody read with their own eyes. The manuscript has not been re-read, so nothing is re-graded; what is written down instead is the ten-minute test — does a running heading carry over onto page 124, is there a fresh heading, are the entries numbered?

A whole record class worked through, and one township that ends a hope

The five remaining York County connected drafts — the Land Office’s compiled warrantee maps — were read: Franklin, Washington and Monaghan; Newberry and Fairview; Chanceford and Hopewell; Digges’ Choice; Chanceford and Lower Chanceford. Not one ladder name on any of them, as warrantee, patentee or adjoiner.

So across all seven York sheets in this class there is one ladder warrantee — Peter Welty of Manheim — and one ladder adjoiner, on that same sheet. That concentration is the finding, and it is the second time in a week the same shape has arrived from an independent direction: the county’s own deed indexes said last week that the eighteenth-century York land record for this surname is essentially Manheim; the state’s cartography now says it too, from the other side of the transaction.

The Newberry and Fairview sheet was read whole — legend and every label on the ground — because Newberry is the township of a Philip Jacob this project keeps firewalled from the Dover man of the same name. Nine warrantees, from James Rankin’s master survey down through tracts named Stragler and Hamburg, and twenty-odd adjoiners lettered across the ground. No ladder name anywhere on the sheet, which removes the cheapest remaining hope that a Newberry warrant stands behind him.

The grade is stated rather than implied, because half of this sweep was done by machine. One sheet is verified whole by eye; three more have their legends verified by eye; their bodies, and the whole of the degraded Digges’ Choice blueprint, were screened by optical character recognition at a recall measured at 61 per cent — measured, because the eye had already established ground truth on that same class first. A rung could sit in the missing 39. Four ladder-shaped machine hits were pulled back to the image and all four dissolved: one was a diagonal read stitched across a legend box, one was William Wilson, one was William Actkins, and one was a name printed upside down.

The new spelling’s first record of its own, in Maryland

Both new rungs — the one that gains a letter at the head, and the one found last week with a two-letter tail — were typed into two large record collections for the first time, across the eighteenth century and the nineteenth. In the eighteenth every new shape returns zero, against controls that reproduced this project’s own earlier counts exactly, so the zero is a measurement rather than a shrug. Neither new rung reaches the arrival window in those corpora.

In the nineteenth, one of them returns its first record anywhere: William Henrey Whelty, born 4 November 1842, baptised 10 October 1846 at Grace Evangelical Lutheran, Rocky Hill, Frederick County, Maryland. The same man is carried as William H. Whelty in 1900 and as William Welty in 1870 — one household lettered both ways across fifty years. Nothing is merged and no relationship is claimed. What it does is corroborate a map label from an entirely independent chain of records, which is what a single-document reading needs before anyone leans on it.

Springfield Township in 1798: the weaver is not on a federal tax he cannot have missed

The Montgomery County household read here last week — Michael Weltz, weaver, who bought a messuage and four and a quarter acres in Springfield Township in July 1781 and was still living in that house when he wrote his will in August 1805 — was carried into the two tax classes nobody had opened for him: the 1798 federal Direct Tax and the county’s own tax and exoneration series. He is on neither, under any spelling on the ladder.

The township’s only Michael with a W‑surname is entry 82 of the particular list, and at the native scan the surname is Wells — double l, clear terminal s — in two different clerks’ hands on two different schedules. Three whole acres, against the weaver’s four and a quarter. Every building column blank, on a page where the form is plainly in live use: entry 80 carries “1 old log Barn,” entry 83 “1 Smith’s Shop 30 by 18,” entry 84 “1 Frame Shop 30 by 20.” The blank is the assessor’s, not the form’s.

Reading the whole block rather than the one line produced a second and independent witness to the same thing. Dollars per acre across entries 78 to 86 sit in a band of $21 to $26 for bare land; the only entry above it is the man with the smith’s shop, at $40. Wells is at $30 — just above bare ground and nowhere near an improved lot. The weaver’s own four and a quarter acres, with the messuage on them, were appraised at $800 in 1811, about $188 an acre. Those are different kinds of property.

And still the entry is not simply somebody else, because the adjoining proprietor named beside it is Philip Peterman — one of the two men named as adjoiners in the weaver’s own 1781 title deed. So either entry 82 is him, badly rendered and strangely under-assessed, or he is missing from a federal tax he cannot have been missing from. Neither horn is chosen here. What did move is a refusal that used to rest on a base rate: the county series carries a Michael Wells as a continuous Springfield taxable from 1779, two years before the weaver bought there, and a separate Michael Wills continuous in Plymouth Township. The look-alike has stopped being a statistical objection and become a documented person with a name and a township of his own.

What an index can and cannot be asked

Almost everything above came out of finding aids other people made: a county archive’s name database, a state land office’s compiled maps, a commercial collection’s search box, an assessor’s ruled form. Between them they produced four zeros, one new spelling standing at last in an eighteenth-century hand, and one flat contradiction with something published on this page. The zeros are the ordinary product of the work and the least interesting part of it. The contradiction is the part worth stopping over.

An index is a claim about a document, not the document. It can hand over a rung that a hundred careful searches missed, purely because it happens to match on fragments rather than on whole names; it can say, in a county’s own published words, that a drawer has no finding aid at all and must be walked in person. What it cannot do is outvote a page somebody has actually read. When the two disagree — as they do above, over whether a name sits on a tavern list — the honest entry is the disagreement itself, with the test that would settle it written underneath, and nothing moved on the tree until somebody goes and looks.

8 August 2026 — one estate, two clerks, two spellings
The same man, spelled two different ways, in one county’s own records

York County Wills Book B, page 26, read at the manuscript. It is not a will but the Register’s own memorandum of the letters he granted: “Letters Testamentary (with a copy of an English Translation of a Will in the German Language annexed) were Granted to Abraham Weldie and Johannes Hunsecker the Executors of the Testament and Last Will of Christian Wampffler Deceased … at York the twenty third day of November 1764.” Signed by the deputy register, Sam. Johnston.

The document itself is a small thing. The pair it makes is not. The same executor, settling the same neighbour’s estate, appears in the York orphans’ court docket of 29 May 1766 as Welty — a reading settled at the image four days ago — and in the Register’s book twenty-six months earlier as Weldie. Two clerks in one courthouse, one man, one estate, two spellings. That is the cleanest demonstration this project has yet found that the spelling ladder this family is chased across is a property of the record-keepers rather than a symptom of separate families. Nothing is merged and no identity is assigned; what moves is the ladder, not the tree.

And the rung itself has a shape nobody had allowed for. Both contested letters were graded against controls in the same line and the same hand — the d against the word “and” standing immediately after the surname, the terminal -ie against the clerk’s own -y in “Testamentary” and “twenty” four words away. Weldie ends in two letters. Every rung on this ladder before it ends in one — y, i, e, t, d — or in -tz. So every tail-anchored search this project has ever run, and there have been many, could not have matched it. Two days ago the head gained a letter; now the tail has too.

A clause that looked like boilerplate turns out to be a filter

The standard wording in these books is “Letters Testamentary in common Form” — which is exactly what stands at Peter Welty’s own memorandum in the previous volume, and at Bartholomew Maul’s beside it. The Register wrote the longer phrase, “with a copy of an English Translation of a Will in the German Language annexed,” only where the will really was in German. Two testators carry it in 1764: Wampffler and Henry Worth.

Two consequences, and the second is the useful one. Where a York testator wrote in German, the will book can only ever hold an English translation of him — the German original stayed with the estate packet, so no will book will give such a man his own orthography. And the phrase is a searchable marker: it finds the German-language testators of a county in one pass. It also sharpens an errand already on the list, since a will that reached the Register untranslated is precisely what a volume of abstracts of unrecorded wills would hold. Peter Welty’s, for the record, was not one of them.

A negative that measures the index and not the volume

Book B carries its own front index, and its W leaf holds no name on the ladder — ten entries, all enumerated in the log, Welshantz through Wilkeston, with the facing leaf clean. That measures the index, not the book, and the proof of the limit is sitting on page 26: Abraham Weldie is in the volume and not in its index, because the index lists testators and he is an executor. A whole class of Welty appearance — witness, executor, guardian, adjoiner — is invisible to every index of this kind, which is the same lesson the orphans’ court compilers taught this project four days ago in their own introduction.

The Springfield weaver’s own will, four pages of it, read at the image

Two days ago a one-line stub in this project’s decoy list turned out to have a whole Montgomery County household behind it. Today that household has its founding document. Montgomery County Will Book 3, pages 327 to 330: “I Michael Weltz of Springfield township in the County of Montgomery … weaver, being far advanced in Years and infirm in Body.” Written 14 August 1805, proved 30 September 1811.

It is a modest, precise, entirely human instrument. To his daughter: the best bed and bedding, a case of drawers, the corner cupboard, the ten‑plate stove and a spinning wheel; fifty pounds within a year of his decease; and the upstairs room he had been living in, with water from the spring, yard room for her wood, use of the oven and the cellar and passage through the kitchen — “provided she live single and unmarried,” ceasing the moment she married. To George, “one of my Weaving Looms.” The messuage and lot to the three of them in common, to be valued by three disinterested freeholders, one chosen by each child, with George free to take it at the appraisement. Executors: his son George and “my trusty friend Jacob Lentz … Shopkeeper” of Philadelphia County. Witnesses Christopher Yeakle and Jacob Sitzman — the vowel in the second surname is not settled even at full resolution.

And what it does for this project is a subtraction, which is what was wanted. The will names three children and no fourth, and no wife anywhere. No Christina. No Henry. No York. A man already “far advanced in Years” in 1805, on ground he bought in 1781, thirty years before the Michael this project follows died in Ohio. The firewall between the two was drawn a month ago on inference; it now rests on the man’s own instrument. Nothing is merged, and the household stays unattached — but it is now an unattached household that has actually been read.

One instrument, one legatee, two given names

The daughter of that will is called Susanna twice and Cathrine three times. Same hand, same four pages, no marginal note, nothing in the document that flags it. A reading of page 327 alone would never have known there was a question; a reading of page 329 alone would have logged a Catharine Weltz who does not otherwise exist anywhere.

There is a tie-breaker and it is the right kind. The three children executed a partition deed themselves on 11 May 1812, and it recites the very freeholder clause the will book renders Cathrine — and renders it Susanna. So the log reads her as Susanna, carries Cathrine as the copyist’s variant, and decides nothing further; she is not entered on the family roster under either name. The general rule earned here has been written into this project’s standing conclusions: when a person is named more than once in one instrument, read every mention and count them before writing the name down — and when they disagree, prefer the instrument the parties themselves signed over the one a clerk copied.

A full-text zero that turned out not to be a wall

Worth recording because it unblocks work rather than closing it. The search that found this household two days ago also established that FamilySearch’s full-text service holds only the two Montgomery County deed volumes and no will book at all. That negative was true and it stopped nothing: the Register of Wills’ own filmed every‑name index produced the will in about twenty minutes, on the first spelling tried. Its arrangement is worth knowing for any county — surname initial plus given‑name initial, each block running chronologically from 1784, so every eighteenth‑century entry sits on the first leaf of its block. When the machine index comes back empty, go to the archive’s own finding aid.

With one caution attached, because it cost an hour. That index’s page column was read through the viewer as 347, which is a different family entirely; pulled at full resolution it reads 327, the first page of the will. A screen‑resolution crop is enough to read a clause and is not enough to read a number or a surname. The finding aid was right and the reading of it was wrong — the second time in two days this project has had to move the blame back onto itself.

A challenge to the oldest age evidence in the project, and it held — but for a different reason than anyone thought

The one primary statement of Michael’s age is a tick in a column of the 1800 census for Donegal Township, Westmoreland County. That tick was looked at again today and read as sitting in the 26–44 bracket rather than 45 and over — which, if right, would knock out the only record contradicting the pedigree’s “born about 1757.” That is a fair misread, and the reason is instructive: the ditto marks in the two columns before it are faint at screen resolution, and reading past them lands the third stroke one column early.

Rather than defend the reading, the page was measured. Thresholding the scan and counting dark pixels per column returns the ruled lines directly: left edge, one name column, twelve tally columns of about eighty‑eight pixels each, right edge. Nothing unnumbered precedes the first, so column one is under‑ten and the head’s stroke, in column five, is the top male bracket. The control is on the next line down — Wilmore David, same hand, ticks 26–44 and dittos the column above it, while Witty Michael dittos 26–44 and ticks the column above. Both columns are in live use on adjacent lines, and Michael is one column older than his neighbour whatever the absolute labels say. The arithmetic checks it a third time: six males and five females is eleven, the index total exactly.

And then a correction this project had been carrying without noticing. The bracket has been quoted here for weeks as “45–65.” There is no such bracket. That ceiling is the index’s rendering; the 1800 schedule’s top male column reads “45 and upwards,” open‑ended, and no United States census carries an age bracket above 45 until 1830. So the record’s age statement is one‑sided: born on or before about 1755, with no lower bound at all. It still contradicts the pedigree’s 1757. It still leaves both readings of him alive — an older Pennsylvania‑born man, or the 1754 shipmate — and it decides neither. An index range narrower than the form’s own bracket is the index talking, not the record.

Where the Springfield weaver was before 1781: nowhere in print

The obvious guess about a German weaver in that corner of the county is Germantown, and it does not survive.
