The only printed Philadelphia County tax lists that exist — 1769, 1774 and 1779 — were swept whole on the ladder and then every W‑surname in the two townships that matter was enumerated by hand. Germantown 1779: four hundred and seventy‑five entries, thirty‑two W‑surnames, not one on the ladder. Springfield in any printed year: none either. And the scope limit matters as much as the zero — Germantown appears exactly once in the whole volume, so this measures one year, not the township.

One entry was tempting and is refused: “Michael Wells” in Springfield in 1779, two years before Weltz bought there, in a county whose transcripts render this very surname as Wells, Wills and Wetts. The base rate says no. Wells is ordinary in this corpus, twenty‑one entries across fourteen wards and townships, and one township carries a “Michael Wells, Jun’r,” which entails a senior. Then the printed page settled it outright: a Michael Wills and a Michael Wells stand twelve lines apart on the same leaf, in the same township, in the same year. Two ordinary men of two ordinary names. Nothing merged, and the man still has no documented existence before 24 July 1781.

7 August 2026 — the will behind the tract
Peter Welty of Manheim, in his own instrument at last

This project has known of Peter Welty’s 1755 will for a month, entirely through a compiled county chronicle. It is now read at the manuscript: York County Wills Book A, pages 113 to 115, dated 30 June 1755 and proved 22 August 1755 — seven weeks between the two, so he was writing it knowing.

To his wife Catharina, thirty pounds “and that forever,” the dwelling house for life “if she remaen a widow,” and a yearly keep set out in wheat, rye, pork, cows and flax. Then the land: “All my Land, so well that Land which is in John Dixons Linne as well that other Pice of Land which is Tacken up by Pennsylvannia, I Do Give to my two sons John and Abraham Welty … Share and Share a Like” — charged to keep their mother and to pay “to theer Shisters” a hundred and fifty pounds, and twenty‑five pounds every year. John takes two cows beforehand. Executors: Michael Danner and “my son John.”

Three things fall out of it. The daughters lose their count. The chronicle says “plus five daughters”; the will gives no number at all, only “theer Shisters.” The five belongs to the compiler, not to the record, and it has been marked as his. A witness ties two record classes together: Henry Bowman, who signs this will, is the eastern adjoiner of the very tract put on the ground yesterday — the first human being to appear in both documents. And a question asked in this log three days ago is answered in the testator’s own words: the discriminator wanted for the John Welty taking a Manheim guardianship in 1774 was a record naming him as somebody’s son, and here it is — “my son John.”

The caution, stated with it. This is the Register’s recorded copy, not the original, so every spelling in it is a clerk’s. And Peter signed by mark — so however many times his name is written on these three pages, not one of them is his own hand, and nothing here adds a rung to the ladder. What would sharpen it is the estate packet at the county archives: the inventory and the administration account are the records most likely to give the daughters their names.

6 August 2026 — a whole record class comes back empty, and a household nobody was looking for
There is no Michael Welty in the York County deed indexes. In any year, on either side.

The grantor and grantee indexes to York County deeds, 1749 to 1912, read at the page image across the whole Welty block — and the block is genuinely whole: the ladder heads Welty, Welte, Weltee, Weldy, Weldi and Wheity all gather under one index heading, and the twenty‑three given names on the grantor side and sixteen on the grantee side are enumerated in the log as the proof that nothing was skipped. No Michael Welty appears on either side in any year.

Two negatives fall out of one reading, and the second was not asked for. Michael’s forty‑four acres never entered the deed record at all, on either side — so there is no instrument to order, and a paid errand comes off the list. And Philip Jacob’s hundred and ten acres in Dover are absent too. Set beside the earlier finding that no state‑land warrant describes those farms either, it means that no recorded paper of either kind describes how the Dover Welty land was held or how it passed.

What that measures is the tenure, not the man. It fits, and corroborates, this project’s own earlier reading that the forty‑four acres were occupied rather than held in fee. It removes the deed class as a way of closing the identity gap on the Dover tax rows, which stay unassigned. What would reopen it: a conveyance recorded under a spelling no ladder head gathers; the sheriff’s and treasurer’s deeds, which this portal serves as a separate index and nobody has swept.

And the bycatch is worth more than the zero

Read whole, the eighteenth‑century Welty deed record of York County is essentially one township, and it is not Dover. Eight instruments between 1760 and 1799, nearly all Manheim: Abraham buying from Adam Simon in 1760 and from John Huntzacher in 1763; John buying from Daniel Reisher in 1764 and again in 1768; “Weltee Peter Est to John Weltee” in June 1764, the same instrument standing on both sides of the index; John selling to Michael Newman in 1771; a John in Heidelberg and Manheim in 1799.

So while the Dover Weltes leave no deed at all, Peter Welty of Manheim and his two sons are a continuously land‑transacting York family across four decades, on the county’s own record. Nothing is merged — but the contrast between the two clusters is now measured rather than assumed.

The same family arrives from a completely different record class on the same day

A Pennsylvania land‑office connected draft for Manheim Township, published free by the state and read at the native raster. Tract 10 letters out in full: “Peter Welty (Weltee) — 97 acres, 120 perches — warrant Feb. 26 1745 — survey Apr. 11 1746 — patent Jul. 6 1764 — to John Welty”, with its complete metes and bounds and its adjoiners lettered around it.

That warrant is the earliest land record on this ladder by five years, and it pre‑dates the 1750 arrival of the Dover progenitor this project follows. The patent issuing to John Welty nine years after Peter’s death is the land office confirming the son the will names, independently of the will — and it seats the John Welty who took a Manheim guardianship in 1774 on his father’s own ground. It also corrects a claim made in this log in July, that the Dover men were the only ladder warrantees in York County. They were not.

And the sheet carries two spellings that were not on the ladder. The draftsman letters the warrantee “Peter Welty (Weltee)” — so Weltee is the land office’s own form of the name. And the adjoining owner, read at ten times magnification after rotating the label a hundred and eighty degrees, letters as “John Whelty” — W‑H‑E‑L‑T‑Y. Every previous rung on this ladder has been a substitution: a vowel, a t for a d, a terminal y for an i. Whelty is an insertion. The head of the name can gain a letter — which means that even a wildcard search anchored on a single W finds nothing. Every machine sweep this project has ever run assumed one letter before the vowel.

A one-line stub in the dead-ends list had a whole household behind it

A Philip Welty of Montgomery County has sat in this project’s decoy list for weeks as a single line, kept apart from the Dover Philip Jacob on a reason that turns out to have been wrong‑footed — it treated the “Mary” beside his name as a test against Philip Jacob’s wife. She is Philip’s own wife. Read at the manuscript, the real firewall is generational and far stronger: this Philip is the son of a Michael, where the Dover Philip Jacob was the father of one and dead by about 1789.

Behind the stub stands a documented household. Michael Weltz, weaver, of Springfield Township, bought a messuage and four and a quarter acres from Frederick Knapp on 24 July 1781 for a hundred and twenty pounds in silver and gold, acknowledged before a justice for the County of Philadelphia — Montgomery not being erected until 1784. The chain runs forward through his 1805 will to his son George by the partition deed of 1812, and out to a Germantown cordwainer in 1816. His adjoiner is Christopher Yeakle.

Its value is not a link. It is a named target that every sweep this project has run through that corridor missed entirely — Montgomery County was swept hard for church records, all zeros, and never once for land. And the reason the sweeps missed it is worth writing down: the transcripts render this surname as Wells, Wills and Wetts, so no spelling of Welty would ever have surfaced it. The hits came from putting place words alongside the name instead.

The name echo everyone notices is exactly as common as it should be

The Dover Weltys run Philip, Michel, Jacob and George; the three Welty men on a 1754 ship run Michel, Jacob and Hans Jerg. It is the kind of echo that feels like evidence. So it was measured against its own county: twenty‑eight thousand two hundred name‑lines parsed out of the printed York County tax returns for 1779 to 1783, across seven thousand three hundred surnames.

Jacob is the second commonest given name in the county at 7.2 per cent, George the third at 5.7, Michael the eighth at 3.0, Philip the sixteenth. Thirty‑five York surnames carry a Michael and a Jacob and a George; thirteen carry all four names — Gross, Hoffman, King, Little, Long, Meyer, Miller, Snyder, Wagner, Welty, Ziegler among them. And Gross is the surname standing two lines above the Weltes on the 1781 militia class leaf. The echo sits at the base rate and carries no weight toward kinship in either direction. It neither supports nor damages the ship‑list question, which is precisely what a base rate is for.

4 August 2026 — the orphans’ court, swept end to end
Thirty-two years of a whole record class, and the man is not in it

The record class named at the end of the last entry has now been run, and it was free, filmed and open the whole time. The York County orphans’ court dockets for 1749 to 1781 are a single reel — one volume run covering the county from the year it was erected — viewable at home, and machine‑transcribed end to end, which means an eighteenth‑century court hand is within reach of a search box. Five hundred and ninety‑three images; the control word “guardian” returns four hundred and thirty‑eight of them.

Thirty‑two spellings were run as whole tokens across the whole reel. Exactly two images carry a Welty‑shaped surname, and neither belongs to the Dover household. There is no orphans’ court entry in thirty‑two years naming a Welty‑shaped minor, no Welty‑shaped decedent leaving minor children, and no guardianship anywhere stating a Welty parentage. Neither a Michael nor a Philip Jacob of any spelling appears on the reel at all.

Two things that costs. It bounds the twenty‑two blank years in front of Michael’s first record from a class nobody had measured: whatever he was doing before 1779, he was not a York County orphan or ward, and no York court appointed anyone to hold property for him. And it is a real cost to one branch of the ship‑list theory — the version in which an immigrant Michael of about fifty dies out of the York orbit in the 1750s or 1760s, because a man of that age dying with minor children generates a guardianship, and there is no such guardianship. Not a refutation: he could have died outside the county, or solvent with an adult son, or never have been in Pennsylvania at all.

And the honest headline is narrower than the finding feels. Five hundred and ninety‑three images were not read by eye; they were read by a machine. This measures a machine’s reach across a reel. What would reopen it is written into the row: the transcriber’s recall on this corpus is unmeasured, the engine matches whole tokens only so no stem or tail could be run, the general‑index reels are unswept, the underlying petitions and bonds are not filmed at all — and Lancaster County, which had this ground before October 1749, has never been touched.

And the printed index to the first thirteen years agrees, read the slow way

Before the film, the cheap pass: a printed every‑name index to orphans’ court Book A, 1749 to 1762. It was not searched by keyword. The whole W band was pulled out and read in alphabetical order, so that every rung could be checked against the slot it would have to occupy: Weldy would sit between Welchance and Weller, Welte and Welty between Welshover and Werdt, Wilty between Wilson and Winrod, Wetty between Wetherspoon and Wetshantz. Every slot is empty, and the printed pages either side are consecutive, so no leaf is missing. Zero Welty‑shaped surnames in the county’s first thirteen years of this court.

The compilers then hand this project one of its own standing lessons, in their introduction. The official index, they write, “does not identify all names found in the recorded entries” — and they give three worked examples of a man named in an entry and indexed only under somebody else’s surname. That is exactly why an index read can never bound a docket, and it is why the reel above had to be swept underneath it.

One method note worth passing on to anybody working the same shelf. This book is copyright‑restricted and its page images cannot be opened from home — but the digital library’s search inside box returns the full text of every matched page to a home user, and thirty‑five of its forty‑two images were recovered that way by querying common words. A copyright wall is not always a text wall.

What the reel does hold: two Weltys, in the right community, in the right decade

The two hits are not the direct line, and both are worth having. 6 December 1774, read at the image, the term heading fixed independently two openings earlier: “Came into Court Catharina Summer and prayed that a Guardian might be appointed for Andrew and Peter Summer both under the age of fourteen Years … Whereupon it is ordered by the Court that John Welty of Manheim Township be and is hereby appointed to be Guardian over the Persons and Estates of the said Children.” That is the earliest dated York County court record of any Welty this project holds, and the first primary record putting the Manheim household in a county court. A man trusted by a widow and confirmed by the bench with two children and their estate.

And eight years earlier, at the term of 29 May 1766, an Abraham Welty producing the administration account of the estate of Christian Wampler as one of its two executors — so a man of full age, born before about 1745, in a Hunsecker–Wampler–Sherman circle. Together these two put the two sons named in Peter Welty’s will into York County court records for the first time in this project, in the right decade and the right community. Nothing is merged. Neither entry names a father, a wife or a child, and Abraham and John are the two commonest given names in that community; a good‑looking fit is not a discriminator, and this project has been burned by economical readings before.

A surname the machine got wrong, and how it was settled

The 1766 executor is worth a paragraph of his own, because of how nearly he became a puzzle. The transcript read his surname “Wetty,” and this project spent some time treating that as a genuine ambiguity of the hand — it has a real instance of exactly that problem elsewhere. It was not one. The leaf was read directly at full screen resolution by a second reader and ruled Welty; this project could not resolve the letter at the resolution the viewer gives it, and said so.

Two things settled it beyond one reader’s eyes. The Wetty reading had exactly one source, the machine — and the same machine’s surname reliability was measured the same day on the same film series, where it rendered one family’s name four different ways inside a single sentence. And there is no Wetty family in York County for the clerk to have been writing: the every‑name index above runs Wetherow, Wetherspoon, Wetshantz, and the slot where a Wetty would sit is empty across the county’s first thirteen years.

The rule this earned has been written down, because it runs the opposite way to the one this project usually needs: an apparent ambiguity in a machine transcript is often not an ambiguity at all. Before writing a surname up as contested, put a human on the pixels at full resolution — and ask whether the alternative reading even has a family behind it.

The eleven-year-old Michael Welty who is not a Welty at all

Set down as a warning, because it would be a spectacular false positive if anybody met it cold. On the neighbouring reel the transcript reads: “Guardians may be appointed to take care of the Estate of Michael Welt aged eleven years, Catharine West aged thirteen years and Mary Well aged eight years, the minor children of Adam West … the Court appoint Christian Keller and Adam Welt Guardians.”

The answer is inside the sentence. The three children are stated to be the children of Adam West; the second child is West in the same line and the same hand; the third is Well. One surname is coming out of the transcriber four different ways in a single sentence, and the one the entry supplies as the father’s is West. Michael Welt is Michael West. It is the same discipline this project applies to handwriting — find the control letterform before you name the letter — turned on a machine instead of a clerk. It cuts both ways, too: if a transcript can invent a ladder name, it can also bury a real one under a token no ladder would ever contain.

A correction, two new leaves, and a “find” this project had already made

Also on the record because the rule exists for exactly this. The sweep of the 1782–1803 reel returned a cluster of entries around the death of a John Welty of Manheim, and they were drafted up as a discovery. They were not a discovery. This project had already read both leaves in July, by a different route, and logged them under a different series on a different tab — the capture files were sitting on disk with the matching archive references in their filenames while the row was being written. A new tool re-finds old evidence, and it feels exactly like finding it. The check costs one command: search the log for the record’s archive reference before writing the row, because that string is the one thing identical across tabs, series and sessions.

Three things did survive the dedupe. The docket sweep is a different kind of evidence from the index read it duplicates, and corroborates it from underneath. Two leaves are genuinely new, and they are new for precisely the reason the compilers warned about — a John Welty settling an estate indexed under the decedent’s surname, invisible to any index read. And a correction against this project’s own earlier row: the minor choosing his guardian in 1795 was read then as “aged nineteen(?)” with an honest question mark. At magnification the clerk wrote “aged sixteen.” He was born about 1778–79, not 1776.

A correction to this page: the 1796 groom goes back on the ladder

Two entries in the York Reformed register were re-read at maximum viewer zoom, and one of them reverses a withdrawal published here on 3 August.

The groom of 29 February 1796 was withdrawn from the ladder two days ago on the reading that his surname opened with a Sch‑ ligature. At full zoom the word is four or five letters, opens with a doubled‑loop capital, and terminates in ‑dy with a trema over the y. Measured against that, the 1935 typescript’s Schacky cannot stand — it needs a Sch‑ ligature and a ck cluster and is too long — and the online index’s Sperry cannot stand either, needing a double r and having no trema. The only surviving reading is the ladder one, and what remains open is a single letter: Waldy, Wady, or Waeldy. The withdrawal is itself withdrawn. A Waldy at York in 1796 is a rung, not a relative — the entry gives no age, no origin and no trade.

The other went the way this page already reported, and confirms it. The bride of 24 September 1781 reads Welschin — We, then l, then a long‑s and ch ligature, then the feminine ending. No t, no terminal y. She is a Welsch and she stays off the ladder. But the typescript that had her right had her groom wrong, and the online index is wrong about both halves of the line. Which is the lesson worth keeping from the pair: no derivative source is reliable across a whole entry. Grade each field, not each source.

3 August 2026 — late: no second Michael in the county
The question that closed the night, run across a whole county

The re-grade below closed with a reopener nobody had ever run: has anyone actually looked for a second Michael Welty in or near Dover Township between 1779 and 1783? Nobody had. Tonight the printed returns of taxables for the whole of York County — twenty-nine townships, five years, in the Pennsylvania Archives, Third Series, Vol. XXI — were swept on the full ladder of spellings crossed with every form of the given name, and then every W‑l/t‑shaped surname line in the volume was pulled out and read by eye.

Michael appears exactly five times in the whole volume, and all five are Dover. A single man in 1779, a single man in 1780, a single man in 1781 assessed beside a Jacob, a forty-four-acre landholder in 1782, a single man in 1783. Zero under any swept spelling in Manchester, Heidelberg, Manheim, Newberry or any other township in any of the five years.

What that measures is uniqueness in the printed county, not identity. Egle’s volume is a transcript, and a copyist’s normalisation can carry a man clean out of the shapes searched; the sweep is machine-assisted over scanned text, and reading the forty-nine Welty-shaped lines by eye mitigates that without eliminating it. The harder control is the manuscript assessments themselves, on film, where four Welty-bearing images remain unread — one of them, in the 1784/85 tail, reading “Willy Michael.”

And the assessor’s own habit points the same way

On the 1781 class leaf the assessor wrote “Philip Welte” and, further down, “Philip Welte Jun.” When two men of one name stood in one township, this clerk marked it — and the church clerks of the same community had the same habit, writing “(smith)” and “(of Geo)” beside duplicate names. “Michel Welte” carries no mark of any kind. That is weak evidence and it is real: the man writing the list appears to have known only one.

A neighbour who could easily become a phantom

Written down here so that nobody has to re-derive it: a Michael William — Wilhelm — farms directly beside the Dover Weltys in print, sixty acres in 1781 immediately after Philip Welty’s hundred and ten, forty acres in 1782 immediately after Philip’s eighty and Jacob’s eighty. His line is badly garbled in the scanned text layer. One tired pass turns Michael Wilhelm into a second Michael Welty, and he is the reason the whole population had to be read by eye rather than by machine alone.

The printed roll is not an alphabet — but it is not the class list’s roll either

Yesterday evening’s entry held one detail deliberately at arm’s length: the five Welte men on the 1781 class list sit between two Rothrauffs, and the Michael of Dover Township married a Christina Ruthrauff three years later. Whether such lists were ordered by neighbourhood had never been tested.

Half of it is now tested, and the half that answered went the useful way. The printed returns are demonstrably not alphabetized — the 1779 Dover run prints Wilte Paul, Wilte John, Welty Philip, Welty George in that order, and 1780 sets “Welty, Widow, 80” beside George — so the print preserves some order taken off a roll. But it is not the class list’s order. In the 1781 Dover print John Rothrauf stands roughly four hundred entries away from the Weltys; on the class leaf he is four lines from Michel Welte. The two documents were not copied one from the other, and the same disagreement shows in a Ramsey trio printed Alexander, Martha, Oliver and classed Oliver, Alexander, Martha. The ordering question can now only be settled at the manuscript rolls, and the adjacency stays at no weight until it is.

One name does move, a little

In the 1783 Dover list of single freemen, Peter Messerly stands two lines from Michael Welty, with an Abraham Messerly beside him. The following year Peter Messerly married a Christina Welty. This is the first record putting the Messerly household on Dover ground, and it strengthens — without proving — the reading that the Christina who married him was a daughter of the Dover Weltys.

Two more lines off the same roll, carried at no weight. In 1780 Dover prints “Wallick, John, 80 / Welty, Philip, 100 / Wallick, Philip, 80” in a run, which gives a Welty–Wallick marriage known here only from family tradition a candidate family on Dover ground for the first time. And Heidelberg’s 1780 return carries “Welty, Jacob” and “Welty, John, 150” adjacent — a line an earlier tally of that township does not include. Manheim adjoins Heidelberg and its own John Welty was assessed at about a hundred and fifty acres two years later, so the two may yet be one man sitting on a township line. Nothing is merged.

3 August 2026 — night: the plot is on the map, and the grave is not
An atlas that was free the whole time, and a plot finally run

A method for locating a Tuscarawas County parcel was written into this log a month ago and never executed, because nobody had found the maps. They were free the whole time: the Ohio History Connection publishes the complete 1875 Everts landowner atlas of Tuscarawas County as an eighty-nine-page scan — no login, no order, no library visit. That is the third time this summer that a class of record queued here as a paid errand or an on-site trip has turned out to be digitised and open.

The parcel is the fifty acres a 1925 family history describes being sold in 1834: the west half of lot 9, fourth quarter, township 8, range 3. The atlas prints the lot numbering, and it agrees with the arithmetic derived from the 1800 statute — each four-thousand-acre quarter drawn five columns by eight rows, numbered boustrophedon from a lot 1 in the south-east corner. Lot 9 is therefore the northernmost lot of the second column from the east. In 1875 it belongs to a J. Hawk, and the reading checks itself twice: four hundred and forty yards by five hundred and fifty is fifty acres, the acreage the deed states, and the printed lot acreages on the plate read 100 throughout.

And the answer is a zero. There is no cemetery, no church and no school symbol in lot 9 or in any lot touching it. The lot carries a single dwelling square with an orchard hatch beside it, both in its north-east corner. The western half — the fifty acres the deed describes — is completely blank.

The limit of that has to be stated with it. A landowner atlas is not a cemetery inventory. Everts mapped dwellings, churches, schools and mills because subscribers paid for them, and an unmarked family burying ground on a farm is precisely what it does not print. The blank measures the atlas, not the ground. The cheapest thing that would reopen it is the USGS fifteen-minute topographic quadrangles for New Philadelphia and Dover, which do print cemeteries and schools by name, are free, and survive in several editions across the twentieth century.

A correction, and it runs against this project’s own reasoning

The same plates knocked out a conclusion drawn here three weeks ago. Dover Township is not township 8, range 3. The Everts plate says so in its own title cartouche — “Map of Dover Township — T. No. 9, R. No. 3” — and a tract in township 8, range 3 is in York Township, about three miles west and a mile and a half south of New Philadelphia.

So the tidy consequence drawn from that arithmetic — that the 1834 tract, the estate farm and the graveyard of family tradition all sat inside one five-mile square, and the search area collapsed to a single quarter — does not survive. Three places that had been handled as one neighbourhood are three places. And the two Crooked Runs in this log disagree with each other, which is the real problem underneath: the only stream the atlas labels Crooked Run lies in the far south-east of Goshen Township, draining to the river from the east — the opposite bank from the 1897 letter’s “opens to the Tuscarawas River from the west” — while a gazetteer coordinate carried here for a Crooked Run cemetery sits five miles due west of New Philadelphia, which is the York Township direction and is consistent with the deed.

Two readings survive and neither is preferred here: either the legal description is exact and the 1925 gloss “near New Philadelphia” is loose, or the gloss is right and “township 8, range 3” was miscopied somewhere between the deed book and the printed page. The deed itself — Tuscarawas Deed Volume 8, page 543 — states its own township, and reading it decides the question outright. The arithmetic was never wrong. It was attached to the township everyone already believed in, because no map carrying the numbers had been opened.

Blicktown is on no plate, and the reason matters more than the absence

The 1897 letter puts the burying ground “a mile west of New Philadelphia, at Blicktown.” Three whole township plates — Goshen, Dover and York — were swept tile by tile. Blicktown does not appear. Neither does Blick. Neither does Pleasant Hill.

The useful part is structural. At New Philadelphia’s latitude the Goshen plate does not map the west bank at all — the township boundary follows the river, and everything beyond it is left white, carrying only the italic words “North West Quarter.” A burying ground a mile due west of the town could not have appeared there, and its absence there measures nothing. The plates that do cover that ground are Dover’s south-east corner and York’s north-east.

Two things on the Dover plate are the right shape and are named by nobody: a church symbol, a building with a cross, standing within J. Knisley’s hundred and fifty-five acres a mile or two west-south-west of the town; and a west-bank hamlet at Dover’s extreme south-eastern tip, between the canal and the river at the mouth of a small run, whose printed labels include “Sch House” and “S. Mill.” A schoolhouse on the west bank at the mouth of a run is the shape the letter describes. It is a shape, and not an identification.

One Welty farm on three plates — and a name that must not be allowed to harden

Exactly one Welty parcel appears on any of the three plates: “Jacob Welty Jr. — 156 acres,” west-central Dover Township, with John Glass to the north, G. D. Stauffer to the east, and a United Brethren church, steeple and cross, standing at the corner where his line meets two others. That image-verifies at the plate a farm this log has known only from description, and it puts a denomination on the ground an 1875 county history described when it said that Stoufer and Welty were Methodists — the Stauffer farm being the one next east.

The farm sits under a standing firewall: it is Mennonite-cluster ground on this project’s own earlier reading, and no identification is claimed in either direction. And then the thing that has to be said out loud rather than left to be noticed later. The neighbour adjoining it on the south is J. Blickensderfer — the only Blick‑ name on any of the three plates — and the letter’s place-name is Blicktown. No weight is put on that, and the reasons are written down so that a later reading cannot quietly drop them: the letter puts Blicktown a mile west of New Philadelphia and this corner is several miles west-north-west, in a different township and a different survey tier; Blicktown is printed nowhere on any plate; and Blickensderfer is a numerous Tuscarawas surname whose appearance beside a Welty needs no explanation at all. Before that adjacency is allowed to become anything, Blicktown has to be located in a source that names it.

And the orphans’ court turns out to be free, filmed, and indexed

The record class named at the end of yesterday evening’s entry now has a route. York County’s orphans’ court dockets run from 1749 — the year the county was erected — through 1881, with a general index to 1887, and they are digitised across twenty-four films. The window that matters here, 1755 to 1775, sits wholly inside that run, and so does every pre-Revolutionary year back to the county’s first day.

The cheapest first pass is not the film. A printed every-name index to Orphans’ Court Book A, 1749–1762, is itself digitised: thirteen years, one book, one sitting, page-turnable and free. Behind it stand the York County Archives, which hold the dockets to 1973 and, as a separate series, the underlying papers to 1976 — the guardian petitions, bonds and accounts, indexed by the deceased’s name — and the county history center’s estate record cards for 1749–1850. Guardianships state parentage, which is exactly what is wanted.

A second lever fell out of the same search, and it is not York at all. York County was erected out of Lancaster in 1749, so every event on this ground before that October is a Lancaster event — and Lancaster County publishes its own orphans’ court index, 1742 to 1800, free on the county website, covering guardian appointments and post-mortem property sales. This log has never swept it. No name has been searched in any of this yet; what is established is that the class exists, that it begins in the right year, and that nobody needs to pay for it.

Six of this project’s own entries are marked down

And one piece of housekeeping that is really a finding. This log has been grading a single record class in two incompatible ways: six entries made in June and July name the subject of the Dover tax and militia assessments of 1779–1783 outright, while the August entries on the very same assessments grade the identity as unassigned. Rather than raise the newer entries or paper over the split with a note, the six older ones have been marked down to the newer and more defensible standard, each with a written statement of what a discriminator would have to look like.

Nothing is withdrawn at the page. A Michael Welty is a single freeman on the Dover returns of 1779, 1780, 1781 and 1783 and a forty-four-acre householder in 1782, and the 1783 septennial census does say Dover. What changes is only the sentence saying which Michael that is. Restated honestly: a Michael Welty appears on the Dover assessments every year from 1779 to 1783; this log holds no second Michael in Dover in those years; and it holds no record stating which Michael this one is. The identification with the Michael of Dover Township this project follows is its best reading, and it is not a documented fact.

The knock-on is written down rather than swallowed. That run of single-man entries is one of two legs under the conclusion that Christina Ruthrauff was the first wife. The other leg is the marriage register itself, and the children who follow it — a record naming the man and his wife — so the conclusion stands; but the tax run may no longer be quoted as independent corroboration of it without saying that its subject is unassigned. Several records already queued here would re-grade all six back up. And the opposite reopener, the one this project keeps forgetting to write: if a second Michael Welty is ever found in or near Dover in those years, the caution stops being a caution and becomes a live problem. Nobody had ever looked.

3 August 2026 — evening: the township on the class list
The five Weltes of 1781 were in Dover all along

This project has carried, for most of a year, a York County militia class list of 1781 that names five Welte men in one block — and a worry attached to it. The sheet filmed immediately before it is a sheriff’s warrant against Peter Fried, “appointed Collector for the third Class of Hidlebey [Heidelberg] Township,” who “refuseth and neglect” to collect. If that warrant governed the list, the five men were Heidelberg men, and a township this project had never placed them in was suddenly holding them.

Read as a packet, the two documents come apart. The warrant is complete in itself — its own docket and the sheriff’s return sit on the left‑hand leaf of the next exposure, and the class list simply begins on that same leaf’s right‑hand page. Two documents, one leaf, no relation. And the heading that made them look like one was misread: it says “Class No 1,” not “Class No 3” — and “Class No 3” was precisely what made the list look like the class the warrant names. The document runs Class 1 through Class 7 across four exposures and closes with the printed January 1781 commissioners’ circular and a manuscript classing order to a Mr George Schettel, whose numbered list reproduces Class No 1 name for name.

The control is independent, printed, and free: the Pennsylvania Archives, Third Series, Vol. XXI, which prints York’s returns of taxables for Dover, Manchester and Heidelberg alike. Matched on surnames distinctive enough to discriminate, the manuscript answers to Dover 13 times out of 15; Heidelberg and Manchester five each. Across about eighty‑two names it is Dover 49, Heidelberg 28, Manchester 27 — and Dover’s lead is not an artefact of one printed list being longer than another. The decider is a single name. The manuscript’s Ramsey trio — Oliver, Alexander and Martha — answers to Dover 1781 line for line, and Martha Ramsey appears nowhere else in the volume: no other township, no other year. The Weltes corroborate it themselves. The list’s two Philips are Dover 1781’s two Philips, of 110 and 100 acres; the township’s single men that year are the Jacob and the Michael this log already holds. Manchester that year carries one Welty and he is a Jacob; Heidelberg carries no Welty of any spelling in 1782 or 1783.

The township is settled. The man is not. A class assessment records no age, no trade and no disambiguator, so the Michel Welte on this list is a Dover single man in the same township, year and record class as the Michael of Dover Township this project follows — which makes him a candidate and not a proof, and he is entered that way.

A detail held deliberately at arm’s length

In Class No 3 the five Welte lines sit between two Rothrauffs — one above them, a John Ruthrauf four lines below. Three years later the Michael of Dover Township married Christina Ruthrauff. It is exactly the kind of adjacency that reads like a finding and may be nothing at all: whether these class lists were ordered by neighbourhood or copied off a roll already sorted some other way has never been tested, and a York tax list of 1787 that once looked adjacency‑rich turned out to be alphabetized. The observation is on the record and no weight is put on it until the ordering itself is tested.

And a birth year comes off the page

The Michael of Dover Township has long been carried as born about 1757 in Chester County. That figure comes from a compiled pedigree, and the only primary age statement this project holds contradicts it: the 1800 census bracket puts the head of his household at 45 or over, with the 26–44 column empty — born by about 1755. The public pages were corrected two days ago; the research log itself still read 1757 until tonight, and both now carry the pedigree‑derived flag on the birth and on the filiation, which no record anywhere states.

There is no birth or baptism record for him in any register this project has opened, and his first appearance in any record at all is a Dover single men’s list of 1779 — twenty‑two blank years in front of it. One class of record aims straight at that blank and has never been swept anywhere in this log: York County’s orphans’ court and guardianship dockets, 1755–1775. Guardianships state parentage. That is the next thing to be done.

3 August 2026 — afternoon: four words under the glass, and a child on the next leaf
Three readings withdrawn, one restored, and the manuscript settles all four

The York Reformed register survives in two forms — the original German hand and a 1935 typescript — and this week the two came to disagree on four one‑word readings, each of them a surname or a date that puts a household on or off this project’s spelling ladder. Rather than count readers, each word was taken back to the manuscript and magnified beside a control letterform written by the same clerk, usually four words away, usually inside somebody else’s surname.

Three went against this project. The groom of a marriage of 29 February 1796, published on this page as a ladder name, opens with a two‑ascender ligature carrying a descender well below the baseline. This clerk’s capital W — in WAABIN, WABB, WELSCHIN and WELD, all within three leaves — is a rounded doubled loop that sits on the baseline and carries no descender at all. His “Sch‑” ligature, in SCHMALL and SCHWERM on the same leaf as the target, is precisely the descender‑bearing form. The 1796 groom is a Sch‑ name, and the reading is withdrawn.

The bride of 24 September 1781, read here as a Welt‑ form, runs W‑e‑l‑[sch]‑i‑n: the fourth element is that same long‑s ligature dropping below the line, and not the “d” of WELD two leaves later, which closes above it. She is Catharina Welschin — Welsch, plus this clerk’s feminine ending — and she belongs to a Welsch family resident at this charge and now documented across half a century. The 1935 transcriber had her right; the groom’s surname, a David whose name begins with C in both readings, confirms it is the same event.

One went the other way, and it is the more interesting. A marriage the typescript gives as “Peter BECKER — Anna Maria WOLF, 20 September 1787” is, in the hand, “Peter Becker & AnnaMaria WELD.” There is no round “o” and no descending “f” in the word. The day is wrong as well: the date brace over the entry reads 10, not 20 — this clerk’s “1” is an angular caret with a short tail, his “2” a rounded top over a swept base, and four dates within six lines of the target supply both forms for comparison. A woman of these names, marrying at this charge on 10 September 1787, is back on the record after two days off it.

And the date the family has always used turns out to be the register’s own

Two Welty weddings were celebrated at this charge on one day in May 1785 — a Jacob marrying an Anna Maria, and a Catharina marrying a Jacob Boehm. A reading made here at page magnification split them, putting the first on the 1st of the month and the second on the 10th, and a correction was published on that basis two days ago. Under word magnification, with the clerk’s own “d. 12t aprill” two lines above as the control, both lines read 10. The second glyph in the 1785 dates is a closed oval; the 12’s is an open figure with a swept base. The first glyph is the same caret in all three.

So both weddings are 10 May 1785, the correction is withdrawn, and the tree on this site has been rebuilt tonight to say so. A third wedding follows them on the same leaf on the same day: 10 May 1785 was a multiple‑wedding day at this church. And the “10 May” that has floated through this family’s compiled records for generations, attached to a marriage whose real date is 18 May 1784, is no longer a mystery date at all — it is a real date belonging to real weddings, copied onto the wrong couple somewhere downstream.

A four‑year silence that belongs to the church, not to the transcriber

The typescript carries no marriage at this charge between 23 August 1789 and 29 October 1793 — fifty months. That could easily have been a transcriber skipping leaves, and a transcript gap and a register gap are very different facts. It is not a transcript gap. The manuscript runs its 1789 block to its tenth and last entry, Heinrich Wehler and Maria Magdalena on 23 August, then a heavy horizontal rule, then a heading reading “Copulirt 1793.” The leaves either side run continuously, so nothing is lost.

This congregation recorded no marriages at all for roughly four years — and those four years sit squarely across the death window of Philip Jacob Welty, who died about 1788–90. Which is to say: if his widow remarried, this register could not have recorded it, and her absence from it means nothing. A negative that had been quietly doing work in this project’s reasoning has been taken out of service.

The English baptism register ends where its clerk says it does — and on the first dark leaf of the book that follows, a child

An English‑language “Record of Baptisms” in the same volume was estimated to run some fifty‑five leaves, and the estimate drove where this project expected to find things. It runs twelve. Its last page closes in the clerk’s own hand: “(Ministry ceased April 4th 1825).” The section is now read in full, 27 February 1821 to 3 April 1825, about 260 baptisms with parents and sponsors both. What takes over on the facing page is a ruled German table headed Kinder | Eltern | Tauffzeugen — a sponsor column on every single entry, which is the one thing the earlier section did not reliably carry.

On the first leaf of that table nobody had opened, under the year heading 1828, third row down:

“Elisabeth geb. 10 May 1828” — parents “Henry W[E]TTY & Catharina” — the baptism date “July 20th” interlined above the cell — and in the sponsor column, “Parentes.” The parents stood for their own child, as they do for roughly half this register’s entries.

A Henry of this charge married a Catharine there on 18 October 1827. Here, at the same charge, is a Henry whose wife is Catharina, baptising a first child born six months and three weeks after that wedding. It is the first baptism of a family of these names anywhere in this register’s nineteenth century. It is also, plainly, an inference: the leaf gives no maiden name, no age and no residence, and nothing here is merged with anything.

And the surname is a problem worth stating rather than smoothing. The hand spells this household WETTY — twice now, in two different sections of the same book, both read at maximum magnification. The 1935 transcriber read WELTY, twice. This page adopts neither spelling and quotes both.

The census test that baptism asked for, and it passes

The find above named its own check: a household of that shape should appear in the 1830 census. Three wildcard passes across Pennsylvania, read in full, return exactly one candidate in the county — “Henry Weldy,” Manchester Township, York County: one male 20–29, one female 20–29, one female under five, three persons. There is no Henry of any other rung of the ladder in the county at all. That is precisely the household the July 1828 baptism predicts, two years on, with the daughter aged two. The identification moves from an inference to an inference corroborated by an independent class of record.

Two limits, both recorded. The census names nobody but the head — there is no Catharina here and no Elisabeth, only a female of the right age in the right place, so it corroborates a household shape and can never do more. And the township is Manchester, which is the ground of a genetically distinct Weldy line this project keeps firewalled; two more Weldy households sit on the same census page as this one. That is a real complication rather than a contradiction — West Manchester is a named preaching point of this very charge — but it is exactly why nothing is merged in either direction. Meanwhile the enumerator writes the name WELDY, with a clear looped “l” and a “d”: a third reader, a third spelling, and this page still adopts none of them.

The same passes place five households of the name in the county that year, and one of them answers a standing question. A Frederick Weldy of Monahan Township has no adult woman in his house at any age and no child under five, and the three girls in it were all born before 1825 — so he cannot be the Frederick who married a Mary Miller at York in January of that year. That Frederick, on this evidence, was not in York County in 1830 at all, which is a far better explanation of eleven childless years in the church register than a thin register would be.

Two silences, measured rather than assumed

The sponsor table was then read on from that leaf — twelve exposures, nine distinct openings, about 210 baptisms, births from 1826 to March 1836, every line, all three columns, on the full spelling ladder and on the tail of each name as well as its head. Zero. So the household above has no second child at this charge; the January 1825 couple have no child here in eleven years of marriage; and a Philip who married in 1812 has none in this run either. Three measurements, not three assumptions — and one of them now has the census explanation above. The reading stopped at the twelfth exposure; the leaves past it are still dark.

And in Tioga County, a second silence closes for a reason worth knowing. A household of these names appears in a Lutheran register there as a communicant without ever being baptised, married or confirmed at that church — the profile of someone who arrived holding a transfer certificate from another congregation, which would name where they came from. The congregation’s own constitution, adopted 30 May 1847, closes that road: at this church confirmation itself is the mechanism of reception into membership. There is no separate register of members received on certificate, and there never was one to find. The same eight leaves, however, gave something better than the thing being looked for — a burial register that writes each person’s birthplace by township and county.

3 August 2026 — midday: the family’s own marriage, in a third hand
The 1780 marriage, read by a third person, and the bride’s name holds

The marriage of Philip Jacob Welty and Anna Maria Wild, 20 June 1780, is one of the load‑bearing records of this project. It has been read twice: once at the original manuscript, and once by a subscription database’s indexer, who read the groom’s surname as UTLEY. The 1935 typescript is a third reader, working from the same book ninety years before us, and printed page 311 gives it plainly:

“Je 20 1780 — Philip Jacob WELTY - Anna Maria WILD.”

Groom and bride both. This matters most for the bride, because an open question here was whether her surname would survive a second reading at all — and if it did not, a whole line of enquiry into her family was chasing a mis‑transcription. It survives. Wild stands, and the Philadelphia arrival inventory being read for her people is the right one. The same block of leaves carries a John Wilt marrying at this charge in April 1779, and — fourteen months before the Michael of Dover Township married Christina Ruthrauff — an Elizabeth Rohtrauf marrying out of it, in March 1783.

Two brides who have never had surnames finally have one each

Printed page 313, two consecutive lines:

“My 10 1785 — Jacob WELTY - Anna Maria MILLER”
“My 10 1785 — Jacob BOEHM - Catharine WELTY”

This register holds two marriages of a Jacob Welty to an Anna Maria, and neither bride carried a surname in anything this project held — which meant that four baptisms recorded for “Jacob Welty and Anna Maria” between 1784 and 1791 could not be assigned to either household. The 1775 bride was named a day earlier as a Schramm. This names the 1785 bride: Miller. Both households can now be told apart for the first time. It does not yet say which of them fathered the four children; that needs the baptismal leaves themselves.

It also opened a disagreement about the day of the month, which was settled at the manuscript a few hours later — see the afternoon entry above.

A fifty‑month hole in the marriage register

Reading the typed marriage column leaf by leaf turned up something the eye only catches when the leaves are consecutive: no marriage at all between August 1789 and October 1793. The register runs continuously either side of it. That gap sits on the death window of Philip Jacob Welty, which makes it worth knowing whether it is the transcriber’s or the church’s. It was tested the same afternoon; the answer is above.

A register read for a family and honest about what it found

The York volume’s English “Record of Baptisms” was read entry by entry, February 1821 to January 1825, parents and sponsors both — roughly 260 baptisms. In all of it, one name touches this project’s spelling ladder: “May 31 [1823] — Elizabeth WILDY, a married woman”, an adult baptism, of the sort this clerk records without parents.

And the same section immediately undercuts the temptation. Two leaves apart, the clerk writes one household two ways: “Parents John WILD & Julianna his wife” in 1821, and “Parents John WILDT & Julianna his wife” in 1823 — same names, same wife, two years apart. Wild, Wildt and Wildy are one range in this hand, belonging to a Wild family resident in the congregation. Reading Elizabeth Wildy as a Welty woman would be reading against the clerk’s own demonstrated spread. She is not entered as one. She is on the record because she is the only name in 260 that comes near, and because someone should know that she was looked at and set aside.

3 August 2026 — morning: a century of marriages in one typed column, and a hypothesis that loses a leg
A marriage register nobody knew was there, and it runs a hundred years

The York Reformed register survives in a 1935 typescript as well as in the original German hand, and this site triaged the typescript’s reel yesterday from thumbnails. That triage labelled images 264 onward “later nineteenth‑century congregational records.” Opened, they are nothing of the kind. Images ~264 to ~325 carry one continuous typed marriage list — date, groom, bride, one line each — running from at least 1774 to at least 1830. Roughly sixty leaves. Six have been read. It is now the largest legible unread body of record this project knows about.

Two readings this page published a day ago with caveats attached can now have the caveats taken off. Printed page 340: “Ja 6 1825 — Frederick WELDY — Mary MILLER.” This section gives every marriage its own date on its own line, so the competing “13 January” that could not be excluded from the manuscript belongs to the couple below — “Ja 13 1825 — Adam FREE — Mary HAKE.” And page 341: “O 18 1827 — Henry WELTY — Catharine COLEMAN” — Welty, not Wetty, in the transcriber’s reading of a stem this site called thin. The bride is Catharine; a compiled pedigree this project holds for a Henry born about 1803 gives her as “Mary Catharine,” and the leaf does not support the first name. The two records are read together and are not merged.

And a marriage this page published two nights ago is now contested — by two other readers of the same line

Printed page 316, first entry under 1796: “F 29 1796 — John SCHACKY — Catharine SENGER.” Same date, same position on the leaf, same bride as the manuscript line published here two nights ago. A different groom.

So there are now three independent readings of one groom’s surname, and they are three different surnames. At the manuscript, this project read Wa[l]dÿ. The transcriber in 1935 typed Schacky. The subscription database’s own indexer of the manuscript read Sperry. Only the reading made here is a spelling on this family’s ladder, and one reader in three is not a record. The 1796 marriage is withdrawn from the list of marriages of these names — it is not disproved either, and it stays in the log with both readings recorded. What would settle it is one load: the manuscript leaf at maximum magnification, on the groom’s initial and stem, now that two rival shapes exist to compare against.

Three things survive it, and they are worth the loss. The month is February, not the March the index gives — 1796 was a leap year and the leaf says the 29th. The bride is Senger, which confirms that the manuscript’s “S[i]ngerin” is the clerk’s feminine ending on a surname and not part of it, exactly as it was read here. And the typescript carries this marriage once — so the struck‑through duplicate flagged on this page two nights ago, and the phantom second marriage it might have bred in an index, cannot come through this transcript.

Three marriages of these names that this project has never held

All three read at the image, none attached to anybody, all in the same typed run. “Mr 17 1812 — Philip WELTE — Catharine KNAB” (page 328) — Welte is the spelling under which the largest historical‑society index files the York Manchester family. “My 15 1775 — Jacob WELTY — Anna Maria SCHRAMM” (page 309), which does real work: this register holds two marriages of a Jacob to an Anna Maria, 1775 and 1785, and the open question published here two nights ago was which of them fathered the four children baptized 1784–91. The earlier bride now has a maiden surname, and the 1785 entry sits a few unopened leaves back. And “Mr 26 1775 — George GAUF — Elizabeth WELTY” (page 309) — the earliest of the women of this name marrying out, into a surname already standing in this corridor.

Two more things came off the same leaves. A measured gap: no marriage is transcribed between 19 July 1825 and 22 April 1827, twenty‑one months in the middle of the window that matters most, and the manuscript leaves that would fill it are named in the log. And a Wild family is at this charge after all, spelled Wildt and Wilt — five marriages between 1806 and 1827. Not one is a Jacob or a George, and all of them are a generation after the 1780 marriage of Philip Jacob Welty to Anna Maria Wild. Nothing merged. The 1780 marriage itself should be sitting in this same typed run, ten or so images earlier, unopened.

The absent‑wife hypothesis loses one of its two legs, and the fault was this project’s own

This page published, two nights ago, a reading of two silences in the Somerset register: Michael Welty stands sole sponsor at a baptism of June 1786, and communes alone at Easter 1790, with no woman of his surname on either leaf — offered as a hypothesis that his household stayed at York while he was west of the mountains. The sponsorship half rested on a sentence: “this register writes a sponsoring couple as X and wife.”

That convention was measured in a different congregation’s book, in another county, by another clerk. Counted in this book, on the very leaf the entry sits on — eighteen baptisms — there are five sponsoring couples, seven single sponsors, and six entries naming nobody at all. When a couple sponsors here, the clerk names the woman: “Peter Friedlein & Catharina,” “Heinrich Stahl & Rosina.” The phrase “and wife” does not occur in the register. A man appearing alone in that column is the commonest thing on the page. Michael standing alone carries no information about whether he had a wife, and that leg of the hypothesis is withdrawn.

The test this page called the cheapest decisive thing in the log — the Easter 1809 communicant list on the next leaf — was opened. Ninety‑seven numbered slots, two columns, households written as a named man followed by “& wife” or as two named people in sequence. No woman of this name in any spelling; no man of it either. But the transcriber skipped numbers 62 to 93 — thirty‑two of the ninety‑seven slots are not in the transcript. So it neither confirms the reading nor kills it, and the reopener is the original 1797 church book, which is unlocated.

What the hypothesis rests on now is one Easter and one leaf — the 1790 communicant list — and a communicant roll leaves women off for reasons that have nothing to do with where they live. It is weaker than when it was raised here, still open, still not adopted, and nothing on the tree stands on it. The tests that would settle it are direct rather than silent, and none has been run: the Somerset County tax lists of 1786 to 1791 first among them.

One correction of the kind worth printing: the man is written Wöldy at the 1786 baptism and Wäldy on the 1790 list — one man, two different umlauts, one book, four years apart, each glossed by the transcriber as “(Welty).” A search spelled without the umlaut finds neither.

The York register does have burials — and its clerk names which church

This site said two nights ago that the York volume’s only death register opens in June 1844. That sentence is wrong and is corrected here. In the reel’s other browse node, the typescript carries a bounded burial section headed “DEATHS or BURIALS — (Page 21 of oldest register)”, opening 31 December 1762: eight entries to March 1763, each giving age to the day and several naming kin — Killian Schmid, aged 69 years 10 months 2 days; Margaretha Hack, infant daughter of Dominicus Hack; Maria Barbara Schultz nee Amment, wife of Jacob Schultz of “Crays Grigg.” Then, under the transcriber’s own rubric “from a copy of certain unidentified records,” a run beginning in 1821.

The conclusion that sentence was supporting survives, in better shape. There is still no burial coverage for the late 1780s, when Philip Jacob Welty died. The difference is that this is now a measured gap in a book that can be read, not an absence inferred from a reel map — and it has a named reopener: page 21 of the oldest register, and the leaves around it, which the transcript proves exist and which nobody has looked at.

On one leaf of that section: “Ag 1 1822 — Jacob WELDTY, adult. (Christ Church)” — the first burial of any man of these names anywhere in the York corpus. Nothing attaches to him, and he sits inside the run whose source the transcriber himself could not identify.

And the parenthesis after his name is the bigger thing. This clerk writes the preaching point on the line: (Wolf’s Church), (Christ Church), (Quickel’s Church), or nothing at all for the mother church at York. None of those three is on the four‑point list this project has been using for the charge — and Quickel’s own film is restricted to viewing inside a FamilySearch centre, while this volume, which anyone with the subscription can open, records Quickel’s burials by name. Nine leaves of that section are still unread.

The book says on its own first page that it covers more congregations than we have been naming

The second leaf of the typescript is the register’s own title page, dated 1754, and it describes its contents in one clause that changes how every York entry on this site should be read: the baptisms are of children “welche … in diesen, u. denen nächst umliegenten reformirten Gemeinden getauft worden sind” — in these, and the next surrounding Reformed congregations. This site has said for a week that a York entry carries a four‑way uncertainty about where the event happened. It is not four‑way and closed. It is four‑way and open‑ended, and the book said so on page one — which is exactly what the burial parentheses show from the other end of the volume.

The same page gives the four points in the clerk’s own spelling — Jork, Croisses Creek, Catores, Pramothien — and a 1763 burial names a man of “Crays Grigg.” A search built on modern place spellings walks past all of them. It also announces the sponsor column in so many words, “die Kinder mit Eltern und Tauffzeugen,” eighteen years before the earliest baptism this project has read out of it. One conflict is flagged and not resolved: the leaf signs itself “Catores 13 April 1754,” while this log has carried the charge as beginning 13 April 1744. One digit, in a transcriber’s reading of another man’s hand; the manuscript leaf settles it and has not been examined.

And a leaf this project’s own triage called a section divider is a second title page: word for word the first one, with “VOLUME III” in place of “VOLUME I.” So the reel carries the first and third volumes of the 1935 transcript, and the missing Volume II is a numbered part of that transcript rather than a lost successor register out in the world. Its baptisms of the 1870s run in three columns with no sponsor column, unlike the first volume’s four — which is why the 1762 burials sit in the late volume as back‑matter.

A second congregation in Tioga County, and a fifth child for the household found there

The reel segment that produced the Tioga household published here two nights ago holds a fourth filmed item, and it is a different church: “Zion Evangelical Lutheran Church, Sebring, Tioga County, Pennsylvania — parish registers, 1836–1870.” Two Lutheran congregations in one county, filmed back to back, under a browse label that names neither of them. Its surname index has never been read by anybody; read whole, letter by letter, it holds one W headword — “Welte: 4” — and no V, U or A section at all.

Page 4: “Friedrich WELTE / Marianna nee Miller”, son Wilhelm Konrad, born 7 March 1838, sponsors Konrad Gundlach and wife. That is a fifth child for the Friedrich Welte household — landing in the one four‑year gap its Salem baptisms leave — in a second congregation, and it is godfather‑naming again, this family’s documented custom, now a third documented instance. The household is still not merged with the York Frederick Weldy who married a Mary Miller in 1825, and the test is still the 1850 census of that county, which now has a five‑child household to check against.

One caution for anyone searching that database by county. It files these Tioga baptisms under a place in Somerset County, two hundred miles away — the browse label of the first item on the reel, applied to a different congregation in a different county. And its place filter reaches only about a third of the records in a result set: filtering by county silently discards the rest. A negative taken from that filter is not a negative.

A record class that says where people came from

One more leaf of the York typescript, printed page 299: “Members received into the German Reformed Congregation on CERTIFICATE” — and every entry names the body the person came from: the Presbyterian church at Sinking Valley in Huntingdon County, a Methodist church in Baltimore, the German Reformed congregations of Lancaster, Chambersburg and Frederick City in Maryland. One names where a woman went to: “dismissed at her request to the German Reformed Congregation at Gettysburg.”

No name from this family is on the leaf. It is recorded because of what the class of record is. This project’s standing difficulty is people appearing in one county and vanishing from another with no document connecting the two — and a certificate of transfer is the ordinary church record that states both ends. The three Weltys received as adults at the Tioga church in May 1857 came from somewhere; this is the kind of book that would say where.

The morning’s ledger, plainly: two readings settled, three marriages added, one marriage withdrawn from the list it was published on, one death‑register statement corrected, and one hypothesis about an absent wife left standing on half the evidence it had yesterday. Nothing on the tree moved.

2 August 2026 — late: a woman on a passenger list, a book that is six books, and one man under three spellings
One household, one register, three surnames — and the index never points at itself

A film segment catalogued under a Somerset County congregation turns out to carry, as its third filmed item, a typescript titled “Salem Lutheran Church, Hartsfield, Tioga County, Penna. — Parish registers, 1831–1859.” Tioga is at the New York line, a hundred and fifty miles north of every place this project works in. It had been left unopened for three sessions running. It holds this:

Friedrich VELTI and Mary Ann Müller, printed page 9, baptizing George Washington, born 15 July 1833 — and again at page 11, Frederic VELTI and Mary Ann Müller, daughter Hannah, born 30 September 1835. Then page 13: Friedrich WELTE and Maria Anna nee Müller, daughter Sarah Melinda, born 26 July 1841. Then page 16: Fridrich WELDE, wife nee Miller, a daughter born 9 August 1847. Same given name, same wife under her own name in both its German and its English dress, one congregation, sixteen years, a continuous run of children. The volume’s own surname index files that one man under Velti, Welte and Welde on two facing pages and never once cross‑references itself.

What that settles, and exactly how far. Here, Velti is a surname — it stands in the surname position of a three‑column register, behind a distinct given name, attached to a named wife, alternating with Welte and Welde on one household. What it does not settle: the standard ship‑list index glosses Velte as a dialect form of the given name Valentin, and that caution still governs every bare V‑spelling with no household behind it. The rule and its reopener are one sentence — find the household, or leave the spelling unattached.

A second lock on the same pages: Philip J. WELDY at page 17 is Philip J. WELLTY at page 20, same given name, same middle initial, same congregation, five years apart. Wellty has sat on this project’s list of spellings since a Camp Security record and has never before been tied to a Weldy household inside one source. (The transcript reads that middle initial I on one leaf and J on the other; in this typeface the two are near‑identical. One letter of unknown identity, not two men.)

One limit, and it is a real one: this is a typescript, not the hand. The transcriber signs off “Attested by me, Schulz” at the foot of page 11. The alternation is certain in the transcript and probable but unproved in the manuscript — and the original Salem Lutheran register has not been located.

And around him, a whole settlement of these names nobody knew was there

Four more households come out of the same book, none of them attached to anybody on this tree and all of them firewalled from the York and Somerset lines on purpose. John WELTI and Elisabeth Lenhard, a son Calvinus born 2 December 1836 — a Reformed given name in a Lutheran book. Philip J. Weldy’s four children, three of them baptized together on Christmas Day 1852, which is the signature either of a household newly arrived in a congregation or of a congregation newly supplied with a pastor, and neither reading is adopted. Jacob WELTE and his wife Elisabetha standing sponsors on 26 May 1833 at Ulrich Furrer’s daughter Elisabeth — a man who appears in this book only as a godparent, and would be invisible to any search that indexes parents. And on 17 May 1857, William, Sophia and Sarah WELTY confirmed together, Sophia baptized the same day with no parents named and standing as her own sponsor. That is an adult reception: those three came from somewhere else, together, and that somewhere is worth chasing.

One candidate presents itself and is not being taken. A Frederick Weldy married a Mary Miller at York on 6 January 1825 — found the same night, in the entry below this one. The Tioga Friedrich’s wife is Mary Ann Miller, and his first recorded child arrives in July 1833. Given name matches, wife’s surname matches on both halves. It is also exactly the shape of coincidence that has burned this log before, and a Frederick married to a Miller is not a rare event in this population. Not merged, not adopted — and the test is cheap and named: the 1850 Tioga County census, which gives every household member’s age and birthplace, and in which a Frederick with a wife Mary Ann and children born 1833, 1835 and 1841 either exists or does not.

One caution for anyone reading indexed entries off this volume: it is not a single‑point charge. Page 13 records the “beginning of the ministry of the third congregations at Blockhouse on Aug. 24, 1841.” A place stamped onto one of these baptisms is the book’s place, not the entry’s.

A woman on a passenger list, for the first time

The Philadelphia oath lists of 1727 to 1775 record males aged sixteen and over. That is the whole reason no woman of these names has ever been found on an arrival: the record class does not contain women. After the Revolution the rules changed. A register kept from 1785 to 1808, printed in full and free to read, was created by statute to record all German passengers and their indentures — and it does, naming wives, widows, daughters and children, and marking its family groups explicitly. This project had never opened it.

Printed page 28, right‑hand column, between Clara Schneider and Charlotta Faulhaberin: “Cath.a WELDEN” — ship Dorothea, Severus Dalsted master, from Rotterdam, arrived Philadelphia 14 October 1787, sworn before Lewis Farmer, Register. Read at the image. The first woman on this ladder ever found on a Philadelphia arrival list.

And she travelled unattached, which here is evidence rather than an assumption. The list marks its groupings all around her and by name — “Jacob Volkel, his wife & 4 children, Viz.t”, “Martin Kruger & 6 children”, “Maria Marg.a Haubergerin & 2 children” — each followed by its members. She carries no qualifier and falls inside no group. An adult woman crossing in her own right.

Nothing attaches, and the near misses are worth listing so nobody re‑runs them. She is not Philip Jacob’s widow, who was Elizabeth. She is not the Catharina who married at York in September 1781, six years earlier. She is not the Catharina Weltÿ who married Jacob Böhm at York in May 1785, two years earlier. She is not the communicant of 1825. One adjacency is recorded as an observation and expressly not a claim: two names below her stands a Johan Daniel Bohm, and this log has a live Welty–Böhm tie at York — which predates this arrival, in a very common surname.

Why she went unfound for the life of this project is one sentence about the index of that printed volume. WELDEN and WELTE are separate headwords about a hundred and fifty entries apart. Both claim the spelling Welde. Neither points at the other — and the headword this project has always used carries no bearers at all from the volume with the women in it. Anyone checking it would have written the family down as absent from those twenty‑three years.

The negative that came with her, scoped: no other spelling on this ladder appears anywhere in the 1785–1808 register, all hundred and eighty lists, machine‑matched at one letter’s tolerance and checked by eye. That is a measurement of the printed Philadelphia transcript. Baltimore, New York and Charleston for the same years are wholly untouched, and the surviving manuscript register is not this printed text. As for turning Cath.a Welden into a person with a county, the action is obvious and has never been taken: the Philadelphia indenture and redemptioner registers of 1785–1800, the record class this very statute created, and an unattached arriving woman is the likeliest passenger on any ship to have been bound.

The York film is not one book. It is six — and the burial question finally has an answer

The five‑hundred‑image node holding the York Reformed manuscript has been treated here as a volume with an unread back half. It is not a volume. A reel of church records is a stack of books, and the filmer photographed a card in front of each one giving the congregation and the volume’s dates. Read off those cards, the reel is: the 1744 volume, images 2–162, with its marriages at about 137–159 and a death register at 160–161; then a volume of 1811–51 opening with the church’s charter; a bounded 1821–25 volume that is entirely communicant registers; then 1845–51, 1852–86, and 1898–1947; then “END OF ROLL.”

Which answers a question three separate work plans have sent readers hunting for. The 1744 volume’s only death register begins in June 1844 — English, giving age to the day (“Jacob Loucks died January 29 1846 aged 88 years 6 mo & 12 days”), running to March 1846, with no name from this ladder on it. And the back half of the reel holds no eighteenth‑century material at all: five volumes, five cards, earliest span 1811. Philip Jacob Welty died about 1788–90, and his burial is not in this book — which is a fact about what the book contains, not a search that failed. Four things would reopen it, and all four are named: image 161, seen only at thumbnail size; the ninety leaves in the middle of the volume (triaged since — below); the charge’s three other congregations, whose books are unlocated; and the foot of image 159, where the clerk writes in so many words “…in the New Church Book for the above Names.” There is a successor volume, this one names it, and nobody has looked for it.

Walking the marriage leaves produced three ladder marriages this project did not have, all read at the image, none merged. “Johannes Wa[l]dÿ u. Catharina S[i]ngerin — d. 29t Febr.”, first under the 1796 heading — and five lines lower the clerk wrote the same couple and the same date again and struck it through, which corroborates the reading and is also a warning: a struck duplicate is still ink, and an index taken off this leaf could carry a phantom second marriage. “Frederick Weldy and Mary Miller”, 6 January 1825 — the second Welty–Miller marriage in this one register, forty years after the first. And “Henry W[e]tty & Cath. Coleman”, 18 October 1827. Two carry an honest caveat: the 1796 stem letter reads l or d, and the 1827 surname’s stem is thin in an English hand.

Those two later marriages do something for the communicant woman found on this site earlier tonight, whose surname reads Weltÿ or Werty and whose stem letter is genuinely ambiguous. They do not read her letter. They do put a documented household of these names in that congregation in January 1825 and October 1827, on either side of her. That is a prior, not a reading, and she stays unattached.

Ninety leaves opened at last — and a clerk who writes the place in the margin

The ninety leaves in the middle of the 1744 volume, never opened by anyone here, were triaged this evening. Image 46 is 1802, headed “Kind | 1802 | Eltern | Taufzeug” — so the manuscript carries a sponsor column of its own, not only the typescript whose sponsor column turned up earlier tonight. Its facing page records the pastor’s removal to Fredericktown in Maryland, last baptism 17 October 1802, and his successor’s first, 13 May 1804.

And at image 81 a new section opens: “Record of Baptisms”, beginning 27 February 1821, in English, sponsors written inline — and the clerk names the preaching point in the right margin. “Creutz creek” twice, “Cadorus creek”, “Hanover”. This register served four congregations from 1744, which is why every York entry on this site carries a four‑way uncertainty about where the event actually happened. From 1821 the clerk resolves it himself, entry by entry. The section runs to about image 91 — and it covers precisely the window of the two marriages above. No name in it has been read yet; this is thumbnail grade, and so is everything else in those ninety leaves.

One measurement to close the night’s bookkeeping, because it changes what “searched” means here. The Pennsylvania church‑records collection this project leans on was swept for the whole eighteenth century and closed months ago. Its nineteenth century has never been touched. Counted for 1800 to 1849 alone it holds roughly three hundred records under one spelling group, two hundred and eighty under a second and a hundred and thirteen under a third — and standing in that third group is a “George Washington Velti”, who is the eldest child of the Tioga household at the top of this entry. About seven hundred records of the right shape, in fifty years, in the half nobody has swept.

Michael communes alone

Earlier this evening this site published one line off a leaf of the Somerset register: “Communicants at Easter, 1790”, with Michael Wäldy (Welty) sixth of eleven men. Twenty‑five other names sat on that page and none of them was read. It was given a second look this evening. This is what the other twenty‑five are for.
