Match the two columns on surname and every household on the page closes: Fischer with Fischer(in), Weimer with Weimer(in) twice over, Müller with Müller(in) twice, Leonhard with Leonhard(in), Schaaf with Schaaf(in), Lohr with Lohr(in), Hoffer with Hoff(in), Agnee with Rosina Ankeny. What is left over is one man and two women — and neither woman can be his, because this list gives wives under their married surname, so his wife would stand here as Wäldyin or Weltin. There is no woman of that name on the leaf, in any spelling. Michael Welty is the only man on the page with no woman of his surname beside him, and he had been married six years.

It is the second silence in the same book. At the baptism of June 1786 he stands sole sponsor — and this register writes a sponsoring couple as “X and wife” when there is one. Two record types, four years apart, both of which name a wife where a wife is present, and in neither is she.

That gives a tension this site has flagged for weeks its first coherent reading, and it is offered as a hypothesis and not adopted: a household that stayed at York while the man himself was west of the mountains would account for the 1786 sponsorship, the York and Dover tax of 1788 and 1789, the Easter 1790 communion and the 1784 marriage without straining any of the four. It rests on two silences, which is thin. She may never have been received into that congregation; she may have been unable to commune that Easter; she may sit in a list this book does not carry. And a competing reading costs no more — that the tax tension is an artefact of which Michael the assessor meant. Nothing on the tree has been changed on the strength of it.

The test is one page, and it is the cheapest decisive thing in this log tonight: the Easter 1809 communicant list begins on the very next leaf of the same volume, and nobody has opened it. A Welty woman on that page confirms or kills the whole reading in a single load.

2 August 2026 — night: the column nobody could search
A whole column of the register was never indexed — and the godparents were sitting in it the entire time

The York Reformed baptism register exists in two forms in the subscription database this project uses: the original German manuscript, and a twentieth‑century typescript of it. The typescript sets each baptism in four typed columns — Birth & Baptism, Child, Parents, and Sponsors. The database’s index carries the child and the parents.

It does not carry the sponsor column at all. Every godparent in the congregation that married Michael, Philip Jacob, Jacob and John has therefore been invisible to every search this project has ever run — inside a source that was already open, already paid for, and already read for other purposes. Sponsors are the record class that turns surnames into kin, and this is the second congregation in a week where the whole family question turned on them.

Read out, the column gives four sponsor pairs for the household of Jacob Welty and Anna Maria, the first godparents this project has ever held for the York charge: Jost and Catharine Wahl at Johannes, baptized 28 December 1784; Peter and Barbara Schmitt at Johann Peter, 4 June 1786; Johannes and Margaretha Schnell at Heinrich, 1 May 1788; Nicolaus Bohn and Anna Maria at Abraham, 21 September 1791, this last one read in the clerk’s own hand. The second of them is godfather‑naming — the child is Johann Peter and the godfather is Peter — which is this family’s documented custom turning up for the fourth time, and it corrects a reading made here a day earlier from the manuscript at too low a magnification.

Two limits, both stated because they matter. Which Jacob fathered these four children is not decided. This register holds two marriages of a Jacob to an Anna Maria — 1775 and 1785 — and nothing here separates them. And the typescript sets one of the birth dates impossibly, giving two sons born six months apart; the baptism dates are not in doubt, the birth date is, and the leaf that would settle it has not been opened.

And in that column, a Welty standing godfather

Eleven lines below the Abraham entry, on the same leaf, in the same hand, on the same day: “Elisabetha, born 5 March, baptized 21 September. Parents: Johannes Schnell and Anna Margaretha. Sponsors: Jacob Weldy and Elisabetha.”

Every Welty this project has found in this congregation until tonight has been a bride, a groom or a parent — which is to say, has been found in one of the two fields the index carries. This is the first ladder man ever found at York as an adult chosen by another family. And it closes a circle, all three legs of it inside this one book: Jacob Weldy sponsors Johannes Schnell’s daughter in 1791; the Schnells sponsored Jacob Welty’s son in 1788; and Jost and Catharine Wahl, who sponsored Jacob Welty’s son in 1784, sponsored the Schnells’ own son in 1786. Welty, Schnell and Wahl, standing for each other across seven years. That is what kinship looks like in a German congregation before it looks like anything else.

It also produces a problem this entry is not going to paper over. On the same day, in the same hand, the clerk writes the parents as “Jacob Weldy and Anna Maria” and the sponsors as “Jacob Weldy and Elisabetha.” On this register’s own convention that makes one man’s wife two different women on one page. Three readings are open — that the two sponsors are independent people rather than a couple, which German registers do routinely; that there is a second Jacob Weldy household here with a wife Elisabetha; or that the clerk slipped — and none of the three is adopted. The child is named Elisabetha, which is godmother‑naming and argues the woman was real and deliberately chosen rather than a slip of the pen. This log does hold an Elisabetha of this congregation, married in 1775. She is not attached to anything, and she is named here only so the next reader does not have to rediscover that she exists.

A bride gets a surname, a ghost date gets a home, and the “double wedding” turns out to be two weddings

Two consecutive lines of the marriage register, read at four times magnification — the magnification an earlier reading said it would need:

“Jacob Weltÿ & An Maria Millern — d. 1t May”
“Jacob Böhm & Catharina Weltÿ — d. 10t May”

Three things fall out, and one of them corrects a call this project has carried for a month. Jacob’s bride has a family for the first time in the life of this project — Millern is the German feminine of Miller, and she has been simply “Anna Maria” until tonight. Catharina Welty has a groom, and he is a Bohn. And the “1785 double wedding” is not one wedding day: the entries are nine days apart, 1 May and 10 May.

Which quietly settles something else. This site has carried a “10 May” ghost for years — a date attached to Michael and Christina’s marriage that the register itself puts on 18 May 1784, and that nobody could source. The ghost was never invented. It was displaced. There is a real 10 May 1785 Welty marriage in this book, and it belongs to Catharina Welty and Jacob Böhm.

And the groom’s surname opens a household. The same family is written Bohn in this register’s baptism section, where “Jacob Bohn and Catharina” baptize two children on 28 November 1791 — on the same leaf as the sponsor entries above. So Catharina Welty, married in May 1785, can now be read forward, which is a thing this project has almost never been able to do with a Welty woman. Not merged and not asserted: the register never writes her maiden name at those baptisms. But it does something better than confirm — it explains. If a Welty daughter married into the Bohns in 1785, then Nicolaus Bohn standing godfather to Abraham Weldy in 1791 is an in‑law tie rather than a neighbourly one. That is a hypothesis with a test attached, not a conclusion.

The register measured: about 290 baptisms, and two families that are not in it

The baptism section was then read entry by entry, every column, from January 1783 to late 1795 — roughly two hundred and ninety baptisms. Exactly one ladder household appears as parents (Jacob Welty and Anna Maria, above) and exactly one ladder man appears as a sponsor (Jacob Weldy, above). That is the whole ladder presence of thirteen years at this charge.

Two absences are worth more than they look, because both are people this project has watched marry in this very book. Peter Messerle married Christina Welty here in April 1784 and baptized no child here in the eleven years that followed. Peter Becker married Anna Maria Weld here in September 1787 and baptized no child here in the eight years that followed. Neither couple can be run forward through their own children out of this register, which was the obvious next move for both. The reopeners are named: this is the Reformed charge, and the Lutheran congregation at York has its own book; so do Codorus and Bermudian once the four‑point charge divided; and this register’s baptisms run well before 1783 and well after 1795, both ends unread. Michael and Christina, married here in May 1784, likewise baptize nothing here — which is a fact about this register and not about them, and sits comfortably with the household being in Somerset County by June 1786.

A volume was declared small this afternoon, and three hundred and forty leaves came back tonight

Earlier the same day, working out how much of the York manuscript was genuinely unread, this project concluded that the five‑hundred‑image browse node holding it actually runs into the typescript partway through, and that the German manuscript is therefore about a hundred and sixty leaves, not five hundred. The evidence for that was the reel identifiers in the viewer’s own URLs, and the observation about them is correct.

The conclusion is not. A second session opened the same node from a different direction and found that images 163 to 502 do exist — they simply do not carry the identifiers anyone would have guessed, resuming several hundred numbers further on. Roughly three hundred and forty leaves that had just been written off are still there. Four probes, each checked against the viewer’s own page counter and breadcrumb on the same screen as the leaf, map what they are: consistory minutes of 1812, naming the board of elders and a April meeting that appointed a sexton at nine dollars; a communicant roll of 1825; confirmations of 1890–91; and a printed attendance register ruled to 1908–10. The back half of the volume is the congregation’s later administrative books, and it is real and unread.

The method that proved it is worth more than the correction, because it will work anywhere in this viewer: the identifier in the URL is not the only way in. Type a page number into the viewer’s own page box, and it navigates inside the volume and rewrites the URL with the true identifier. Guessing identifiers cannot reach the back half of that book. Nothing about the earlier reading was careless; it was a correct observation carried one step too far, and it was caught within hours by the plain expedient of asking the same question a second way.

One name came out of the probes. In the 1825 communicant roll, fourteenth in a women’s column of about twenty‑eight, between an Elizabeth Small and a widowed Catharine Bernhart, stands “Catharine Weltÿ” — or Werty. The stem letter is genuinely ambiguous, l or r, and Werty is an established lookalike on this ladder with a documented family of its own. So she is not entered as a rung and nothing is merged. What would settle it is cheap and specific: the men’s column on the facing page, and the other communicant rolls in the same unread stretch. And it is worth saying why a communicant roll is a prize at all — it is the one record class that names a woman who signed nothing, owned nothing, and was never taxed. This project has lost most of its Welty women to exactly that gap.

Which leaves the most honest sentence of the night. No burial section was found in this volume — and that is now a statement about four probed leaves inside a three‑hundred‑and‑forty‑leaf stretch, not a statement about the book. Philip Jacob Welty’s death, around 1788–90, has no record anywhere in this project, and it is not ruled out of here. The next slice is images 163 to 200, leaf by leaf, which is where an eighteenth‑century book of the dead would sit if this volume has one.

The line above the passenger list says less than we said it did

Two days ago this site made much of the heading above the 1754 crossing: the clerk at the Philadelphia court house describing the ship Richard & Mary’s passengers as “Inhabitants of the Dukedom of Wirtemberg,” with the three men on this ladder signing immediately below it. That heading has now been tested the only way it should have been tested in the first place: all five hundred and twenty‑three list headings in the printed volume were scored, 1727 to 1775, for whether they name a territory at all.

The field is period‑bound, and that is the finding. Every list from 1731 to 1741 carries a territory clause. Then: none at all in 1742–44 and 1748; eighty‑two per cent of them in 1749; one out of thirteen in 1750, none out of twelve in 1751, none out of fourteen in 1752, none out of eighteen in 1753; eighty‑eight per cent in 1754; and essentially none for the twenty years after. It is a clerical habit that switches on and off by season.

Half of the earlier claim survives and half does not. What stands: within 1754 the clause is not boilerplate — fifteen of seventeen lists that year carry one, they name different territories, and this is still the only 1754 arrival described as Württemberg and nothing else. That comparison is unharmed and remains the best American‑side datum the Württemberg thread has. What does not stand: the idea that a silent heading says anything about its shipload. The Royal Union’s silence in 1750 — Philip Jacob’s own crossing — is one of thirteen silences that year. It is a clerk’s habit, not a statement about any ship, and no split origin should be read out of it in either direction.

The same sweep produced a structural fact that constrains every future attempt at these three years. Strassburger printed three classes of list where they survived — captain’s list, oath list, signature list. For the whole of 1750, 1751 and 1752 — the Royal Union, both Neptunes, the Janet, the Forest, the Snow Ketty — only one of the three survives at all. There is no second exemplar to compare, for any ladder arrival in those years, and there never will be from this book.

The wrong Neptune, and a pair of Weltys nobody had looked for

Two ships named Neptune qualified their passengers at Philadelphia on consecutive days in September 1751, under different captains, and the running head at the top of the printed page says only “Neptune 1751,” which is true of both and identifies neither. This log picked the wrong one. The Johannes Welte it has carried since July is on Captain John Mason’s list of 24 September, not Captain James Wier’s of the 23rd — established by walking back to the bracketed heading itself, which begins at the foot of a page two leaves earlier. There is no ladder name anywhere on Wier’s list.

That matters because a resolution rested on it. This man was identified in July with a Catholic John Welty of Maryland on the reasoning that Wier’s Neptune carried a particular cohort. He was not on Wier’s Neptune. The identification is not disproved — the two ships sailed the same route a day apart — but its stated ground has moved, the cohort that has to be checked is now a different and much larger one, and the man goes back to unassigned. This is the third time a running head in this one volume has mis‑assigned a ship in this log. A running head names the ship a page mostly belongs to. Only the bracketed heading settles a boundary.

And because the whole arrival inventory was rebuilt rather than spot‑checked, something new fell out of it. On the ship Sarah, 20 September 1764, “Johannes Welte” and “Johan Philipp Welde” stand consecutively, both signing their own names — the fourth pair of ladder men to cross together, and a crossing this project had never looked at. Nothing attaches to anybody. The reason it was never looked at is the part worth recording: the arrival inventory stopped at 1754 out of habit, not evidence.

One more thing opened while the volume was in hand. Strassburger’s third volume is not only an index — it prints the Philadelphia arrival lists of 1785 to 1808, which are the only arrival class in this corpus that names women. Its own index already shows a ladder woman on one of them. That volume is free, it needs no login, and this project has never opened it.

Six of tonight’s eight finds came out of sources this project already had open — a column that was never indexed, a page box nobody had typed into, a heading nobody had counted, a volume nobody had turned to. That is not a coincidence, and it is not new: it is the shape almost every good result on this site has had.

2 August 2026 — evening: the hand itself, and a church that was never called what we called it
The marriage read in the clerk’s own hand — and a spelling that loses its first letter

This project has worked on the 1784 marriage of Michael Welty and Christina Rothrauff for its entire life without seeing the page — only a printed transcription and a later typescript, both copies. The database carries the York Reformed congregation twice: as that typescript, and, in a browse node nobody had walked into, as the original German manuscript register. The line is there in the pastor’s hand: “Michael Weltÿ & Christina Rothrauff — 18t Mayj.”

There has been a memory in this family all along of an umlaut on this name, unsourced. The memory was right, and it is not on a vowel at all — it is the German scribal trema on the final y, carried identically in two lines of the same hand five weeks apart.

And the database’s own index reads that trema’d surname as “Utly” — the W taken for a U, the dotted y for ly. Four York marriages come back under that spelling, and three match marriages already on this log exactly, on date, place and spouse: Michael’s own, the 1785 double wedding, Philip Jacob’s of 1780. The fourth has no counterpart: Catharina Utley married a David at York on 24 September 1781, invisible to every search ever run here. Every spelling sweep in this log had been anchored on the letter W, which the hand does not always survive; any negative on this site resting on a list of W‑spellings has a hole in it of exactly that shape.

A second Welty bride at the same altar, five weeks earlier

The same page carries a marriage nobody here had seen: “Peter Meßerle & Christina Weltÿ — 13t aprill” 1784, thirty‑five days before Michael’s wedding, confirmed independently in the typescript. Welty women are nearly absent from this corridor, and Messerly is already on this project’s list of militia neighbours who travel with Michael’s name in the York records. A kinship signal, not an identification: she is filed unattached, nothing merged.

The Somerset church is not called what this site has been calling it

The congregation holding Michael and Christina’s family west of the mountains has been cited here as Center Lutheran Church, Stanton Mills. That is the archive’s browse label. The whole book was read this evening, twelve images end to end, and it names itself on its own title page: “Baptismal, and other, Records of the Samuel’s Church, Lutheran and Reformed, 1784–1793 … about three miles west of Somerset,” in Somerset Township. The film segment carrying that label holds three unrelated volumes, one of them from Tioga County at the far end of the state. The citation here has been corrected. It was also a union congregation, Lutheran and Reformed together, which weakens an argument this project has leaned on about confession.

Two records came out of it. On 13 June 1786 Michael stands sole sponsor at a neighbour’s son’s baptism — and the child is named Michael, godfather‑naming for the third time. The earliest previous trace of the household beyond the Susquehanna was a son baptized in 1788: Michael is in the Casselman valley nineteen months earlier than this site has been saying. And “Communicants at Easter, 1790” has Michael Wäldy sixth of eleven men — an entry that sat in an index for months labelled a “residence.” Christina is not in the facing column of fourteen women, recorded as an absence and expressly not an argument, because the columns are not paired.

Which sharpens the tension this site has flagged: Michael sponsors in Somerset County in June 1786 while the York assessor names him at Dover in 1788 and 1789. Three years wide, left standing on purpose.

Two negatives. The book contains no marriages and no burials at all, so a marriage or burial for this family in these years was never going to be here — the reopener is another volume: the German original this translation was made from, a later book of the same congregation, or another congregation in that end of the valley. And Philip, born 2 May 1789, is not in it: the baptisms run cleanly April to October with no gap for him. One caution on the whole volume — it is a translation by a private individual, so every umlaut in it is one further remove from the pen.

The bonds were pulled, and the answer is no

The last entry named a cheap next step: an administration bond sits within a few leaves of the account and names its sureties, who in a German settlement are overwhelmingly kin. Both have been read, and Michael Welty is not a surety on either. The 1755 bond for John Welty of Hannover Township, Casper Singer administrator, has Michael Walther and Adam Mayer — a name close enough to raise the pulse and, on checking, the New Hanover congregation’s own schoolmaster. The 1767 bond for the Douglass George was entered by Anna Barbara Wilte, “widow and relict,” signing by her mark.

The files gave up two things anyway. The 1755 one calls its decedent “John Welte, black smith” — a trade this project did not have, and the kind of detail that makes a man findable in records that never mention his family. And across the two files the same clerks spell one surname Welte, Welty, Welly, Wilte and Willy, now attested inside a probate file rather than guessed at.

The godparents, recovered from a table nobody could read

The Falckner Swamp Lutheran register was opened here days ago, but its baptisms stayed dark: a hundred and forty‑six pages of wide table set sideways on the page, whose published machine text is pure noise. Every leaf was pulled at full resolution, rotated, read again by machine, swept against twenty‑five spellings, then every hit read with human eyes. It is the section with the sponsors in it.

Anna Barbara Welte stands godmother in February 1757 — the first woman of that name in this corridor, ten years before the widow of the same name on the 1767 bond — and her surname is invisible to any text search because it wraps onto its own line. George Welte and his wife stand godparents to all three children of the Johann Jacob Welte household, and at the third, in 1766, the printer names her: “and wife Barbara” — the discriminator the Douglass estate has been waiting for, and still not merged, because a sponsor line carries no township and no age. A Jacob Welte stands alone for the son of Thomas and Margareth West in 1762, and this log holds a Margareth Weld who married a Thomas Weston in 1761: on the face of it, a sister.

One entry does something quieter and better. A household baptizes a daughter as Johannes Welde and Christina in May 1753 and another as Welty and Christina in January 1754 — one printer, one book, one household. The equation Welde = Welty is now attested inside a source instead of assumed by us. And the negative: across the whole table, all twenty‑five spellings, no Michael on this ladder anywhere in that register — which names its own reopener on the same page, since all twenty‑five spellings begin with a W.

A negative that is a shape rather than a zero

The other congregation in that township — the Reformed one, same families — was swept whole: a hundred and seventy pages, twenty‑eight spellings, marriages, burials and early baptisms read line by line. Not one name on the ladder. Ordinarily that closes a lead. It does not, because of what the reading measured: the marriage register in this copy is blank from February 1755 to March 1769, and the burial register from September 1755 to February 1772. The marriage this project most wants from that corridor, and the burial that would go with it, fall inside those holes — they were never going to be there, whatever had been searched for, which is a different statement from “they are not there.”

What is clean is the stretch the copy covers: the blacksmith who died by February 1755 is not among the eleven burials recorded in those years. One positive fell out — the administrator of that estate buried his wife in this Reformed congregation in January 1752. And the transcript caught itself being incomplete, in print, on its own contents page: “IV. Confirmations. (Not Copied) 1439 listed.”

The top of the passenger list says where they came from

The 1754 crossing has been read here name by name for weeks. Nobody had read the sentence above the names. On 30 September 1754 the clerk at the Philadelphia court house wrote that the foreigners from the ship Richard & Mary were “Inhabitants of the Dukedom of Wirtemberg” — and the three men on this ladder sign immediately below it. The other 1754 arrivals are described by the same clerk as Lorain, Alsace, Franconia, Swissers, and four mixed descriptions: this is the only arrival of that year described as Württemberg and nothing else. The first corroboration of the Württemberg case from the American end — and it describes a shipload, not a man. Scoped the following night: across all five hundred and twenty‑three headings the clause proves period‑bound, so the comparison holds within 1754 and a silent heading in other years says nothing at all.

2 August 2026 — morning: the searches that were never run, and one man with six spellings
What the old negative actually searched

Yesterday this project admitted that a Pennsylvania negative broke when re‑run across the whole spelling ladder. This morning asked the duller question: not was it wrong but what did it actually type. Of the four men it claimed to cover, it queried three given names — Michael, Michel, Jacob — and exactly one surname unattached to a given name: Welthe. George was never typed at all. And the one spelling that ran unqualified is the one this family stopped using the moment it landed.

It was run again properly — eighteen spellings, none tied to a given name, across thirteen Pennsylvania collections for the whole eighteenth century, with the given name read off each hit afterwards. It produced three records, all below. And a caution worth passing on: in an eighteenth‑century Pennsylvania deed, “Welthe” is almost always the word Commonwealth, and “Wealty” is the same word again. The one rung the old search ran unqualified is a rung whose Pennsylvania hits are legal boilerplate.

Berks County, opened for the first time

The 1754 shipload has been traced before by the rarest surname on its passenger list, and that calibration pointed along the Berks–Montgomery–Philadelphia corridor. Berks itself had never been searched. Its printed tax lists for 1767, 1768, 1779, 1780, 1781, 1784 and 1785 hold one ladder man: George Welde in the Oley return of 1767 and “Welte, Geo., cooper” in Oley in 1768 — no acres, no stock, both years, with the same block of neighbours holding its order across the two lists, which is what makes them one man. A separate Peter Weld appears in Cumru. There is no Michael and no Jacob of any spelling in Berks across all seven years, and the Oley man is gone by 1779.

The Oley taxables of 1734 and 1759 carry nobody on the ladder, so this George arrives between 1759 and 1767 — and the reason he leaves no church paper is structural: Oley had one German congregation and it had no settled pastor until 1771. He is taxed in exactly the pastorless stretch. One trap fell out of the same volume: the scan turns printed ei into el with great consistency, producing four handsome false hits for a family spelled Welda, including a Michael. At the image every one is Weida, a different Berks family.

The donation‑land grant does not reproduce

A compiled reference records a two‑hundred‑acre donation‑land grant to a “Michael Welty” in December 1786, never traced to source. The source has now been read — the state archives printed the entitlement roll itself in 1896 — and there is no Welty on it under any spelling, the whole W run read leaf by leaf at full resolution. What the roll does hold is “Welsh, Michael”, and in the compiled book a Michael Welsh with the same rank and the same two hundred acres stands four lines above the Michael Welty entry. Not stated as proof of a transposition, but precisely the shape one leaves. The limit: the eyes‑on read covers the letter W, and a clerk who wrote this name with an F or a V sits elsewhere in the alphabet.

Getting there required breaking the volume open. That register is set as wide tables turned sideways on the page, so its published machine text is upside‑down gibberish while the horizontal running heads read perfectly — the section looks searchable and is not. Any keyword search of it, anywhere, returns a false negative, and nothing in the result tells you so.

One private, one company, six spellings

The best thing the morning turned up is not a person but a proof about names. In one company of the third battalion of Northampton County militia, under one captain, across the battalion’s own returns of 1780–82, one private is written down under four spellings in five successive returns: Philip Weldy, Weldy, Welday, Wealty, Welty. A church index adds Welti and Waldy for the same household in the same township — six in all. What locks it is not the name but the neighbours: Sheneperger, Walker, Cromly, Diel, Sigll, Rence, travelling with him in every list under the same officers, their own spellings wobbling just as freely.

The spelling “Welday” has been treated for months as the marker of a distinct branch. It carries no such information; any argument leaning on it is void, and the question has to be settled on records, residence and DNA. The same sweep demoted a rung the ladder had carried since a 1938 list: “Felty” in these records is not a surname at all but the Pennsylvania‑German short form of the given name Valentin.

A bounty account, and the test that took the wind out of it

A volume of the printed state archives that a previous sweep listed as unread — then quietly reasoned about as though it had been read — contains “George Welty,” paid a six‑pound enlistment bounty as a soldier of the 2d Pennsylvania Regiment of the Continental Line in 1781, read at the image with the governing heading walked back four pages. That alone undoes an earlier conclusion here that the Continental‑service question was closed.

The regiment’s rolls were then searched for him and he is in none of them. The obvious reading — a bounty taken and a service never rendered — was written into the log this morning and withdrawn again the same morning, because it was tested: all hundred and thirty‑seven men on that account were matched against the regiment’s printed roster, and only about three in five appear on it, roughly a quarter not at all. Absence from that roster is ordinary and cannot carry an inference about anybody. The facts stand as read; the story draped over them does not.

Five corrections at one York register — and a household this project had already read

A church‑records index was swept for the whole eighteenth century, partitioned decade by decade so its broken paging could not hide anything: a hundred and eleven records under one spelling group, fifty‑five under the other, all read. Most of what it produced is correction. The double Welty wedding of 1785 is 1 May, not 10 May — both halves, read at the record pages. (Corrected again the following night at the manuscript: the entries are nine days apart, and the “10 May” ghost belongs to the second.) A marriage of 1780 carried with two conflicting dates resolves to 20 June. A 1775 marriage recorded as Dover belongs to Trinity Reformed at York. A daughter baptized in July 1809 appears who was not on the tree at all, and a Henry baptized the same day confirms his mother’s name from a record rather than a descendant’s tree. Two unread spouse surnames were read; both are Miller, at one congregation inside twenty‑three years — a prompt to check and nothing more.

One find looked briefly like the best thing on the page: a Jacob and Anna Maria with sons Johannes, Johann Peter, Heinrich and Abraham, reading exactly like the head of another Welty family this site tracks. Opened at the record pages, it is a household this project has already read, at York, resurfacing under a second index that spells the family differently. No new person, no merge. All of this is index‑grade — and one defect in the same collection says why that caveat is not a formality: it carries a child with a birth date recorded after his baptism date.

2 August 2026 — the page turns the right way up
The estate of John Welty of Hannover Township

The rebuilt Pennsylvania search flagged one document and could not read it: the leaf is written sideways, the viewer’s rotate control would not move, and the machine text was poor enough that the entry went into the log graded unusable, the decedent’s name read as “John Willy.” The rotation was cleared by hand this morning, and the page turned. The heading reads: “Dr. Casper Singer, Adm’r of the Estate of John Welty of Hannover Townsp: County Phil’a, dec’d.” The decedent is on the ladder himself — this is a Welty estate, not a Welty standing about in somebody else’s. Hannover Township then lay in Philadelphia County; it becomes New Hanover when Montgomery is struck off in 1784 — the German settlement called Falckner Swamp.

He was a tenant: no real estate in the account, and the discharge side pays rent to a John Moyer. It also pays office fees, funeral charges, two constables, the clerk of the vendues, two notes of hand, and Henry Antes Esq’r — the leading household of that settlement and its justice, which places the decedent socially in one line. And on the charge side, among the named debtors, above and separate from the two vendue totals: “To d’o of Michael Welty — 10/6.” The distinction is the whole point of the line. He is not somebody who bought a pot at the estate sale and got lumped into a total. He owed John Welty ten shillings and sixpence in his own name, before the sale, and the administrator went and collected it.

The Richard and Mary docked at Philadelphia on 30 September 1754. Within two years a Michael Welty is present, transacting, and known to a household of his own name in the German settlement the shipload’s own dispersal points at. Yesterday’s Dover leaf had already dented the old claim that the shipmate leaves no trail in Pennsylvania; this removes it, much closer to the landing. It was missed for exactly the reason the morning’s audit identified: the spelling “Welty” was never once searched on its own.

What does not follow is that this is the shipmate, or the Michael this project follows to Dover and then to Ohio. A debt line carries no age, origin, wife, trade or signature, and this log already keeps several Michaels firewalled from one another. Nothing has been merged. An absence that made the immigrant reading look unsupported has been removed; the positive case has not been made.

One clerk, one page, two spellings

The same leaf settles a smaller question. In the heading and the Michael Welty line the clerk writes Welty — a looped l, then a t with a crossbar running out to the right. Four lines down the other column, paying for the burial, the same hand writes “the funeralls of John Welly” — two ascenders of equal height, no crossbar, identical to the double l he has just written in Campbell. One clerk, one page, one man, two spellings. This project has never had that attestation inside a single document before; the “Welly” rung had rested on machine misreadings and one printed entry that turned out at the image to say Kelly. The consequence is practical: a search for Welty finds the heading and misses the funeral line, and a search for Welly finds the reverse.

Two Georges, two probates, and an age that will not fit

George Welty of Douglass Township, Philadelphia County, dead by 21 October 1767, when two neighbours appraised his goods. The inventory is the man: apparel at nine shillings and ninepence, a hammer and anvil, an axe and drawknife, scythes, two casks, a pail, a tub, a reel, a spinning wheel, old books, a bed at fifteen shillings. No land, no stock. A cooper’s and jobbing tradesman’s kit — and the Oley man in the Berks list of the following year is entered as “Welte, Geo., cooper.” One year and one county apart, and therefore two different men, since the Oley one is alive and assessed in 1768.

George Weldy of Berks County, dead intestate by 30 June 1794, administration granted at Reading to his son John, the widow Dorothy having renounced her right, the decedent described as “late a Soldier in the Service of the United States of America.” The first ladder man ever placed in Berks with a family attached — and the widow’s name is a genuine discriminator: a Georg Welde and his wife Maria Dorothea baptize children at Reading in 1771 and Philadelphia in 1772, a couple this project has held for weeks with nothing to test them against. Still not merged, but the question has moved from is there any link to is this the same Dorothy.

Then the arithmetic, which cuts against the tidy version. The Johann Georg this project reads as the third man on the 1754 list was baptized in 1710. A soldier of the United States dying in 1794 was not born in 1710. The 1710 baptism and this administration cannot both describe one person — so either the Berks soldier is not the Oley cooper, or the Oley cooper is not the man off the ship. Saying which is not possible today, and saying that plainly is worth more than an identification would have been.

Six exposures, one settlement, one generation

Set the day’s finds on a map and they stop being a scatter. Inside about ten miles and twelve years, on the Philadelphia–Berks line, this log now holds: John Welty, tenant, of Hannover Township, dead in 1755 or 1756; Michael Welty, his named debtor, in 1756; Johann Jacob Welte and his wife Maria Margareth, baptizing children in the New Hanover congregation to 1763; Johannes Welty and his wife Christina in that same congregation; George Welty of Douglass Township, landless, dead in October 1767; and George Welde, later Welte the cooper, in Oley across the county line in 1767 and 1768.

Six exposures, one settlement, one generation — in the corridor a calibration off the ship’s own passenger list had already pointed at. Not one is attached to a person on this tree, and several are firewalled from each other on purpose. What the pattern changes is the method: this is now one problem, not six, and chasing each George and each Michael separately is the wrong shape of work. It also makes the next step obvious and free — two of these estates were administered through the same Philadelphia office, and an administration bond names its sureties, who in a German settlement are overwhelmingly kin. (Both bonds were read that evening. He is not on either.)

1 August 2026 — late: the week pauses, and the page could use more eyes than one
The last read before the lights go down

One more pass at the Gültstein baptism register, testing the question the evening left open: could the Michael Welthe who married there in 1732 have fathered a son Michael early enough to be the man who later appears in Dover Township? The book was read line by line, 1739 to the middle of 1745, every father’s name down the parentage column of every page — and in those years there is no son Michael to any Welthe couple. Three baptisms this project did not have turned up instead.

Anna Maria, baptized 8 October 1741, daughter of Michel Welthe — citizen, day‑labourer — and his wife Catharina: the first child recorded to that couple since a son Jacob in 1736, so that five‑year gap is now known to be a real silence rather than an unread stretch of pages. The other two belong to Hans Jerg Welthe and Maria Agnes — a son Johann Georg in August 1741, a daughter Anna Maria in February 1744 — with one godparent couple at both fonts, the sort of cross‑lock that keeps two entries from drifting into two different families.

Two things follow, neither a conclusion. The window this test still has to cover has narrowed to mid‑1745 to November 1747, and those pages are not read. And both 1741 infants carry the register’s mark for a child who died, which promotes a nicety into a real next step: the village’s funeral book for the 1730s and 1740s has never been opened, and a series of infant burials would account for this family’s spacing better than any amount of inference. The parish’s family‑register volume was searched the same session and the household’s page was not found. It remains the single most valuable unread thing in that village.

Where the tree stands tonight — and why the open questions are the good part

A week ago the German origin of this line was a family legend with a wrong year stapled to it. Tonight there is a house at Gültstein holding three brothers of exactly the three remembered names, whose record in that village ends the way an emigrating family’s does — graves for everyone who stayed, and none for the three whose names turn up on a 1754 passenger list. That is a hypothesis with its remaining tests written down. On the American side the Dover Michael went from a man known by a marriage and an estate to a man standing on the record year over year, with a column of five Weltes, one under another, on a single 1781 Dover leaf that nobody had read.

And here is the part worth being honest about. The line’s one unproven rung is more open tonight than it was on Monday, not less. Reading the 1800 census at the manuscript removed the birth year that had been quietly doing the work of ruling out the 1754 crossing — it came from a compiled pedigree, not a record. So three readings sit live at once: that this Michael was born in Pennsylvania to the Dover Philip Jacob, which is where the tree still draws him; that he was himself aboard the ship in 1754; or that he was the son of a man who was. Each has a test attached in the log, none is settled, and the tree will keep drawing that link dashed until a record settles it. A genealogy that only ever narrows is a genealogy that has stopped checking itself.

Why it goes quiet for a bit

This has been an unusually heavy stretch — long daily runs, whole films read end to end, registers worked line by line — and that pace has a bill attached. The German church‑register subscription has lapsed; the American ones have roughly a month left. So the heavy research pauses for a while. Not stopping — pausing, with the log, the leads and the unread page numbers written down precisely so the next run starts where this one stopped. Everything already found stays where it is: free, unwalled, public domain.

What is worth looking at in the meantime

Several of the most promising things left are free, online and open to anyone. If you have an evening and a tolerance for eighteenth‑century handwriting: the York County township assessment film (FamilySearch 008350826, 793 images, home‑viewable), the film that produced the 1781 Dover leaf, with many Welte‑bearing leaves located but not read closely and the Manchester ones barely touched. The ship‑list facsimiles — Strassburger’s Pennsylvania German Pioneers volume II, holding the actual signatures, including two same‑day exemplars of the 1754 trio; and volume III, which prints the 1785–1808 arrival lists, the only Philadelphia arrival class that names women, and which this project has still never opened. The Gültstein registers — the baptisms from mid‑1745 to November 1747, the funeral book for roughly 1736 to 1748, and the parish family‑register volume. The Pennsylvania Donation Land patents (state archives, free, no login). The printed Pennsylvania Archives depreciation lists on archive.org, where mapping the block headings would turn every bare name into a name with a county attached.

And newspapers, Bibles and drawers. The York papers of 1789–1798 have never been browsed for the estate notice that would be the first direct record of Philip Jacob’s death. And the thing no archive holds: a family Bible, a German Familienregister, a fraktur baptismal certificate, a hand‑copied sheet of names in somebody’s attic. Those have decided more questions on this site than any subscription has.

And please check the work — especially the parts that look finished

This is the ask, and it is meant plainly: this project could use other people’s eyes on the actual pages. Much of what is written up here was read fast, and some of it was read by a machine that is very good at not getting tired and entirely capable of being confidently wrong about a letter. A second reader who slows down over one leaf catches what a fast reader does not.

No sweep here is finished, no register is exhausted, and no “not there” means more than not found yet, by these eyes, under these spellings. Add a rung to the spelling ladder and you have quietly reopened every search run before you added it — not hypothetical: a negative on this very page, published in good faith on four spellings, broke on re‑running, and what fell out of it was the 1781 Dover leaf. If you read one of these films and find something that was walked past, that correction is worth more than another week of new searching. The address is at the foot of the page.

1 August 2026 — evening: Dover fills in its years, and a village funeral book answers
Dover, year by year

With the township question settled, the film that raised it was worked to its end — every Welte‑candidate leaf on all 793 exposures opened at the image. The Dover Michael now stands on the record year over year: the 1781 class list places Michel Welte in the township’s third class, inside the five‑man Welte block; the same year’s property valuation lists him again among the single men, a Jacob of the name beside him; in 1782 he occupies a forty‑four‑acre farm; in 1784 he is on the township assessment once more — and on 18 May 1784 he marries at York. One township, one name, four documents, straight into the marriage record.

The same read‑out killed two lookalikes at full resolution — a promising 1785 “Welty” trio that resolved into the Holly family, and a “Willy Michael” that resolved into a Worley — and opened a thread deliberately not attached to anyone: a Jacob in Manchester Township, present every year from 1781 to 1785 among Manchester’s own community of names. A Jacob also recurs beside the Dover Michael. Whether either is the same man, a brother, or another family is a question for records, not name‑matching. One limit holds through the whole run: not one of these documents states an age.

A cousin’s challenge, and what it bought

A living cousin, reading along, wrote to say he had found the 1754 trio himself — and to propose that the third signature reads not “Hans Jerg Welte” but a different name altogether. Run down at the facsimile, it paid twice. The check surfaced something never catalogued here: the volume holds a second, same‑day exemplar of all three signatures, so every reading of these hands can now be tested against two independent specimens. And the proposed reading does not survive: at both exemplars the letterforms rule it out, and the ship list’s clerk — a fourth, independent hand — wrote the man down as “Hans Jurg.” Hans Jerg Welte stands. But one instinct in that letter was exactly right in a way its writer could not have known: reading “Hans Jurg” as a Johann Georg is precisely what a German marriage register proved the same afternoon, an ocean away.

The funeral book answers

The morning left the Gültstein family as a hypothesis with three named tests. By evening the decisive one had run. The parish’s death register was read line by line, 1749 to 1790 — and everyone in that family who stayed has a grave. The mother, “Michel Welthin’s wedded wife,” buried February 1752 at seventy‑five. The old father, buried April 1756 at seventy — at home, two years after the crossing. An unmarried daughter, buried 1767 at sixty, the last of the name in the book. And the three men whose names sailed have no graves — not in Gültstein, not in the neighbouring parishes, not anywhere in the fifty‑million‑row index. Absence, with coverage verified, is what emigration looks like.

The same pass repaired the cast. The man first cast as the trio’s “Hans Jerg” — seated a village over, at Mötzingen — was buried in January 1746: he boarded no ship. The marriage‑book index supplies his replacement and more. It records the 1749 remarriage of the Gültstein brother Johann Georg, a fresh widower, under the name “Hans Jerg” — the parish’s own hand equating the two names exactly as the ship list does — and discloses a brother the hypothesis did not have: Jacob, the eldest, married 1726. The reading tightens from a family circle to three full brothers of one Gültstein house — Jacob, Michael, Hans Jerg: the very names the family’s hand‑copied papers have always given the trio. And the register’s years 1749 to 1756 read like a departure: Johann Georg buries a wife, an infant son, then a second small boy; the mother dies; the three names sail; the old father dies with no man of his name left in the book.

The German end now stands on register‑level evidence. That the trio are these three brothers is still a hypothesis, strengthened, with its remaining tests named. And whether the Michel Welte who signed that list is the Michael this project follows through Dover to Ohio is untouched by anything a Württemberg funeral book can say: no record on either side of the ocean yet states that man’s age.

1 August 2026 — the family legend goes on trial, on both sides of the Atlantic
The book with the actual hands in it

Yesterday’s signature — the 1786 assignment a living second cousin’s tip brought to light — demanded a second one to stand next to, and the search for one corrected a long‑standing mistake. The page this project has carried for months as Philip Jacob’s immigration oath is not a signature at all: it is the typeset transcription from volume I of Strassburger’s Pennsylvania German Pioneers. The signatures live in volume II, a facsimile photographed from the original lists. On the leaf reproducing printed p.499, List 149 C of the ship Royal Union, 15 August 1750, second line from the top, is Philip Jacob’s own signature — a small, cramped, confident German school hand, exactly where the printed list places “Phipps Welde.”

And he signed. Strassburger prints a cross or an initial for every man who could only make his mark — five on these same two pages. There is no mark by this name. Writing‑literacy is now documented in this family a generation before the 1786 autograph, which removes the most obvious objection to that find before anyone raises it.

Two more hands, and a rule against reading too much into any of them

The same volume holds List 218 B: the ship Richard and Mary, 30 September 1754. In the left column, one directly above the other, stand Michael Weldta and Jacob Weld[t], each in his own hand, neither a mark. The men behind the legend now have faces. What those faces cannot do is prove kinship — and that was tested rather than assumed. Nine unrelated German immigrants sampled off the same page share the same loops, the same rounded W, the same slant: the letterforms are the curriculum, not the person. Sharper still: the 1754 Michael’s hand resembles the 1750 Philip Jacob’s at least as closely as the 1786 signature does, and nobody claims those two were kin. The ruling is now in the log: letterform resemblance is not evidence of identity or kinship in this corpus, in either direction.

The only age statement on record, read with our own eyes

Michael’s birth year of “about 1757” — the date on every online tree, and until this morning on this page — comes from a compiled pedigree, not a record. There is no baptism for him anywhere. The one primary age statement that exists is the 1800 census, read this morning at the manuscript at high zoom: the head of the household stands in the 45‑and‑over column, and the 26–44 column holds no adult man at all. The only record that states his age says he was born on or before about 1755.

That single tick of an enumerator’s pen — and one tick can err — opens a question this project had treated as closed: the man who signed the 1754 list was assumed ruled out because “Michael was born in 1757.” Dating a man from a pedigree and then using that date to exclude a record candidate is circular, and the log now carries the rule permanently: never use a pedigree‑derived date to exclude a record candidate. The census bracket cannot by itself split an older Pennsylvania‑born Michael from an immigrant one — his single‑freeman years and an evident first marriage in 1784 still lean toward the younger reading — but the question is open, on the record, where it belongs.

Maryland answers, and the answer is nobody

The family papers hand the 1754 trio a tidy dispersal: Jacob to York, Hans Jerg to Hagerstown, Maryland. Maryland had never been swept. It has now been — the 1776 state census and 1778 oath rolls read eyes‑on at their index leaves, the 1783 Washington County tax, the 1790 federal census, the county histories, and the full‑text corpus for the whole eighteenth century, across every spelling on the ladder. There is no Michael of any spelling anywhere in Maryland in the window, and — the sharper result — no Hans Jerg at Hagerstown either: not one George of the name in Washington County until an 1808 bond surety, half a century after the crossing. The trio’s “known” dispersal was never known. Maryland’s real eighteenth‑century Weltys are there — Johns, Jacobs, a Christian, a Frederick, in traceable and separate streams — and none of them is any of these men.

A village raises its hand

Then the trail crossed the ocean. The indexed Württemberg church registers — fifty million rows — turn out to be searchable under the project’s current subscriptions, and the first calibration shot used the rarest surname on the 1754 passenger list to find the shipload’s recruiting ground: the middle Neckar, around Stuttgart and Böblingen. Inside that pocket, at Gültstein near Herrenberg, sits a family: Michael Welthe Sr., citizen and farmer, with sons Hans Jerg (married 1719), Michael Jr. (married 29 July 1732, the register page read at the image) and Johann Georg (married 1739) — and eight kilometres north at Magstadt, a Jacob Welthe, baptized 10 April 1721, who was thirty‑three the September the ship landed. The hand‑copied family papers have always named the trio “Jacob, Michael, Hans Jury.” Here are those three names in one extended family, in one ten‑kilometre circle of parishes.

Two quieter observations lean the same way. The only Welthe burial the index holds in that orbit belongs to a kinsman who demonstrably stayed; the three men whose names sailed have no burials there at all. And the parish’s family‑register index, compiled around 1808, has no Welthe page. Absence of burials is what emigration looks like in an index — and also what incomplete coverage looks like, and the register’s death sections have not yet been read. So this stands as a hypothesis, the best‑shaped one this question has had, with three named tests in the log.

And Pennsylvania declines to make it simple

One more correction, freely admitted: yesterday this project recorded that the 1754 shipmate “leaves no post‑arrival trail under his own spelling in Pennsylvania.” That negative rested on four keyword forms. Re‑run today across the full eighteen‑rung ladder, it broke — and what broke it is the day’s last find. On a militia‑class assessment film this project had never worked stands a 1781 York County leaf carrying, in one column: Philip Welte Jun · Michel Welte · Jacob Welte · George Welte, read at the image at high zoom. Which township it belongs to is now the live question, and it cuts both ways cleanly: the warrant sheets ahead of it name Heidelberg Township, a different Welty family’s country — but the neighbours on the leaf, the Quickels, Ramseys, Reisingers and a Wilt, are the documented Dover and Manchester community. Until the township is established, nothing on that leaf attaches to anyone.

The township has a name, and the name is Dover

By the end of the day that question had an answer, established four separate ways, none of them the warrant that made it ambiguous. The packet’s own docketed cover sheet reads “1781 Township Assessments — Dover, Manchester.” A few leaves on, a recruiting‑class notice names Dover Township outright and numbers its first class down the page. A return further still is headed “Dover … First Class Duplicate.” And the neighbours standing around the Weltes — Quickels, Ramseys, Grosses, a Reisinger, a Rothrauff, a Wilt — are the documented Dover and Manchester households, name for name. The Heidelberg warrant that sheeted ahead of the list is a stray: a summons against a delinquent collector from another township’s paperwork, interfiled in the same run of film.

Read at the image, the Welte column holds five men, one under another: Philip Welte, Philip Welte Jun, Michel Welte, Jacob Welte and George Welte. (The two heavy marks crossing the lower names are not cancellations but ink bleeding through from the back of the sheet — read as strikes on first pass and corrected on the second.) The largest assessment on the block is the first Philip’s; and a second Dover leaf, a property valuation, carries two Philip Weltys holding land, at a hundred and ten acres and a hundred. It is the fullest single‑document view of the Dover Welty group this project has found.

What this is not, yet, is an identification. The one documented Dover Michael of these years was a single freeman in that township in exactly this window — so a Michel Welte standing among a Philip, a Philip Jun, a Jacob and a George in one 1781 Dover column is the first document that so much as shows a Michael inside a Welte family group there. It is not proof that he is the Michael this project follows west to Ohio, and it names no father. What it does close is the older worry, admitted only that morning, that the man had left no trail in Pennsylvania at all. He was in Dover, on a tax roll, spelled plainly, the whole time.

31 July 2026 — a record that was never printed, and a hand
The back of the discharge

A living second cousin, working the same ground from a different direction, sent word of something this project had no reason to expect: the Camp Security Virtual Museum has photographed the original manuscripts behind the Camp Security paperwork — the actual strips of paper, filmed from the Comptroller General’s records at the Pennsylvania State Archives — and put them online. Among them is the discharge of Michael Weltty, dated at the stockade 20 September 1781, the original of a certificate the state printed in 1906. And on the back of that same strip, four and a half years later, something the 1906 editors never printed at all.

It is an assignment: “For a valuable Consideration to me in hand paid by Jacob Sittler I do hereby Assign, transfer and make over unto the said Jacob Sittler … all my Right and Title of in & to the within Discharge and all Monies due & to become due thereon. Witness my Hand & Seal this 22d day of April 1786.” Two men witnessed it — Conrad Laub and a Forsyth — and the archivists’ docket reads No 39. A soldier had turned an unpaid claim on the state into cash, and signed away the paper that proved it.

What it says about the archive matters beyond its contents. The printed Pennsylvania Archives have been the spine of this whole militia investigation, and the volume in question was swept end to end only yesterday. That sweep is not wrong — it is simply not the whole record: the originals in Harrisburg hold documents the editors left out. Every negative this project has drawn from the printed volumes is now a negative about the print.

And the signature is not the scribe’s

The body of the assignment is a clerk’s English round hand — even, thin‑nibbed, practised. The signature at the right is not. It is heavier, differently slanted, built out of the letterforms of a German school hand, and followed by a drawn seal. Crucially there is no “his mark” formula and no cross, which is what a clerk writes when the man in front of him cannot sign. So this is almost certainly the assignor’s own hand: the earliest handwriting this project holds for anyone in the investigation.

What it is not, yet, is a way of telling men apart. This is the hand of the Michael of Camp Security. Whether that man is the Michael of Dover Township who later farmed at Crooked Run is an identification this project supports but has never proved: the strongest thing behind it is that the class‑levy he served in is the same one the only documented Dover men served in, which is an alignment and not an identity. A signature can settle that, but only against another signature, and the Dover‑to‑Ohio man died intestate in 1815 without leaving one on any document yet found. It is filed on the tree exactly that way.

A spelling that turns out to belong to the editor

The 1906 print gives the discharge under “Michael Willty.” The manuscript does not. Read at full resolution the clerk’s surname carries a plain double t — Weltty — and the vowel is the ordinary ambiguity of the hand, not an i. So one rung of this project’s spelling ladder was never a rung at all: it is a nineteenth‑century editor tidying a soldier’s name. The ladder has found real men, but it is worth knowing which of its rungs were cut by clerks and which by typesetters.

Six months before the money

The state settled this man’s militia service with certificate 12,706, issued 26 October 1786 for £10.10.0. The assignment is dated 22 April 1786 — six months earlier. Whoever collected that certificate, it was very probably not the soldier: he had already sold the claim. The interest register in Harrisburg records who was paid on each certificate, and the leaf is known — volume B, folio 95. Whether Jacob Sittler’s name stands there is a specific question with a specific page number attached, which is the most useful shape a research question can take.

Meanwhile, the Palatinate closes

On the other side of the project, a subscription ran out and two loose ends were pulled in before it did. The Edenkoben Reformed baptism register has been searched for the baptism of the Croissant daughter who married into this family in 1738; the last unswept leaves were read this morning, and she is not in that book between 1713 and 1723. Nothing after that can be her either — a baptism in 1724 or later would make her a bride under fifteen. If she was baptized at Edenkoben at all, it happened before 1713. Separately, the Lutheran marriage register at Gommersheim gave up its remaining years — 1736 to 1743, read entry by entry — and returned nothing under any spelling. With that, the ring of parishes around Edenkoben is finished. Two candidate books remain, both long shots.

The week’s two halves pull in opposite directions and both are gains. In Germany a search closed, and closing it means the next person who opens those registers will not spend a month learning what is already known. In Pennsylvania a search opened, on the discovery that the printed archive is an abridgement of a fuller one still sitting on microfilm in Harrisburg. And in between sits a signature — the first handwriting of a man this project has been reading about for months, now finally read from.

30 July 2026 — the class number turns out to be a date
Everyone at the stockade was carrying a date, and nobody had read it

Camp Security was the prisoner‑of‑war stockade outside York, holding men taken at Saratoga and Yorktown, and the York County militia guarded it in shifts from July 1781 to April 1782. The printed archives carry the paperwork in two bodies: the captains’ pay rolls, and then eighty‑eight pages of discharge certificates, powers of attorney and pay assignments filed alphabetically by claimant — no index, no headings, no unit structure, nothing findable by searching for a regiment. Read whole and sorted by the one thing nearly every certificate states — the soldier’s class — the section gives up the rotation itself. The 4th class served under Capt. Peter Ford, with John Mappin as the lieutenant signing releases, 8 July to 8 September 1781. Then the 5th under Capt. Samuel Fullton and Maj. John Edie; the 6th under Maj. William Ashton, with Capts. Edgar, Dodds and Forman; the 7th under Capt. Ephraim Pennington, styled in one document “Capt. Comdt. 7th Class,” through the winter; the 8th under Lieut. Charles Barnitz into April 1782. A class was never a squad. It was the county’s draft cohort, and whose turn it was. Which means any York County man whose Camp Security paper names a class can now be placed to a two‑month window and a named set of officers — and two men in different classes were never there at the same time.

The captain states his own class, and a Dover man answers

Two certificates, both read as page images. On the first, Capt. Peter Ford certifies over his own signature that a soldier “as Sarved is two month in Camp Securtey in my Companey in the Forth class of York County Militia” — and that soldier is himself a private on Ford’s July–September 1781 pay roll, so the class statement and the roll describe one body of men. On the second, a man declares: “I Joseph Bigler of Dover Township York County have in the 4th class Sarved a touer in the Militia in Camp Security … for Casper Spahr of the Same place” — with the clerk’s own footnote underneath putting Spahr in the 4th as well. The class that guarded the stockade that summer is the class in which the only documented Dover Township men appear. That is an alignment and not a proof, and it rests on a single document naming two men — but the objection it answers was the last structural one standing, and what replaced the objection leans the other way.

One tour, documented at both ends

A Michael Wellty appears on Ford’s pay roll. A Michael Willty has his own discharge certificate, dated at Camp Security on 20 September 1781 and signed by Lieut. John Mappin. These have been carried as two records in two spellings. Mappin signs twelve discharges in the alphabetical section and every one falls between the 11th and the 29th of that September — and four of the men he releases (Bowman, Philip Henry, Ramsey, and McWhirter, discharged “as Orderly Serj.”) are privates on Ford’s roll. Mappin was letting Ford’s company go as its two months ran out. So it is one service by one man, enrolled and paid at one end and discharged on his own certificate twelve days after the roll closed. The weight of the evidence does not double. The record beneath it gets a good deal harder.

A soldier standing in the wrong column

A state index card described a John Welty in the 4th class of Capt. Michael Hahn’s company, 1st Battalion, on a “True List” of 12 October 1780 — and cited the printed page where that list appears. The machine‑readable text of that page puts him in the first class. One of them was wrong, and it was not the card. The page is set in two columns with the class headings centred between them, and reading it column‑by‑column — which is how the scanned text is assembled, and how every sweep of this volume has read it — throws names into the wrong class. On the image he stands plainly under 4th Class. The volume was then rebuilt from scratch out of its own word coordinates, 155,820 words across 896 leaves, reassembled row by row into true reading order, and swept again. A second, independent return of the same company confirms him in the 4th class — and spells him “John Wealty.” That spelling is on no list this project has used. It would have defeated every search ever run here, and it is now on the ladder.

What the correction buys is a whole militia identity for one man: enrolled in the 4th class of a named company under a named captain in October 1780; called up when the 4th class took its turn at the stockade the following July; a private on Ford’s roll; and paid, at the end, on a numbered certificate. Four records, no gaps. The rebuilt volume also settles a smaller thing by the same means. On Ford’s roll read properly by rows, the two Weltys are not side by side at all — one sits in the left column about ten rows above the other in the right. A shared surname and a shared roll were never evidence of kinship; now they are not even evidence of proximity.

Three numbered certificates, and a ledger that still exists

Pennsylvania settled its militia debts through a loan raised in 1784 and 1785, and the Comptroller General issued each veteran a numbered certificate. Three York County Weltys hold one. John, certificate 12,665, issued 26 October 1786. Michael, certificate 12,706, the same day, for £10.10.0. Philip, certificate 13,453, issued 30 December 1787, for the same sum. That figure is not arbitrary: a receipt in the same archive itemises it as “Five Pounds for Two Months Melitia Duty and Five Pounds Ten Shillings state Bounty” — the settlement for exactly one two‑month tour. Each card names the leaf of the interest register the entry was copied from: folios 94, 95 and 117 of volume B. That ledger is still in Harrisburg. This trail had been written off a week ago as unrunnable, on the correct finding that a particular printed volume could not carry it — which was true, and beside the point. It runs through the comptroller’s own records, and it now has three numbers and three folios attached to it.

And a Philip who is not the Philip

Two cards away in the same drawer, under an identical name, sits a Philip Welty of Northampton County — John Ritter’s company, under Lieut. Col. Christian Shouse, 1st class, entered 16 September, duty recorded as “actual service on the frontiers.” Wrong county, wrong war — frontier defence, not the prisoner guard — wrong officers, wrong class. He is filed with the decoys, where he will be waiting for the next researcher who searches Philip Welty, Pennsylvania militia, 1781 and takes the first hit.

The advertisement that was never an advertisement

A lead had been open for weeks on a reasonable premise: York County had no newspaper of its own until 1789, so the county Lieutenant would have published his militia delinquents — the men who failed to turn out for their class — in the Philadelphia press, where a name might sit that no muster roll preserved. The Philadelphia papers of those years are digitised and searchable, and the phrase to look for is a fixed one. Across every location, 1779 to 1788, “list of delinquents” returns nothing at all. “Delinquents in the militia” returns two, and both are the same thing: the text of the 1787 militia act, which directs that delinquent returns go from the commanding officer to the county Lieutenant. It was a filing, not a notice. Nobody printed it because printing it was never what the law asked for. The premise was wrong, and the search that proved it wrong is worth more than a hundred empty queries against a surname — a negative that rests on what a document type was survives bad transcription, and a negative that rests on not finding a name does not. Three archive volumes long carried as “missing” closed out the same afternoon: two of them turn out to be forfeited‑estate books that never held a muster roll, and the third covers 1783 to 1790, which is after the war it was wanted for.

A schoolmaster stands as godfather, and gives the child his name

Back in the Palatinate, in the Edenkoben Reformed baptism register, folio 346: “d. 26 [Sept] nat. / 29 bapt. Hanss Jacob Waeldy, & ux. Anna Cath. filius. Testes: H. Georg Wolfg[ang] Georg Schaefer, Kirchenschuldiener, & ux. Anna Elis. m. inf. Georg Wolfgang.” Born 26 September, baptized 29 September 1716. The surname carries both its marks plainly — the umlaut over the a, the diaeresis over the y — so there is no reading to argue about. The man standing as godfather is the village Kirchenschuldiener, the church schoolmaster, and the child is given his name. This family named its children after their sponsors and not after their fathers, which is now documented at every baptism in the sibship, and it is the reason a namesake hunt in these registers has to chase godparents. The entry had been inferred before, from a bracketed note in a sibling list. It is now an entry: a date of birth, a date of baptism, a leaf, and two witnesses.

The last line of the page

Three weeks ago a reading of this register reported a faded August 1714 baptism for a daughter, Anna Barbara, with a godmother from Neustadt. A later sweep went back through 1714 entry by entry, found nothing, and concluded that no such baptism existed and that the girl’s year had only ever been back‑calculated from her age at confirmation. Both accounts could not stand. The page was opened again and read at full magnification, and the entry is there: “Aug. d. 4 nat. / d. 6 bapt. Hanss Jacob Waeldj, & ux. Anna Cath. filia” — born 4 August, baptized 6 August 1714, the godmother an Anna Barbara of Neustadt, the child named for her. It is the final line of the leaf, squeezed in under the last full entry in a smaller and lighter hand, on a lower edge that has browned with age. At the magnification a page‑by‑page sweep normally runs at, it reads as staining rather than as writing. The first reading was right and the second was wrong, and the correction has been written into the record beside it rather than quietly replacing it. The rule it leaves behind is a cheap one and will be followed from here: read the last line of every page deliberately and separately — a scribe filling the foot of a leaf writes small, and that is exactly where an eye running down a column has already stopped paying attention. With the entry recovered, the Edenkoben children of this household are complete and every one of them is dated: 1710, 1713, 1714, 1716, 1719. The intervals are ordinary and there is no gap left for a sixth to be hiding in.

28 July 2026 — the War of 1812 runs out of platform
Two Marys, and only one of them is the wife

The pension file predicted a week ago turned out to exist, and it was read: thirty‑one pages for Jacob Welday Jr of Cross Creek Township, Jefferson County, Ohio. His own 1850 bounty‑land declaration gives the man in the first person — “Jacob Wilday aged Sixty two years”, sworn November 1850, which puts his birth at about November 1787–88, a year or two earlier than every estimate the tree was carrying. He was a private in the company commanded first by Capt. John Alexander and lastly by Capt. Thomas Latta, Col. John Andrews’ First Regiment Ohio Militia; he went in as a substitute for one John Long at the end of September 1812, served five and a half months, and was discharged at Fort Stephenson in mid‑March 1813, his discharge papers long since lost. He signed his own name. He died 9 December 1866.

And then the file settles a question two records had been quietly disagreeing about. The widow who drew the pension is Mary Price, married 20 December 1842 under a certified public record — which means the Mary “Polly” Lockart of the 1816 marriage is a different woman, dead before that second wedding. Two wives, both named Mary, one of them married twenty‑six years before the other: exactly the shape that collapses into a single person in an online tree and stays wrong for a generation. They are now firewalled apart. One further page‑jacket detail is worth its own note: the 1930s correspondence bound into this file traces to the same researcher whose 1938 letter is the only source anywhere for the competing claim that Jacob’s father was a “John Jacob” — a claim that now has a name, a decade, and no evidence behind it.

A private Philip Welty, in the regiment the newspapers named

Last week a Pennsylvania newspaper trail argued that the Stark County Philip Welty (b. 1789) served in Col. Rees Hill’s regiment — the thousand‑man Erie detachment raised in April 1813. This week the federal index answered: a card exists, and it reads “Welty, Philip / Hill’s Regiment Pennsylvania Militia / Private.” A Philip Welty demonstrably served in that regiment. What the card does not carry is an age or a residence, so the identity is not closed — the muster rolls behind it, held at the National Archives and orderable for a fee, would give an enrollment date and place, and that is the test. Two New York decoys were disarmed in the same sweep: a Philip Welty of Hopkins’ New York militia appears in both the service and the pension indexes as one man, and he drew a survivor’s pension — something a man buried in Stark County in 1848 could not have done.

Lot 21, Grave 3

The state grave‑registration card for the same Philip, paywalled and unread for weeks, opened: died 11 September 1848, born 1789, Welty Cemetery, Sugar Creek Township, Stark County — Lot 21, Grave 3, upright marker. It carries the War of 1812 service as a bare claim, Private, and then the line that says as much as any of it: “Regt. unknown.” The 1930s compiler who filled the card in could not name the unit either.

The grandsons stayed home

The last leg was a clean sweep of both federal 1812 indexes — service and pension applications, every spelling on the ladder. The result is a negative worth as much as a find: apart from Philip and the Welday cousins, no federal service or pension record exists for any of Michael’s other sons — not Jacob, George, Michael Jr, Samuel, Daniel, nor Henry — under Welty, Weldy or Welday. One card stays open on the table: a John Welty of Bay’s 3rd Ohio Militia, no rank, no residence, unidentified.

That empties the War of 1812 of everything the subscriptions can reach. What is left of it is offline and costs money: two bounty‑land files in Washington, and the muster rolls that would put a first name to Hill’s private. Next, and quickly — the German pass lapses on the 31st.

27 July 2026 — a baptism dated, a partition read word for word
Johannes Wäldi, 2 February 1713

Eight lines of Kurrent on a page of the Edenkoben baptismal book, and a name on the tree that had carried only a year now carries a date: “Hanss Jacob Wäldj … et ux. Ann Cath. filius. Testes: Johannes Neu et ux. Maria Cath. — inf. Johannes.” Three things come out of one entry. The child is named for his godfather, not his father — the third documented instance of the naming custom in this family, and the reason namesake hunts here chase sponsors rather than fathers. The godfather is a Neu, which puts the two families in god‑kinship as early as 1713 and bears directly on a Doll × Neu wedding at Edenkoben in 1754 that has been the soft origin of an unsourced “married 1754” floating around the trees. And the register gives the first occupation ever recorded for the man at the trunk of this line: the word reads Küfer — cooper — and not Müller, miller, which is what the family’s mill‑byname tradition would have preferred. That one is flagged for a second pair of eyes before it hardens.

Anna Barbara is not where the tree has her

Her sister’s turn went the other way.
